    In order to see the two definitions given above note that the latter is a sieve and contains all $U_i$ and indeed a sieve on $X$ which is minimal among ones contains all $\{U_i\}_{i\in I}$. The case $I$ is finite follows from Proposition \ref{fact_8_1}.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
    A $W$-sieve is finitely generated if it has the form $[\Omega_1,\cdots,\Omega_n]$ where all $\Omega\in\mathcal{B}_W$. If several topology bases are under consideration, we will use $[\Omega_1,\cdots,\Omega_n]_\mathcal{B}$ for disambiguation.
    \par Let $U\in\Ouv_X$. $U$-sieve $\sS$ is admissible (denoted by $\sS\vDash U$) by the Grothendieck topology $(\mathcal{T}_U)_{U\in\Ouv_X}$ if $\sS\in\mathcal{T}_U$. Also for an arbitrary sieve $\sS$, we write $\sS//U$ if $\sS|_U\vDash U$.
\end{definition}

% REVIEW [major]: Proof of the bijection between Grothendieck topologies stops in the middle of GT-Trans. This proposition is not proved as written. [original line 1475]
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_9_1}
    Let $X$ be a topological space and $\cB$ be a topology base for it. Then we have a bijection between the following two sets.
    \begin{enumerate}[1.]
        \item Grothendieck topologies $\mathcal{T}$ on $\cB$.
        \item Grothendieck topologies $\mathcal{T}$ on $\Ouv_X$ with the additional property that $[\cB_U]\in\mathcal{T}_U$ holds for all $U\in\Ouv_X$.
    \end{enumerate}
    The bijection is explicitly given by the following way.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item If a Grothendieck topology $\mathcal{T}^{(\cB)}$ on $\cB$ is given, $\sS\in\mathcal{T}_U$ if and only if $\sS\cap\cB_\Omega\in\mathcal{T}_\Omega^{(\cB)}$ holds for all $\Omega\in\cB_U$.
        \item If a Grothendieck topology $\mathcal{T}$ on $\Ouv_X$ is given, $\sS\in\mathcal{T}_\Omega^{(\cB)}$ if and only if $[\sS]\in\mathcal{T}_\Omega$, (where $[\sS]=[\Theta\sep \Theta\in\sS]$ is the sieve on $\Ouv_\Omega$ generated by $\sS$).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}$\:$
    \par We first show that the map in a is well-defined. GT-Triv is obvious as $\Ouv_X\cap\mathcal{B} = \mathcal{B}\vdash\mathcal{T}^{(\mathcal{B})}$. For GT-Trans, let $\xi:V\hookrightarrow U$ and $\sS\in\cT$, then
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_4}
    A $G_+$-topological space is a $T_0$-space $X$ with a Grothendieck topology on $\Ouv_X$ such that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\emptyset\vDash \emptyset$ (implies $\mathcal{G}(\emptyset)=\{0\}$ for sheaves $\mathcal{G}$ of abelian groups).
        \item If $\sS\vDash U$ then $U = \bigcup_{V\in\sS}V$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_5}
    If $X$ is a $G_+$-space then a $G_+$-base for $X$ is a subset $\cB\subseteq\Ouv_X$ such that $\cB$ is a topology base in the ordinary sense and $[\cB_U]\vDash U$ for all $U\in\Ouv_U$. We say that $\cB$ is $G_{++}$ if in addition, we have,
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $\Omega,\Theta\in\cB \Rightarrow \Omega\cap \Theta \in\cB$.
        \item Membership in $\cB$ is local in the sense that $\Omega\in\cB,U\in\Ouv_\Omega$ such that $U\subseteq \Omega$ and $[\Theta\in\cB_\Omega\sep U\cap \Theta\in\cB]\vDash \Omega$ then $U\in\cB$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_6}
% REVIEW [variables]: G_+-quasi-compact is defined for V but the quantifiers/use switch to U. Use one variable consistently. [original line 1509]
    If $X$ is a $G_+$-space then $V\in\Ouv_X$ is called $G_+$-quasi-compact (abbreviated as $(G_{+qc})$) if for all $\sS\in\mathcal{T}_U$, there are finitely many $(U_i)_{i=1,\cdots,n}\in\sS^n$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        [U_1,\cdots,U_n]\vDash U.
    \end{equation*}
    (i.e. every covering sieve of $U$ has a finitely generated covering subsieve).
\end{definition}
\begin{example}
    If $X$ is a $T_0$-space it gets the structure of a $G_+$-space by
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{T}_U=\left\{\text{sieves $\sS$ in $U$ such that $U = \bigcup_{V\in \sS}V$}\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{example}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_9_2}
    Let $X$ be a $T_0$-space, $\cB$ a topology base for it such that $\Omega,\Theta\in\cB$ implies $\Omega\cap\Theta\in\cB$. For each $U\in\Ouv_X$, put
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{T}_U = \left\{\text{sieves $\sS$ on $U$}\:\bigg{|}\:\forall \Omega\in\cB_U,\exists (\Theta_i)_{i=1}^n\in(\sS\cap\cB_\Omega)^n\text{ such that $\Omega=\bigcup_{i=1}^n\Theta_i$ }\right\}
    \end{equation*}
    Then $X$ becomes a $G_+$-topological space and $\cB$ is a $G_+$-base and the elements of $\cB$ are $G_{+qc}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    GT-Triv follows since for any open set $U$, $U$ itself is a subset of $U$. Let $\sS$ be an admissible $U$-sieve and $\nu:V\hookrightarrow U$. Then as $\mathcal{B}_V\subseteq\mathcal{B}_U$, we have GT-Trans. Let $\mathscr{S},\mathscr{T}$ be $U$-sieves such that $\sS$ is admissible. Suppose for any $\nu:V\hookrightarrow U\in\mathscr{S}$, $\nu^*\mathscr{T}\in\mathcal{T}_V$ holds.
    For $\Omega\in\cB_U$, take $\{\Theta_1,\cdots,\Theta_n\}$ as in the construction. Set $\nu_i:\Theta_i\to U\in\sS$ for each $i=1,\cdots,n$. Then for each $i=1,\cdots,n$,
    \begin{equation*}
    (\nu_i)_*\sT\vDash \Theta_i\Rightarrow \exists (\Xi^i_j)_{j=1,\cdots,r_i}\in(\sT\cap\cB_{\Theta_i})^{r_i}\:\st\: \Theta_i = \bigcup_{j=1}^{r_i}\Xi_j^i.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, $\Omega$ is covered by finitely many elements of $\sT\cap\cB_\Omega$. Thus $\mathscr{T}\in\mathcal{T}_U$.
    \par To show that it is a $G_+$-topological space. First, $\emptyset\vDash \emptyset$ is clear from the definition. The other parts of $G_+$ for $X$ is also clear. Also for $U\in\Ouv_X$, $[\cB_U]\vDash U$ as for any $\Omega\in\cB_U$, $\Omega\in[\cB_\U]$ hence $n=1,\Theta_1=\Omega$ can be taken.
    \par Finally let $\sS\vDash \Omega$ for $\Omega\in\mathcal{B}$. If $\Theta_1,\cdots,\Theta_n$ are as in the formulation of the Proposition. Let $\mathscr{T} = [\Theta_1,\cdots,\Theta_n]$ then $\mathscr{T}\vDash \Omega$ as when $\Xi\in\cB_\Omega$ and $\Xi_i = \Xi\cap \Theta_i\in\cB\cap \mathscr{T}$ by the definition of topology bases and $\Xi=\bigcup_{i=1}^n\Xi_i$.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
    The assertion of the Proposition \ref{proposition_9_2} will still hold when we relax the condition as follows.
    \begin{equation*}
        \Omega,\Theta\in\cB\Rightarrow \exists n\in\N, \exists (\Xi_i)_{i=1,\cdots,n}\in\cB^n\:\st \: \Omega\cap\Theta = \bigcup_{i=1}^n \Xi_i.
    \end{equation*}
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_7}
    The above Grothendieck topology on $X$ is called the $G_+$-topology on $X$ enforcing the quasi-compactness of the elements of $\cB$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_8}
% REVIEW [definition]: In a prime X-sieve the U_i are open subsets of X, not elements of O_U. [original line 1551]
    A sieve in $\Ouv_X$ is called a prime sieve if $n\in\N$ and $\bigcap_{i=1}^nU_i\in\sS$, (intersection in $X$ and all $U_i\in\Ouv_U$) implies that there is $1\leq i\leq n$ such that $U_i\in\sS$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
    It is sufficient to require this when $n=0,(X\not\in\sS)$ and $n=2,(U\cap V\in\sS\Rightarrow U\in\sS$ or $V\in\sS$).
\end{remark}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_9_3}
    Let $X$ be a $G_+$-space. For a subset $\xi\subseteq\Ouv_X$ the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $\xi$ satisfies
            \begin{enumerate}[i.]
                \item $U\in\xi,V\in\Ouv_X,U\subseteq V\Rightarrow V\in\xi$.
                \item $U,V\in\xi\Rightarrow U\cap V\in\xi$ and moreover $X\in\xi$.
                \item If $U\in\xi$ and $\sS\in\mathcal{T}_U$ then $\sS\cap\xi\not=\emptyset$.
            \end{enumerate}
        \item $\mathscr{T}=\Ouv_X\backslash\xi$ is a prime sieve containing every open subset covered by it ($U\in\Ouv_X$ and $\mathscr{T}//U$ implies $U\in\mathscr{T}$).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}$\:$
    \par a$\Rightarrow$b). $\sT$ is indeed a sieve on $X$. Let $U\hookrightarrow X\in \sT$. Then for any $V\hookrightarrow U$, if $V\not\in \sT$ then $U\not\in\sT$ by i). Thus $V\in\sT$.
    By ii), $\sT$ is prime. Finally, if $\sT// U$ and $U\in\xi$, then $\sT\cap\xi\not=\emptyset$ which is impossible.
    \par Taking contrapositives of these we see b)$\Rightarrow$a.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_9}
    A collection $\xi$ of open subsets of a $G_+$-space $X$ satisfying above equivalent conditions is called a van der Put point.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_10}
    The set of vander Put points of $X$ is denoted by $X^*$.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_9_2}
    Let $X$ be a $G_+$-space and $U\in\Ouv_U$ be an open subset. Equip $U$ with the $G_+$-structure $\mathcal{T}^{(U)}=\mathcal{T}|_{\Ouv_U}$, we then have a bijection
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
\{\text{van der Put points $\xi_U$ of $U$}\} \arrow[r, "\xi_U\mapsto \xi=\{V\in\Ouv_X\sep V\cap U\in\xi_U\}", shift left=2] & U^*=\{\xi\in X^*\sep U\in\xi\} \arrow[l, "\xi_U=\xi\cap\Ouv_U\mapsfrom \xi", shift left=2]
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    We have $(U\cap V)^*=U^*\cap V^*$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    $\xi_U\mapsto\xi$ is justified by the counterparts for $\xi_U$ by using that $\cdot\cap U$ preserves inclusion and $\cap$ is associative.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_9_11}
    We equip $X^*$ with the topology for which $\{U^*\sep U\in\Ouv_X\}$ is a topology base.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}$\:$
    \label{fact_9_3}
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item If $\sS\vDash U$ then $U^*=\bigcup_{V\in\sS}V^*$.
        \item If $\cB$ is a $G_+$-base then $\cB^*=\{\Omega^*\sep \Omega\in\cB\}$ is a topology base for $X^*$.
        \item $\xi\in X^*$ is a specialization (i.e. $\xi\in\overline{\{u\}}$ of $u\in X^*$ ) if and only if $\xi\subseteq u$.
        \item If $\sS$ is a $X$-sieve which is maximal with respect to inclusion among all non-admissible $X$-sieves. Then $\xi=\Ouv_U\backslash\sS$ is a closed point of $X^*$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}$\:$
    \par For a, $\xi\in U^*$ then $U\in \xi$ by the assumption. BY Proposition \ref{proposition_9_3}, for an admissible $U$-sieve $\sS$, we have $\sS\cap \xi\not=\emptyset$. Therefore $\xi\in V^*$ for some $V\in\sS$.
    \par By a and $[\cB_U]\vDash U$, $\cB^*$ covers the space. By Proposition \ref{fact_9_2},
    \begin{equation*}
        \Xi\subseteq\Omega\cap\Theta\stackrel{\text{Prop. \ref{proposition_9_3} a.i)}}{\Rightarrow }\Xi^*\subseteq \Omega^*\cap\Theta^*.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore it is indeed a topology basis.
    \par $\xi$ is a specialization of $u$ if and only if for any $\Omega^*\in\cB^*$, $\xi\in\Omega^*\Rightarrow u\in\Omega^*$ if and only if $\Omega\in\xi\Rightarrow \Omega\in u$. This extends to the set of all open subsets by Proposition \ref{proposition_9_3} a.i).
% REVIEW [major]: The proof of primeness/maximal noncovering sieve is garbled; explicitly assume U1,U2 notin S and U1 cap U2 in S for contradiction, then run GT-Loc. [original line 1617]
    \par Finally for d), if $X\in\sS$ then $\sS$ is the all-sieve on $X$, therefore $X\not\in\sS$ by GT-Triv. Let $U_1,U_2\not\in\sS$ and set $U=U_1\cap U_2$. We now show that $\sS$ is prime. By maximality of $\sS$, $\sS_i=\sS\cup\Ouv_{U_i}\in\mathcal{T}_X$. If $V\in\sS$ then But $\sS|_V=\sS_2|_V$ is immediate as these are all sieves on $V$. Suppose $V\in\Ouv_{U_1}\backslash \sS$, we have $\sS|_V\subseteq\sS_2|_V$. Let $W\in\Ouv_{U_2}|_V$. Then $W\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow U$. Since $U\in\sS$ and by the definition of sieves, we see $W\in\sS|_V$.
    But then $\sS\in\mathcal{T}_X$ by GT-Loc, a contradiction. Thus $\sS$ is a prime sieve. If $\sS//V$ and $\sS\cup\Ouv_V\in\mathcal{T}_X$ then
    \begin{align*}
    W\in\sS&\Rightarrow \sS|_W = \Ouv_W\vDash W,\\
    W\subseteq V&\Rightarrow \sS|_W = (\sS|_V)_W \stackrel{\sS|_V\vDash V, \text{GT-Trans}}{\vDash} W.
    \end{align*}
    Thus by GT-Loc, $\sS\vDash X$ which is a contradiction. Thus $\sS\cup\Ouv_V\not\vDash X$. By the maximality of $\sS$, $V\in\sS$. By Proposition \ref{proposition_9_3}, $\xi=\Ouv_X\backslash\sS$ is a van der Put point. If $\xi$ has a proper specialization $s$ then by c), $s\subsetneq\xi$ thus, $\sS\subsetneq\mathscr{T}=\Ouv_X\backslash s$. Again by the maximality $\sT\vDash X$ but $X\not\in\sT$ a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_10_10}
    Let $X$ be a $G_+$-space. We say $X$ has sufficiently many van der Put points if for all $U\in\Ouv_X$, and every $U$-sieve $\sS$, $U^*=\bigcup_{V\in\sS}V^*$ implies $\sS\vDash U$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_10_1}
    If $X$ is $G_+$-quasi-compact and $\sS$ is an $\Ouv_X$-sieve such that $X^*=\bigcup_{U\in\sS}U^*$ then $\sS \vDash X$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Assume that $\sS$ is not covering $X$. Set,
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{M} \defeq\{\mathscr{T}\sep \sS\subseteq\mathscr{T},\mathscr{T}\not\in\mathcal{T}_X\}.
    \end{equation*}
    We apply Zorn's lemma to $(\mathcal{M},\subseteq)$. This is possible as $\mathcal{M}\not=\emptyset$ because $\sS\in\mathcal{M}$ and if $\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{M}$ is non-empty subset linearly ordered by $\subseteq$, then
    \begin{equation*}
    \widetilde{\sS}=\bigcup_{\mathscr{T}\in\mathcal{L}}\mathscr{T}
    \end{equation*}
    is a sieve containing $\sS$. If $\widetilde{\sS}$ $\in\mathcal{T}_X$, then by the $G_+$-quasi-compactness of $X$, there are $U_1,\cdots,U_n\in\widetilde{\sS}$ such that $[U_1,\cdots,U_n]\vDash X$. Thus there is $\mathscr{T}\in\mathcal{L}$ such that all $U_1,\cdots, U_n\in\mathscr{T}$ therefore
    \begin{equation*}
    [U_1,\cdots,U_n]\vDash X,[U_1,\cdots,U_n]\subseteq\mathscr{T}\stackrel{\text{Proposition \ref{fact_8_1}}}{\Rightarrow}\mathscr{T}\vDash X.
    \end{equation*}
    This is a contradiction to $\mathscr{T}\in\mathcal{M}$.
    \par It follows that $\mathcal{M}$ has a $\subseteq$-maximal element. By Proposition \ref{fact_9_3}, $\xi=(\Ouv_X\backslash\sS_{\max})\in X^*$. If $U\in\sS\subseteq\sS_{\max}$, then $\xi\not\in U^*$ (otherwise $U\in\xi$, but by Proposition \ref{fact_9_2}, $\xi\cap\sS_{\max}\not=\emptyset$). A contradiction to our assumption that $X^*=\bigcup_{U\in\sS}U^*$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    If $X$ is a $G_+$-space which has a $G_+$-base $\cB$ where elements are $G_+$-quasi-compact. Then $X$ has sufficiently many van der Put points.
    If $\cB$ is closed under finite intersection in $X$ (including intersection over an empty set thus $X\in\cB$), then $X^*$ is a spectral space.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let $U\in\Ouv_X$ and $\sS$ any $U$-sieve such that $U^* = \bigcup_{V\in\sS}V^*$. Let $\Omega\in\cB_U$ and $\sS_\Omega = \sS|_\Omega$. Then $\Omega^*\subseteq U^*$ hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \Omega^*=\bigcup_{V\in\sS}\Omega^*\cap V^*=\bigcup_{V\in\sS}(\Omega\cap V)^*.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $\Omega\cap V\in\sS_\Omega$, it follows that $\Omega^* = \bigcup_{V\in\sS_\Omega}V^*$. By Lemma \ref{lemma_10_1}, $\sS// \Omega$.
    \par Set $\sT = [\cB_U]$ which is admissible as $\cB$ is a $G_+$-base. For any $V\to U\in\sT$, there is $\Omega\in\cB_U$ such that $V\to \Omega\to U$ by definition. Therefore, from $\sS//\Omega$ for any $\Omega\in\cB_U$ and GT-Trans, we get for any $\nu:V\to U$, $\nu_*\sS \vDash V$. By GT-Loc, $\sS\vDash U$ hence $X$ has sufficiently many van der Put points.
    \par For the spectrality of $X^*$, it is easy to see that $\Omega^*$ is quasi-compact if $\Omega$ is $G_+$-quasi-compact. Then $\{\Omega^*\sep \Omega\in\cB\}$ satisfy the requirement for the quasi-compactness part of the definition of spectrality. If $Z\subseteq X^*$ is an irreducible subset, set,
    \begin{equation*}
        \xi=\{U\in\Ouv_X\sep U^*\cap Z\not=\emptyset\}.
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [sobriety]: To prove sobriety start with an irreducible CLOSED subset Z (or explicitly pass to its closure). [original line 1669]
    Then by definition conditions i), iii) of Proposition \ref{proposition_9_3} are satisfied. Furthermore, using the Irreducibility of $Z$, ii) also holds. In particular, $\xi$ is a van der Put point. Moreover, $\xi$ is a generic point of $Z$ as it is contained in all elements $\cB^*$ intersecting $Z$.
    Finally, if $x,y\in X^*$ are distinct points, then there is $U\in\Ouv_X$ such that $U\in x$ and $U\not\in y$. Thus $x,y$ are topologically distinguishable by $U^*$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    \begin{equation*}
        \glim_{X^*}\U = \{U\in\Ouv_X\sep \text{$U$ contains some $\Omega\in\cB$ such that $\Omega^*\in\mathcal{F}$}\}
    \end{equation*}
    All $\Omega^*$ with $\Omega\in\cB$ are closed under this.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Sheaves}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_10_1}
    A presheaf on $X$ with values in (sets, abelian groups, rings) is a contravariant functor $\mathcal{G}$ from $\Ouv_X$ to one of these categories. Then all $\mathcal{G}(U)$ are objects in these categories and we have
    \begin{equation*}
    V\subseteq U\Rightarrow \mathcal{G}(U)\stackrel{g\mapsto g|_V}{\to}\mathcal{G}(V),
    \end{equation*}
    which is a morphism in the target category such that $g\in\mathcal{G}$ then $g|_U = g,(g|_V)_W=g|_W$ if $W\subseteq V\subseteq U$.
    If this is only given on elements of a certain topology base $\cB$, we speak of a presheaf on $\cB$. In all cases, if a Grothendieck topology in $\cB$ is given, then a sheaf is a presheaf $\mathcal{G}$ satisfying the additional assumption that
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{G}(\Omega)\stackrel{\sim}{\to}\varprojlim_{\Theta\in\sS}\mathcal{G} = \left\{(g_\Theta)_{\Theta\in\sS}\in\prod_{\Theta\in\sS}\mathcal{G}(\Theta)\sep g_\Theta|_{\Xi} = g_\Xi\text{ where $\Theta\in\sS, \Xi\in\cB_\Theta$}\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    If the map is only injective, then $\cG$ is separated.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}$\:$
    \label{definition_10_2}
    Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a sheaf on a $G_+$-space $X$.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item A stalk of $\mathcal{G}$ at $\xi\in X^*$ is such that
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{G}_\xi = \varinjlim_{\Omega\in\xi}\mathcal{G}(\Omega) = \{(\Omega,g)\sep \Omega\in\xi\cap\cB,g\in\mathcal{G}(\Omega)\}/\sim
        \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [typo with content]: Stalk equivalence must read g|_Xi = r|_Xi, not g|_Xi = g|_Xi. [original line 1703]
        where the equivalent relation is given by $(g,\Omega)\sim (r,\Theta)$ if there is $\Xi\subseteq\Omega\cap \Theta$ such that $\Xi\in\cB$ and $g|_{\Xi} = g|_{\Xi}$.
        \item A $G$-skyscraper sheaf of $\xi\in X^*$ is \begin{equation*}
            ([G]_\xi)(U) = \begin{cases}
                G,\quad U\in\xi,\\
                \{0\}
            \end{cases}
        \end{equation*}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    Let $X$ be a $G_+$-space with sufficiently many van der Put points and $\mathcal{G}$ be a sheaf on $X$. For an open subset $U\subseteq X$, pick
    \begin{equation*}
        r\in \prod_{\xi\in U^*}\mathcal{G}_\xi.
    \end{equation*}
    We denote the sieve $\mathscr{S}_r$ on $U$ generated in $\Ouv_X$ to be such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathscr{S}_r \defeq \left[\Omega\in\cB_U\:\bigg{\vert}\:\exists g\in\mathcal{G}(\Omega) \st\forall \xi\in \Omega^*, r_\xi = g_\xi\right].
    \end{equation*}
    Then we define,
    \begin{equation*}
        (\mathrm{Gerb}(\mathcal{G}))(U) = \left\{r\in\prod_{\xi\in U^*}\mathcal{G}_\xi\:\bigg{\vert}\:\sS_r\vDash U\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_10_1}
    Let $X$ be a $G_+$-space with sufficiently many van der Put points.
    \begin{enumerate}[a)]
        \item The stalk is exact on presheaves.
        \item $[G]_\xi$ is a sheaf and $\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathcal{G}_\xi$ is left adjoint to $G\mapsto [G]_\xi$ on (pre)sheaves.
        \item If $\mathcal{G}$ is a presheaf, the morphism $\mathcal{G}\to\Sheaf(\mathcal{G})$ sending $g\in\mathcal{G}(U)$ to $($image of $g$ in $\mathcal{G}_\xi)_{\xi\in U^*}\in\prod_{\xi\in U^*}\mathcal{G}_\xi$ defines an isomorphisms on stalks.
        \item $(\mathcal{G}_\cB)_\xi\stackrel{\simeq}{\to}\mathcal{G}_\xi$ for all $x\in\in X$.
        \item A morphism of presheaves defining isomorphism on stalks defines an isomorphism on sheafification.
        \item If $\mathcal{G}$ is a sheaf, $\mathcal{G}\to\Sheaf(\mathcal{G})$ is an isomorphism. If $\mathcal{G}$ is only defined on $\cB$, $\mathcal{G}\stackrel{\simeq}{\to}\Sheaf(\mathcal{G})|_\cB$.
        \item A morphism of sheaves defining isomorphism on all stalks is an isomorphism.
        \item Sheafification $(\text{Presheaves on $\cB$})\to(\text{sheaves on $\Ouv_X$})$ is left adjoint to the functor of restriction to $\cB$.
        \item One has an equivalence of category between sheaves on $X$ (for the Grothendieck topology on $X$) and (ordinary) sheaves on $X^*$, sending a sheaf $\mathcal{G}$ on $X$ to the sheafification of the (pre)sheaf $U^*\to \mathcal{G}(U)$ on $\{U^*\sep U\in\Ouv_X\}$ ( a topology base for $X^*$) and a sheaf $\mathcal{H}$ on $X^*$ to $\mathcal{G}(U)\to\mathcal{H}(U^*)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [major]: "a-h are shown ..." is not a proof. Either give the arguments or label the proposition as quoted/omitted. [original line 1745]
    a-h) are shown in some ways as in derived sheaf theory. i) follows from these
\end{proof}

\section{General non-Archimedean topological ring}
\subsection{General topological rings and modules}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_1_1}
    A topological group is a group $G$ with a topology on the underlying set such that multiplication and inversion are continuous. That is
    \begin{equation*}
        \mu(g,h)=gh,\iota(g) = g^{-1}
    \end{equation*}
    are continuous.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_1_2}
    A topological ring is a ring $R$ with a topology on the underlying set such that it is a topological group under addition and
    \begin{equation*}
        R\times R\ni(a,b)\mapsto ab\in R
    \end{equation*}
    is continuous. (We do not assume the continuity of $a\mapsto a^{-1}$ for $a\in R^\times$).
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_1_3}
    Let $R$ be a topological ring. A topological $R$-module $M$ is an $R$-module with topology on its underlying set such that $(M,+)$ is a topological group and
    \begin{equation*}
        R\times M\ni (a,m)\mapsto am\in M
    \end{equation*}
    is continuous.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_11_1}
    For a topological group $G$, the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $G$ is $T_0$ (Kolmogorov).
        \item $G$ is $T_1$ (Fréchet).
        \item $G$ is $T_2$ (Hausdorff).
        \item $\{1\}$ is closed in $G$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}$\:$
% REVIEW [proof]: To prove T0/Hausdorff-equivalent group statement, start with x in closure({1}), not x in {1}; inversion sends it to x^{-1} in closure({1}). [original line 1785]
    \par We have $T_2\Rightarrow T_1\Rightarrow T_0$. Therefore we only need to show that a $\Rightarrow$ d) and d) $\Rightarrow$ c). Suppose $G$ is $T_0$. Recall that two elements are topologically indistinguishable if and only if $x\in\overline{\{y\}}$ and $y\in\overline{\{x\}}$. Suppose $x\in\{1\}$. By the continuity of inversion, $x^{-1}\in\overline{\{x\}}$, hence $1\in\overline{\{x\}}$ by continuity of $\mu(x,\cdot)$ hence $x,1$ are topologically indistinguishable thus $x=1$.
    Suppose $\{1\}$ is closed. Note that
    \begin{equation*}
      \delta =  \mu\circ\id\times \iota [G\times G\ni (x,y)\mapsto xy^{-1}\in G].
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore such map is continuous. Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        \Delta_G = \delta^{-1}(\{1\}) = \{(x,x)\sep x\in G\}.
    \end{equation*}
    is closed thus $T_2$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_11_2}
    Every open subgroup $H$ of a topological group $G$ is also closed, and every group $H\subseteq H'\subseteq G$ is also clopen.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We have,
\begin{equation*}
    G\backslash H = \bigcup_{g\in G/H\backslash\{0\}}gH
\end{equation*}
is open therefore $H$ is closed. Every $H'$ subset is a union of right or left $H$-cosets in $G$ which is also open.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_2}
    Let $R$ be a topological ring and $M$ be a topological $R$-module. A subset $X\subseteq M$ is bounded or $R$-bounded if for every neighborhood $U$ of $0$ in $M$, there is a neighborhood $V$ of $0$ in $R$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        V\cdot X = \{vx \sep v\in V,x\in X\}\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
\begin{example}
    When $R$ has the discrete topology, every subset is bounded (take $V=\{0\}$).
\end{example}
\begin{example}
    This defines a boundary on $X$. If $X_1,X_2$ are bounded then so is $X_1\cup X_2$. Every $\{m\}$ is bounded, $M\subseteq N$ and $N$ bounded then $M$ is also bounded.
\end{example}
\begin{example}
    If $R=K$, equipped with an valuation $\ab{\cdot}$ and $M$ is a (valuative) $K$-vector space which is not discrete, then a subset $X$ of $M$ is bounded if and only if there is $B\in[0,\infty)_\R$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall x\in X, \norm{x}\leq B.
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [example]: The normed-vector-space boundedness example uses U as a subset of X instead of the ambient module and has inconsistent variables. Rewrite. [original line 1825]
    For example, take $U = \{x\in X\sep \norm{x}\leq 1\}, B = \ab{x}^{-1}$ where $x\in V\backslash\{0\}$ and $V$ is as in the definition.
\end{example}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_3}
    For subsets $X_1,X_2\subseteq R$, we define,
    \begin{equation*}
        X_1X_2 = \{x_1x_2|x_1\in X_1,x_2\in X_2\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Let $X^0 = \{1\}$. We define recursively $X^n$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        X^{n+1} = X\cdot X^n.
    \end{equation*}
    We say that. a subset $X\subseteq R$ is powerbounded if
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcup_{n\in\N}X^n
    \end{equation*}
    is bounded. It is topologically nilpotent if for every neighborhood $U$ of $0$ in $R$, there is $n\in\N$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \bigcup_{m=n}^\infty X^m \subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    We call an element $r\in R$ topologically nilpotent / powerbounded if $\{r\}$ satisfies these properties.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_11_3}
    Let $R$ be a topological ring and $M$ be a topological $R$-module. We have the following statements.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item A finite union of bounded subsets of $M$ is bounded.
        \item Trivially, every finite subset of $M$ is bounded.
        \item If $X_1,X_2\subseteq M$ are bounded then so is $X_1+X_2 = \{x_1+x_2\sep x_1\in X_1,x_2\in X_2\}$.
        \item If $X\subseteq R$ and $Y\subseteq M$ are bounded then so is $XY\subseteq M$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}$\:$
    \par Suppose $X_1,\cdots,X_n\subseteq M$ are bounded. Then for $U\subseteq M$ an open neighborhood of $0$, there is $V_1,\cdots,V_n$, such that
    \begin{equation*}
    \forall i=1,\cdots,n, V_i X_i \subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore take $V=\bigcap_{i=1}^n V_i$. Then,
    \begin{equation*}
    V\bigcup_{i=1}^n X_i \subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    To show that a singleton $\{x\}\subseteq M$ is bounded, note that for any open neighborhood $U$ of $0$ in $M$, we have, $0\cdot x =0\in U$. Thus $\mu^{-1}(U)$ contains open neighborhoods $V,W$ of $0$ and $x$, respectively such that $VW\subseteq U$. As $x\in W$ is bounded so is $\{x\}$.
    \par For c), let $U$ be a neighborhood of $0$ in $M$, there is a neighborhood $\tilde{U}$ of $0$ in $M$ such that $\tilde{U}+\tilde{U}\subseteq U$ and another neighborhoods $V_1,V_2$ of $0$ in $R$ such that $V_1X_1,V_2X_2\subseteq \tilde{U}$. Take $V = V_1\cap V_2$ in Definition \ref{definition_11_2}.
    \par Lastly, as $Y$ is bounded, we have $VY\subseteq U$ for arbitrary open neighborhood $U$ of $0$. Then we can find an open neighborhood $W$ of $0$ such that $WX\subseteq V$. Therefore $WXY\subseteq U$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_3_another}
    If $X\subseteq M$ is $R$-bounded and $M\stackrel{f}{\to}N$ a continuous morphism of $R$-modules then $f(X)$ is bounded in $N$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    If $U\subseteq N$ is a neighborhood of $0$, let $\tilde{U} = f^{-1}(U)$ and $V$ a neighborhood of $0$ in $R$ such that $VX\subseteq\tilde{U}$. Then $Vf(X)\subseteq U$.
\end{proof}
% REVIEW [example]: Q_p -> Q_q (p!=q) is not a natural continuous unital ring morphism; this is not a valid counterexample as stated. [original line 1877]
It is not always true that the image of a bounded subset of a $R$ under. a morphism $R\to S$ of topological ring is bounded. (The image is $R$-bounded but not necessarily $S$-bounded) for example take $R=\Q_p,S=\Q_q$. However, the following conditions on $R$ asserts that this does not happen.
\begin{align*}
    &\text{$R$ has a topologically nilpotent unit}\tag{T}\label{T}\\
    &\text{$R$ has a sequence of units converging to $0$}\tag{H}\label{H}\\
    &\text{Every neighborhood of $0$ intersects $R^\times$}\tag{E}\label{E}
\end{align*}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_11_4}
    Let $R\stackrel{f}{\to}S$ be a continuous morphism of topological ring. If $R$ has \eqref{T},\eqref{H},\eqref{E}, then so does $S$ and the image $f(X)$ of a bounded subset of $R$ is a bounded subset of $S$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    If $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $S$ and $R$ has \eqref{E}, put $\tilde{U}=f^{-1}(U)$ and $r\in R^\times\cap \tilde{U}$. $f(r)\in S^\times \cap U$. If
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{j\to\infty} r_j = 0
    \end{equation*}
    in $R$ and all $r_j\in R^\times$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{j\to\infty}f(r_j) = 0
    \end{equation*}
    in $S$ and all $f(r_j)\in S^\times$.
    showing that \eqref{H}. For \eqref{T}, take a topologically nilpotent unit $r\in R$, then by setting $r_j = r^j$ in the previous argument, we see \eqref{T} is inherited.
% REVIEW [proof]: rho must be chosen in V_prime AND f^{-1}(U_tilde), otherwise rho X subset U_prime does not follow. The last neighborhood multiplication line also needs rewriting. [original line 1898]
    \par If $R$ has \eqref{E} and $X\subseteq R$ bounded and $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $S$, there is $\tilde{U}$ a neighborhood $0$ in $S$ such that $\tilde{U}\tilde{U}\subseteq U$ by the continuity of $S\ni s\mapsto s^2\in S$. Let $U' = f^{-1}(\tilde{U})\subseteq R$ a neighborhood of $0$. There is a neighborhood $V'$ of $0$ in $R$ such that $V'X\subseteq U'$. As $R$ has \eqref{E}, we can take $\rho\in R^\times\cap f^{-1}(\tilde{U})$. As $f$ is a ring homomorphism, $f(\rho)\in S^\times$.
    Furthermore, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
        f(\rho)f(X) = f(\rho X) \subseteq f(U')\subseteq \tilde{U}.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
        f(X)\subseteq f(\rho)^{-1}\tilde{U}.
    \end{equation*}
    Now we have,
    \begin{equation*}
       U f(\rho)f(X) \subseteq f(\rho)f(\rho)^{-1}\tilde{U}^2\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_11_5}
    Let $R$ be a commutative topological ring.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item Every powerbounded subset is bounded.
        \item A finite union of powerbounded subsets is powerbounded.
        \item A subset $X\subseteq R$ which is both bounded and topologically nilpotent is powerbounded.
        \item Let $(X_i)_{i=1,\cdots,n}$ be such that for each $i$, $X_i$ is bounded and topologically nilpotent. Then $\bigcup_{i=1}^nX_i$ is also topologically nilpotent.
        \item If $X$ is powerbounded or topologically nilpotent and $Y$ is topologically nilpotent, $XY$ is then topologically nilpotent.
        \item The image of a topologically nilpotent $X\subseteq R$ under a morphism $R\to S$ of topological rings is topologically nilpotent.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

        (V\cap \tilde{U})\left(\bigcup_{k\geq0}X^k\right) = (V\cap\tilde{U})\left(\bigcup_{k\geq0}^{m-1}X^k\right)\cup(V\cap \tilde{U})\left(\bigcup_{k\geq m} X^k\right) \subseteq U\cup \tilde{U}^2\subseteq U.

    \begin{equation*}
        V\cup

    \par Let turn our attention to e). If $X,Y$ are both topologically nilpotent, then take $\tilde{U}$ such that $\tilde{U}^2\subseteq U$ and apply the property to it. If $X$ is powerbounded then there is $V$ open neighborhood of $0$ such that

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_4}
    Let $R$ be a topological ring, we define the following subsets.
    \begin{align*}
        R^\circ & = \{r\in R\sep \text{$r$ is power bounded}\},\\
        R^{\circ\circ}&=\{r\in R\sep \text{$r$ is topologically nilpotent}\}.
    \end{align*}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
    Without further conditions, these may fail to be additionally closed. By Proposition \ref{fact_11_3} b), and Proposition \ref{fact_11_5} a, $R^\dcirc\subseteq R^\circ$ always holds.
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_5_1}
    Let $(m_n)_{n\in\N}$ be a sequence in $M$. It is Cauchy if for every neighborhood $U$ of $0$ in $M$, there is $k$ such that $m_i-m_j\in U$ where $\min\{i,j\}\geq k$. $M$ is sequentially complete if every Cauchy sequence has a limit in $M$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_5_2}
    A filter $\mathcal{F}$ of $M$ is Cauchy if every neighborhood $U$ of $0$ in $M$, there is $X\in\mathcal{F}$ such that $x-y\in U$ where $x,y\in X$. $M$ is complete if every Cauchy filter has a limit.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_11_5_3}
    The sequential completion of $M$ is a set of equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences in $M$ where two Cauchy sequences $(m_i),(n_i)$ are equivalent if for every neighborhood $U$ of $M$, there is $k\geq0$ such that $i\geq k$ then $m_i-n_i\in U$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
    If $G$ is a non-commutative topological group, then it typically requires that both $g_ig_j^{-1}\in U$ and $g_i^{-1}g_j\in U$ where $\min\{i,j\}\geq k$. Respectively, $x^{-1}y\in U$ and $xy^{-1}\in U$ for $x,y\in X$ for filters.
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
    One can complete by taking equivalence classes of Cauchy filters. But how to continue multiplication in that completion of $R^2$. (Sequential completion does not have this problem).
\end{remark}
\begin{remark}
% REVIEW [claim]: The statement about every real/complex topological vector space being a quotient with all bounded sets finite-dimensional is nonstandard and needs a precise theorem/reference or deletion. [original line 2002]
    It is known that topological vector space over $\C$ or $\R$ is the quotient of a complete topological vector space $V$ over that field such that every bounded subset of $V$ is contained in a finite dimensional subspace.
\end{remark}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_11_7}
    Every quotient of a complete topological group with a countable neighborhood base of $1$ by a closed normal subgroup is complete.
\end{proposition}
\subsection{Continuous Valuations}
For an ordered group $\Gamma$ equip $\Gamma\cup\{0\}$ with the topology where $U$ is open if and only if $0\not\in U$ or there is $\gamma\in\Gamma$ such that $[0,\gamma)_\Gamma\subseteq U$.
\par Consider a valuation $A\stackrel{v}{\to}\Gamma$ where $A$ is a topological ring. Recall that $\Gamma_v$ is the subgroup of $\Gamma$ such that
\begin{equation*}
    \Gamma_v = \langle\{v(a)\sep a\in A\backslash\supp v\}\rangle_\Gamma.
\end{equation*}
Also recall that for $a\in A$ and $\gamma\in \Gamma$,
\begin{equation*}
    K_{v<a} = \{\alpha\in A\sep v(\alpha)<v(a)\},K_{v<\gamma} = \{\alpha\in A\sep v(\alpha)<\gamma\}.
\end{equation*}
Similarly,
\begin{equation*}
    K_{v\leq a} = \{\alpha\in A\sep v(\alpha)\leq v(a)\},K_{v\leq\gamma} = \{\alpha\in A\sep v(\alpha)\leq\gamma\}.
\end{equation*}
They come with an exception such that
\begin{equation*}
    K_{v<a}= \emptyset,
\end{equation*}
when $a\in\supp v$. They are subgroups of $(A,+)$.
\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_fact_12_1}
    Let $G$ be a topological group and $H\subseteq G$ be a subgroup. Then $H$ is open if and only if $H$ is a neighborhood of the identity (i.e. $H$ contains an open neighborhood of $0$).
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $e_G\in U\subseteq H$ be an open neighborhood. Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        H = \bigcup_{h\in H}hU,
    \end{equation*}
    which is open. For the other direction, simply take $U=H$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_12_1}
% REVIEW [proof]: v^{-1}({v(a)}) is not generally equal to a+K_{v<a}; only the inclusion a+K_{v<a} subset v^{-1}({v(a)}) is needed. [original line 2040]
    Let $A$ be a topological ring and $v:A\to\Gamma\cup\{0\}$ be a valuation. The following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A\stackrel{v}{\to}\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}$ is continuous.
        \item For all element $\gamma$ of the convex hull of $\Gamma_v$ in $\Gamma$, $K_{v<\gamma}$ is open.
        \item For all $\gamma\in\Gamma_v$, $K_{v<\gamma}$ is open.
        \item For all $a\in A\backslash\supp(v)$, $K_{v<a}$ is open.
        \item For all $a\in A\backslash\supp(v)$, $K_{v<a}$ is a neighborhood of $0$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    By Lemma \ref{lemma_fact_12_1} d) and e) are equivalent. d)$\Rightarrow$c) is trivial.
    \par For c)$\Rightarrow$a, consider $\gamma\in\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}$ and a neighborhood $U$ of $\gamma$ in $\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}$. We must show that $v^{-1}(U)$ is a neighborhood of $a$ in $A$. If $v(a)\not=0$, it is sufficient to consider
    \begin{equation*}
        U = \{v(a)\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        v^{-1}(U) = K_{r<a}+a.
    \end{equation*}
    which is a neighborhood of $a$. If $v(a)=0$, we may assume $U=[0,\gamma)_{\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}}$ with $\gamma\in\Gamma_v$. Then $v^{-1}(U)=K_{v<\gamma}$ is open by the assumption c).
    \par a$\Rightarrow$d) is trivial by the definition of the topology on $\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}$, that is for an open set $[0,v(a))_{\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}}$, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
     K_{v<a} = v^{-1}([0,v(a))_{\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}}).
    \end{equation*}
    \par b)$\Rightarrow$ c) is trivial. For d)$\Rightarrow$b), every $\gamma\in\Gamma_v$ has the form,
    \begin{equation*}
        \gamma  = {\frac {v(a)} {v(b)}},
    \end{equation*}
    with $a,b\in A\backslash\supp v$. Then $K_{v<\gamma}$ is the inverse image of $K_{v<a}$ under the endomorphism $A\stackrel{\cdot b}{\to}A$ of the topological group $(A,+)$. Hence it is also an open subgroup. For general element $\gamma$ of the convex hull, by definition there is $\delta\in\Gamma_v,\delta\leq 1$ such that
    \begin{equation}
        \delta\leq \gamma\leq \delta^{-1}.
    \end{equation}
    Then $K_{v<\delta}\subseteq K_{v<\gamma}$ and in particular, the former is an open subgroup. Therefore, the statement is proven.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_12_1}
    A valuation of $A$ is called continuous if the equivalent conditions in Proposition \ref{fact_12_1} hold.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}$\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item  This only depends on the equivalent class of $v$.
        \item $\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}$ carries the topology induced from $\Gamma\cup\{0\}$ only if $\Gamma$ is the convex hull of $\Gamma_v$ in $\Gamma$. Hence $\Gamma$ can be replaced by $\Gamma_v$ in c) only if $\Gamma$ is equal to that convex hull (in which case the c) is b)).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}
\begin{example}$\:$
    \label{example_12_1}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $\Q_p\stackrel{\ab{\cdot}_p}{\to}[0,\infty)_\R$ is a continuous valuation.
        \item The trivial valuation for $\p\in\Spec A$ is continuous if and only if $\p$ is open.
% REVIEW [word/math]: A valuation is continuous, not "open". State: the trivial valuation with support p is continuous iff p is open. [original line 2089]
        \item If $\p$ is an open prime ideal, then any valuation with support $\p$ is open.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{example}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_12_2}
    If $v$ is a continuous valuation. Then $\supp v$ is closed.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Indeed $\supp v = v^{-1}(\{0\})$ and $\{0\}$ is closed in $\Gamma_v\cup\{0\}$.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_12_2}
    We denote the subset of equivalence classes of continuous valuations of $A$ to be $\Cont(A)\subseteq\Spv(A)$.
    Let $A^+\subseteq A$ be a subset we then denote $\Spa(A,A^+)$ to be the set of equivalence classes of continuous valuations $v$ of $A$ such that $a\in A^+\Rightarrow v(a)\leq 1$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}$\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item At this point, we do not yet introduce a topology on these sets.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_12_3}
    A Oka system is a ring $A$ and a set $\mathfrak{M}$ of ideals in $A$ such that for an $I$ in $A$ and $a\in A$, the ideals $I+aA$ and
    \begin{equation*}
        (I:a) \defeq \{\alpha\in A\sep a\alpha\in I\}
    \end{equation*}
    both belong to $\mathfrak{M}$ then $I\in\mathfrak{M}$.
\end{definition}
\begin{example}
    \label{example_12_2}
% REVIEW [attribution]: The criterion "ring Noetherian iff every prime ideal is finitely generated" is Cohen's theorem; Eakin-Nagata is a different result. [original line 2119]
    The set of finitely generated ideals in $A$ form a Oka system. This observation gives Eakin-Nagata or Cohen's theorem which state that a ring is Noetherian if and only if all of its prime ideals are finitely generated.
\end{example}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_12_1}
    Let $\mathfrak{M}$ be an Oka system and $\p\not=A$ be a $\subseteq$-maximal element of the set of ideals of $A$ not belonging to $\mathfrak{M}$. Then $\p\in\Spec A$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Assume $ab\in\p$. Let $a\not\in \p,b\not\in\p$. Then $\p+aA$ and $(\p:a)$
    contain $\p$ and not equal to $\p$ as $b\in(\p:a)$. By the maximality, both belong to $\mathfrak{M}$ thus $\p\in\mathfrak{M}$ which is a contradiction.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_12_3}
    Let $\mathfrak{M}$ be a set of ideals such that $I\subseteq J$ and $I\in\mathfrak{M}$ implies $J\in\mathfrak{M}$. Suppose further that $\mathfrak{M}$ is closed under products, that is $I,J\in\mathfrak{M}$ then $I\cdot J\in\mathfrak{M}$. Then $\mathfrak{M}$ is an Oka system.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Indeed $I\supseteq (I+aA)(I:a)$ holds. Therefore by definition, it is Oka.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
    The Oka system from Example \ref{example_12_2} satisfies the assumptions if and only if $A$ is Noetherian.
\end{remark}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_12_3}
    The set $\mathfrak{M}_S$ of ideals not disjoint from a multiplicative subset $S\subseteq A$ is an Oka system. Then every $\subseteq$-maximal non-element of $\mathfrak{M}_S$ is a prime ideal. This could also be seen by that all maximal ideals of $A_S$ are prime. By Zorn's lemma, every ideal not belonging to $\mathfrak{M}_S$ is contained in an ideal which is maximal with respect to this non-containment.
    By Proposition \ref{proposition_12_1}, such ideal is prime, or even more maximal.
\end{corollary}
% REVIEW [CRITICAL]: Condition (ii) must use v(ab)>1, not >=1. With >=1 a trivial valuation gives a counterexample. The next proposition actually constructs >1. [original line 2144]
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_12_4}
    Let $v$ be a valuation of a topological ring $A$ satisfying the following conditions.
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
    \item $K_{v\leq 1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$.
    \item For each $a\in A\backslash\supp v$, there is $b\in A$ with $v(ab)\geq1$.
    \end{enumerate}
    Then $v$ is continuous.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    If $a\in A\backslash\supp v$, and $b$ is as above, then $K_{v<a}$ contains the inverse image under the continuous endomorphism, $A\stackrel{\cdot b}{\to} A$ of $K_{r\leq 1}$ which is a neighborhood of $0$. Then Proposition \ref{fact_12_1} e) is satisfied.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_12_2}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring, $A^+$ be an open subring of $A$ which is integrally closed in $A$ and $\alpha\in A\backslash A^+$. Then there is $v\in\Spa(A,A^+)$ such that $v(\alpha)>1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    By assumption, $\alpha$ is not integral over $A^+$, hence $S=\{p(\alpha)\sep p\in A^+[T]\text{ monic}\}$ is a multiplicative subset of $A$ not containing $0$. By Corollary \ref{corollary_12_3}, there is $\p\in\Spec A$ maximal among all ideals of $A$ disjoint from $S$. It follows from the maximality of $\p$, that for every $a\in A\backslash\p$, there is $b\in A$ and a monic polynomial $P\in A^+[T]$ such that $P(\alpha) -ab\in\p$. Since $\p$ is disjoint from $S$, the image $\alpha\tmod\p$ of $\alpha$ in $k(\p)$ is not integral over the image $A^\#$ of $A^+$ in $k(\p)$. By Proposition \ref{proposition_8_2}, there is a valuation ring $R$ of $k(\p)$ such that $A^\#\subseteq R$ and $\alpha\not\in R$. Consider a map $v$ which is the composition given by,
    \begin{equation*}
        A\to k(\p)\stackrel{\text{$v_R$ in Prop. \ref{proposition_7_1}}}{\to}\Gamma_R.
    \end{equation*}
    Since $\alpha\tmod\p\not\in R, v(\alpha)>1$. By the definition, $K_{v\leq1}$ contains $A^+$ as $A^+\tmod\p\subseteq A^\#\subseteq R$, hence $K_{v\leq1}$ is open. If $a\in A$ and $v(a)\not=0$, then $a\not\in \p$ and we have $b\in A$ and a monic $P\in A^+[T]$ such that $P(\alpha)-ab\in\p$. Since $P$ is monic and $v(A^+)\subseteq[0,1]_{\Gamma\cup\{0\}}$, $v(P(\alpha))>1$. Since $\supp v=\p$, $v(ab)=v(P(\alpha))>1$. By Proposition \ref{fact_12_4}, $v$ is continuous.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_12_1}
    For a subset $A^+$ of $A$, the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A^+ = \bigcap_{x\in\Spa(A,A^+)} K_{x\leq 1} = \{a\in A\sep \ab{a}_x\leq 1$ for all $x\in Spa(A,A^+)\}$
        \item $A^+$ is the intersection of a set of open and integrally closed subrings of $A$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    For b$\Rightarrow$a, note that $\subseteq $ in a is trivial and $\supseteq$ is by Proposition \ref{proposition_12_2}. For a$\Rightarrow$b, note that $K_{x\leq1}$ for $x\in\Spa(A,A^+)$ are integrally closed by Proposition \ref{fact_6_5} and open subrings of $A$ by Proposition \ref{fact_12_1}.a.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_13_5}
    Let $\eta\in\Cont(A)$ and $s\in\Spv(A)$ a specialization of $\eta$. Then $s\in\Cont(A)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $a\not\in\supp s$. Then by Proposition \ref{fact_6_4}, $\supp\eta\subseteq\supp s$. Hence $\ab{a}_{\eta}\not=0$. Thus $K_{\eta<a}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$ by Proposition \ref{fact_12_1}.e. By Proposition \ref{fact_6_6}, $K_{\eta<a}\subseteq K_{s<a}$ hence $K_{s<a}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$ and again by Proposition \ref{fact_12_1}.e, hence for $s$ as well.
\end{proof}
% REVIEW [type]: An ultrafilter on Spa(A,A+) cannot meaningfully be said to contain Cont(A) unless the ambient set is specified. This sentence likely means an ultrafilter on Spv(A) with Cont(A) in it. [original line 2185]
Without further assumptions, $\Spa$ and $\Cont$ (to be equipped with a topology below) fail to be spectral spaces and there are ultrafilters $\U$ on $\Spa$ such that $\Cont(A)\in\U$ but $\glim_{\Spa A}\U$ fails to have any continuous specializations.
\par The following condition on $A$ are often useful.
\begin{align*}
    &\text{If $I\subseteq A$ is an open ideal, $I^2$ is also open} \tag{oisq}\\
    &\text{Every open ideal $I\subseteq A$ contains a finitely generated open ideal of $A$.}\tag{oifin}
\end{align*}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_13_6}
    If $I$ and $J$ are open ideals of a topological ring $A$ satisfying (oisq), then $IJ$ is open. In particular, by induction on $n$, all $I^n$ are open.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    As $I\cap J$ is open $(I\cap J)^2 \subseteq I\cdot J$ is also open by (oisq).
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_13_6_dash}
    If $a\in A^\dcirc$ and $x\in\Cont(A)$, then $\ab{a}_x<1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    By assumption, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\ab{a}_x^n = 0
    \end{equation*}
    in $\Gamma_x\cup\{0\}$. By the continuity of $x$, the above equality does not hold if $\ab{a}_x\geq1$.
\end{proof}
We further introduce following conditions.
\begin{align*}
    &\text{The ideal generated by $A^{\dcirc}$ of $A$ is open}\tag{tn0}\\
    &\text{$A^{\dcirc}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$.}\tag{tn1}
\end{align*}
Obviously (tn1)$\Rightarrow$(tn0).
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_13_7}
    If $A$ contains an open subring $B$ satisfying one of (oifin),(oisq), (tn0),(tn1), then $A$ also has that property.
\end{proposition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_13_4}
% REVIEW [notation]: "x in Spa(A)" is undefined here; the intended ambient set is Spv(A). [original line 2221]
    We say that $x\in\Spa(A)$ can be made continuous if $K_{x<1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
    The condition in Definition \ref{definition_13_4} is already satisfied if $x$ is continuous by Proposition \ref{fact_12_1} e).
\end{remark}
\begin{proposition}$\:$
    \label{fact_13_8}
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item If $x\in\Spv(A)$ can be made continuous then $K_{x<\gamma}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ where $\gamma\in\cGamma_x$.
        \item If $K_{x\leq 1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ then $K_{x<\gamma}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ where $\gamma\in\cGamma_x\backslash\{0\}$.
        \item If $K_{x\leq 1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$, then $\cGamma_x=\{1\}$ or $x$ can be made continuous.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    For b, If $\gamma>1$ then $K_{x\leq1}\subseteq K_{x<\gamma}$ and the claim follows. Otherwise $\gamma<1$ and there is $a\in A$ such that $\gamma\ab{a}_x\geq1$. In that case, $\gamma\ab{a}_x^2>1$ and $K_{x<\gamma}$ contains the preimage under the continuous map $A\stackrel{a^2}{\to} A$ of $K_{x\leq1}$, a neighborhood of $0$.
    \par a. For $\gamma=1$, this follows from Definition \ref{definition_13_4} and for $\gamma\not=1$, we can apply b).
    \par c. Assume $\cGamma_x\not=\{1\}$ so we can pick $\gamma\in\cGamma_x\backslash\{1\}$. Without loss of generality, assume $\gamma<1$. Then $K_{x<1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ as it contains $K_{x<\gamma}$, a neighborhood of $0$ by b.
\end{proof}

% REVIEW [major]: The proof that sGamma_eta is a subgroup never proves closure under inverses, and the stated definition appears to make every gamma>1 belong once K_{eta<1} is a neighborhood. This needs a careful re-check against the intended continuation construction. [original line 2240]
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_13_3}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring with (oisq) and (tn0), and let $\eta\in\Spv(A)$. Assume $\ab{x}_\eta<1$ for all $x\in A^{\dcirc}$. In the following the word "specialization" always means "specialization in $\Spv$".
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item There is a continuous specialization of $\eta$ if and only if $\eta$ can be made continuous.
        \item If this is the case, then,
            \begin{enumerate}[I.]
                \item The following subset of $\Gamma_\eta$ is a convex subgroup containing $\cGamma_\eta$.
                \begin{equation*}
                    s\Gamma_\eta \defeq \{\gamma\in\Gamma_\eta\sep K_{\eta<r}\text{ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$}\}.
                \end{equation*}
                \item The horizontal specialization $s=\eta|_{s\Gamma_\eta}$, which is well-defined by I, is continuous.
                \item The sets of specializations of $s$ and of continuous specializations of $\eta$ coincide.
                \item Let $\Omega=\mathcal{R}(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$ with $(f_i)_{i=0}^{m+1}\in A^{m+1}$ be such that the ideal of $A$ generated by $K_{\eta\leq f_0}$ is open, then $s\in\Omega$ if and only if $\eta\in\Omega$.
            \end{enumerate}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Assume that $\eta$ has the continuous specialization $s$. As $K_{s\leq 1}\subseteq K_{\eta\leq 1}$ by Proposition \ref{fact_6_7} and $K_{s<1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ by Proposition \ref{fact_12_1}.e, $K_{\eta\leq1}$ is a neighborhood of $0$. By Proposition \ref{fact_13_8}, $\cGamma_\eta=\{1\}$ or $\eta$ can be made continuous.
    If $\cGamma_x=\{1\}$, then $A=K_{\eta\leq1}$ and $K_{\eta<1}$ is an ideal in $A$. By assumption about $\eta$, $A^\dcirc\subseteq K_{\eta<1}$ and thus $A^\dcirc$ contains an open ideal by (tn0). Hence $K_{\eta<1}$ is an open ideal of $A$. Therefore $\eta$ can be made continuous.
    \par Assume that $\eta$ can be made continuous. To finish a, and to show b, it suffices to show I-IV under this assumption.
    The inclusion $\cGamma_\eta \subseteq s\Gamma_\eta$ follows from Proposition \ref{fact_13_8}.a. The convexity of $s\Gamma_\eta$ as a neighborhood of $\Gamma_\eta$ then follows from Proposition \ref{fact_6_8.5}.
    To show that it is indeed a group, we first show that $\gamma\in s\Gamma_\eta\Rightarrow \gamma^2\in s\Gamma_\eta$. By the convexity, we can assume $\gamma\leq 1$. If $\gamma\in\cGamma_\eta$ then it holds as $\gamma^2\in\cGamma_\eta$. Otherwise, there is $a\in A$ such that $\gamma\not\in[\ab{a}_\eta,\ab{a^{-1}}_\eta]_{\Gamma\cup\{0\}}$ thus $\gamma\ab{a}_\eta<1$ for all $a\in A$.
    Let $I$ be the ideal generated by $K_{\eta<\gamma}$ in $A$. Let $U\subseteq K_{\eta<\gamma}$ be an open neighborhood of $0$, then
    \begin{equation*}
        I = \bigcup_{x\in I}(x+U).
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore $I$ is open. By (oisq) and Proposition \ref{fact_13_6}, $I^3$ is open. For $\alpha\in I^3$, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
        \alpha = \sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_ic_id_i,
    \end{equation*}
    where $a_i\in A$, and $b_i,c_i,d_i\in K_{\eta<\gamma}$ for each $i$. Hence,
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{a_ib_i}_\eta=\ab{a_i}_\eta\ab{b_i}_\eta <\ab{a_i}_\eta\gamma<1.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
    \ab{\alpha}_\eta \leq \max_{1\leq i\leq n}\{\ab{a_ib_ic_id_i}\}\leq \max_{1\leq i\leq n}\{\ab{c_id_i}\} <\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\gamma\cdot\gamma=\gamma^2
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore $I^3\subseteq K_{\eta<\gamma^2}$. In particular $\gamma^2\in s\Gamma_\eta$.
    Finally, let $\gamma_1,\gamma_2\in s\Gamma_\eta$. Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        (\min\{\gamma_1,\gamma_2\})^2\leq \gamma_1\gamma_2\leq(\max\{\gamma_1,\gamma_2\})^2.
    \end{equation*}
    By the convexity of $s\Gamma,\gamma1\gamma_2\in s\Gamma$.
% REVIEW [incomplete sentence]: "Let a in A be such that |a|_eta." is unfinished. The intended choice is an a outside supp(s), then show K_{s<a} is a neighborhood. [original line 2285]
    \par For II, note that as $s$ is a specialization, we have $K_{\eta<a}\subseteq K_{s<a}$. Let $a\in A$ be such that $\ab{a}_\eta$. Then $K_{s<a}$ is a neighborhood of $0$. By Proposition \ref{fact_12_1}.e, $s$ is continuous.
    \par For IV, by Proposition \ref{fact_7_10}, it suffices to show that $\ab{f_0}_s\not=0$ so we can drop the condition. That is $\ab{f_0}_\eta\in s\Gamma_\eta$. if $\ab{f_0}_\eta\in\cGamma_\eta$, then this follows from I. Otherwise $\ab{a}_\eta\ab{f_0}_\eta<1$ for all $a\in A$.
    Moreover, the ideal $I$ of $A$ generated by $K_{\eta\leq f_0}$ is open by our assumption. By (oisq), $I^2$ is also open. Its elements have the form, $\alpha=\sum_i^n a_ib_ic_i$ with all $a_i\in A, b_i,c_i\in K_{\eta\leq f_0}$. Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{\alpha}_\eta\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\{\ab{a_i}_\eta\ab{b_i}_\eta\ab{c_i}_\eta\}<\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\ab{c_i}_\eta\leq\ab{f_0}_\eta.
    \end{equation*}
    Then $K_{\eta<f_0}$ contains the open ideal $I^2$, hence is a neighborhood of $0$. Thus $\ab{f_0}_\eta\in s\Gamma_\eta$ as claimed.
    \par For III, every specialization of a continuous valuation is continuous by Proposition \ref{fact_13_5}. In particular, specializations of $s$ are all continuous by II.
    Let $t\in \Spv(A)$, be any continuous specialization of $\eta$. For every rational open subset $\Omega = \cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_n)$, we want to show that $t\in\Omega$ implies $s\in\Omega$. But $t\in\Omega$ implies $\ab{f_0}_t\not=0$ by definition.
    Hence $K_{t< f_0}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ by Proposition \ref{fact_12_1} d.
    Also $K_{t< f_0}\subseteq K_{t\leq f_0}$ thus $K_{t\leq f_0}$ is a neighborhood of $0$. By Proposition \ref{fact_6_7} and  $K_{t\leq f_0}\subseteq K_{\eta\leq f_0}$, $K_{\eta\leq f_0}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ as well. Apply IV with $\eta\in\Omega$, $s\in\Omega$ follows as $t\in\Omega$ since $t$ is a specialization of $\eta$.
    \par We explicitly constructed a continuous specialization of $\eta$. Thus a is also shown.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    The horizontal specialization $s = \eta|_{s\Gamma_\eta}$ in Proposition \ref{proposition_13_3} is called the continuation of $\eta$.
\end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_14_5}
    The strong topology on $\Cont(A)$ or on $\Spa(A,A^+)$ is the one induced from $\Spv(A)$. That is for which the set of all $\cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$ for arbitrary $n\in\N$ and $(f_i)_{i=0}^n\in A^{n+1}$ form a topology base.
    \par If $A$ satisfies (oisq), the weak topology on both spaces is the one for which the set $\Rat_A = \Rat_A^{(t)}$ of $\cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_n)$ for which $\langle f_0,\cdots,f_n\rangle_A$ is an open ideal of $A$ is a topology base.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    Note that $\cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)\cap\cR(g_0|g_1,\cdots,g_m) = \cR(f_0g_0|f_ig_j)$ and
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_ig_j\rangle_A = \langle f_0,\cdots,f_n \rangle_A \langle g_0,\cdots,g_m\rangle_A
    \end{equation*}
    is open by (oisq). Hence $\Rat_A^{(t)}$ is closed under finite intersection. In most case, $A$ will be equipped with a topology and may the notation $(t)$ be dropped.
    When the ring is equipped with a topology only the elements of $\Rat_A^{(t)}$ will be called rational open subsets of $\Cont(A)$ or $\Spa(A,A^+)$.
\end{remark}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_14_9}
    If $A$ has (oisq) and (oifin), then these topologies coincide.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $U = \cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_n)\subseteq X$ where $X=\Cont(A)$ or $X=\Spa(A,A^+)$. For $x\in U$, we want to show that there is $\Omega\in\Rat_A^{(t)}$ such that $x\in\Omega\subseteq U$. As $x$ is a continuous valuation, $K_{x<f_0}$ is open and by (oifin), there are finitely many $\varepsilon_1,\cdots,\varepsilon_l\in K_{x<f_0}$ such that $\langle \varepsilon_1,\cdots,\varepsilon_l\rangle_A$ is open.
    This case
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_0,\cdots,f_n,\varepsilon_1,\cdots,\varepsilon_n\rangle_A
    \end{equation*}
    is open as it contains an open ideal. Then $x\in\Omega=\cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_n,\varepsilon_1,\cdots,\varepsilon_l)\subseteq U$ and $\Omega\in\Rat_A^{(t)}$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_14_10}
    $\Cont(A)$ and $\Spa(A,A^+)$ are $T_0$ for the strong topology.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_14_4}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring with (oisq), (oifin) and (tn0). For an ultrafilter $\U$ on $\Cont(A)$, let $\cV$ be its direct image on $\Spv(A)$ by the inclusion $\iota:\Cont(A)\to\Spv(A)$. The following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $\U$ has a generic limit in $\Cont(A)$.
        \item $\U$ has some limit in $\Cont(A)$.
        \item The generic limit $g=\glim_{\Spv(A)}\cV$ of $\cV$ in $\Spv A$ can be made continuous.
        \item The set
        \begin{equation}
            \{a\in A\sep \{v\in\Cont(A)\sep \ab{a}_x<1\}\in\U\}
            \label{equation_14_4}
        \end{equation}
        is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$.
    \end{enumerate}
    In this case, a generic limit $\gamma$ of $\U$ in $\Cont(A)$ is given by the continuation $g|_{s\Gamma_g}$ of $g$. If $\Spa(A,A^+)\in\U$, then $\gamma\in\Spa(A,A^+)$ and $\gamma$ is a generic limit of the intersection of $\U$ with the set of subsets of $\Spa(A,A^+)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    a$\Rightarrow$b is obvious. For b$\Rightarrow$c, first observe that due to Proposition \ref{fact_14_9}, the strong and weak topologies coincide in our case. If $\lambda$ is any limit of $\U$, it is a limit of $\cV$ in $\Spv(A)$.
    Hence $\lambda$ is a specialization of $g$ in $\Spv(A)$. By Proposition \ref{proposition_13_3} a, $g$ can be made continuous.
    \par For c$\Rightarrow $ a, if $g$ can be made continuous and $\gamma$ is its continuation. $\gamma$ is in the closure of $g=\glim_{\Spv(A)}\cV$, hence is a limit of $\cV$. As $\Cont(A)$ carries the induced topology, it is a limit of $\U$ in $\Cont(A)$. Let $\lambda$ be any limit of $\U$ in $\Cont(A)$. Then $\lambda$ is a limit of $\cV$ in $\Spv(A)$. Hence by Proposition \ref{proposition_13_3}.b.II, $\lambda$ is a specialization of $\gamma$ in $\Spv(A)$. Using once again the fact that $\Cont(A)$ carries the induced topology, $\lambda$ is a specialization of $\gamma$ in $\Cont(A)$, showing that $\gamma$ is a $\glim_{\Cont(A)}\U$.
% REVIEW [incomplete]: Sentence "We have shown ... and also the" is unfinished. [original line 2352]
    \par We have shown the equivalence of first three statement and also the
    \par Observe that
    \begin{equation*}
        \{x\in\Cont(A)\sep \ab{a}_x<1\} = \{x\in\Spv(A)\sep \ab{a}_x<1\}\cap\Cont(A).
    \end{equation*}
    Also $a\in A$ belongs to the set in \eqref{equation_14_4} if and only if
    \begin{equation*}
        M_a = \{x\in\Spv(A)\sep \ab{a}_x<1\}\in\cV.
    \end{equation*}Therefore, we can rewrite \eqref{equation_14_4} as,
    \begin{equation*}
        \{a\in A\sep M_a\in\cV\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Now note that
    \begin{equation*}
        a\in K_{g<1}\Leftrightarrow g\not\in\cR(a|1)\Leftrightarrow \cR(a|1)\not\in \mathcal{V}\Leftrightarrow M_a\in\mathcal{V}.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore, we see,
    \begin{equation*}
        K_{g<1} = \{a\in A\sep \{v\in\Cont(A)\sep \ab{a}_x<1\}\in\U\}.
    \end{equation*}
    By definition, this is a neighborhood of $0$ if and only if $g$ can be made continuous. Thus we obtained c$\Leftrightarrow$d.
    \par Finally let $\Spa(A,A^+)\in\U\subseteq\cV$. We have
    \begin{equation}
        \Spa(A,A^+)=\left[\bigcap_{\alpha\in A^+}\cR_{\Spv(A)}(1|\alpha)\right]\cap\Cont(A)
        \label{equation_14_4_2}
    \end{equation}
    and since the open sets $\cR_{\Spv(A)}(1|\alpha)$ are all closed under generic limits, thus $g$ is contained in all of them, hence $\ab{\alpha}_g\leq1$ where $\alpha\in A^+$. Since $\gamma$ is a horizontal specialization of $g$, this implies $\ab{\alpha}_\gamma\leq1$. As $\gamma$ is continuous, by \eqref{equation_14_4_2}, $\gamma\in\Spa(A,A^+)$.
    Since $\Spa(A,A^+)$ carries the topology induced from $\Cont(A)$ and $\Spa(A,A^+)\in\U$, using Proposition \ref{proposition_bijection_ultrafilters}
    \begin{equation*}
        \U' =\{U\in\U\sep U\subseteq \Spa(A,A^+)\}
    \end{equation*}
    is also an ultrafilter and $\gamma$ is its limit.
    If $\lambda$ is any limit of $\U'$ in $\Spa(A,A^+)$, it is a limit of the image $\U$ of $\U'$ under the continuous map $\Spa(A,A^+)\to \Cont(A)$. Hence it is a specialization of $\gamma$ in $\Cont(A)$. Hence also in $\Spa(A,A^+)$ which has the induced topology.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_14_2}
    If $A$ is a topological ring satisfying (oisq),(oifin), and (tn1). For $X=\Cont(A)$ or $\Spa(A,A^+$) for some subset $A^+$, $X$ is spectral and the elements of $\Rat_X^{(t)}$ are quasi-compact open subsets of $X$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\U$ be an ultrafilter on $\Cont(A)$. If $a\in A^\dcirc$, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \{x\in\Cont(A)\sep \ab{a}_x<1\}=\Cont(A)\in\U
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [logical order]: Spectrality is invoked before the rational basis is shown closed under generic limits. First establish generic limits and a glim-closed basis, then apply the spectral characterization. [original line 2395]
    By Proposition \ref{proposition_14_4}, $A^{\dcirc}\subseteq K_{g<1}$. By (tn1), $g$ can be made continuous. Therefore every ultrafilter on $\Cont(A)$ admits a unique generic limit. Hence, $\Cont(A)$ is spectral by Proposition \ref{proposition_4}. Furthermore, $X$ is also spectral using the closing claim of Proposition \ref{proposition_14_4}.
    \par We now show that the elements of $\Rat_X^{(t)}$ are quasi-compact. By Proposition \ref{proposition_4}, we need to show that these are closed under taking $\glim_X$. Observe that we have a canonical inclusion $\iota:X\hookrightarrow \Cont(A)$. Let,
    \begin{align*}
        \Omega & = \cR_{\Spv(A)}(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m),\\
        \Omega' &= \cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)=\Omega\cap X.
    \end{align*}
    with $I=\langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle_A$ being open. As $\Omega$ is closed under $\glim_{\Spv(A)}$ and again by the closing line of Proposition \ref{proposition_14_4} and $\Omega' = \Omega\cap X$, $\Omega'$ is also closed under taking $\glim_X$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
% REVIEW [proof]: Showing every horizontal specialization is continuous is not enough; use closedness of x to conclude it has no proper horizontal specialization, hence cGamma_x=Gamma_x. [original line 2405]
    \label{fact_15_11}
    If $X = \Cont(A)$ or $\Spa(A,A^+)$, and $x\in X$ is closed then $\cGamma_x = \Gamma_x$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Clearly horizontal specializations preserve boundedness on $A^+$, thus we only need to show that when $X=\Cont(A)$. Let $s$ be a horizontal specialization of $x$, then by the definition of horizontal specializations, we see that for all $\gamma\in\Gamma_s$,
    \begin{equation*}
        K_{s<\gamma} = K_{x<\gamma}.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore, $K_{s<\gamma}$ is open for all $s\in\Gamma_s$. Thus $s$ is continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_15_12}
    For a topological ring $A$, let $X = \Cont(A)$ or $\Spa(A,A^+)$. Suppose $s\preceq_X \eta$ and $x\preceq_{\Spv(A)}\eta$ such that $s\preceq_{\Spv(A)}x$.
    Show that $x\in X$ and $s\preceq_X x\preceq_X \eta$ also holds.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    By Proposition \ref{fact_6_7}, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
    A^+\subseteq K_{s\leq 1}\subseteq K_{x\leq 1}.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore we reduce the statement to show when $X=\Cont(A)$. When $a\in A\backslash \supp(x) $ we have,
    \begin{equation*}
    K_{\eta<a}\subseteq K_{x<a}.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore $x$ is continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
\label{proposition_15_5}
Let $A$ be a topological ring and $\mathcal{M}$ be the set of open ideals in $A$.
\begin{enumerate}[a.]
    \item If $A$ has (oifin) then every ideal $I$ not in $\mathcal{M}$ is contained in some ideal of $A$ which is $\subseteq$-maximal in the set of ideals of $A$ not in $\mathcal{M}$.
    \item If $A$ has (oisq), then $\mathcal{M}$ is an Oka-system. If $\p$ is any ideal of $A$ which is $\subseteq$-maximal among ones which are not in $\mathcal{M}$. Then $\p$ is a prime ideal such that either it is closed or it is a dense maximal ideal.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\mathcal{N}$ be the set of ideals in $A$ which are not open. For a, let $\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{N}$ be linearly $\subseteq$-ordered non-empty subset. Let $I = \bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{L}}J$. Then $I$ is an ideal in $A$. If $I$ is open then by (oifin), there are $f_1,\cdots,f_m\in I$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        K = \langle f_1,\cdots,f_m\rangle_A \subseteq I
    \end{equation*}
    is open. As $\mathcal{L}$ is linearly ordered, there is $J\in\mathcal{L}$ containing all $f_i$. Hence $K\subseteq J$ and $J$ is open. This contradicts to that $J\in\mathcal{N}$. Applying Zorn's lemma, we derived the statement.
    \par For b, $\mathcal{M}$ is closed under product by Proposition \ref{fact_13_6} and any ideal containing an open ideal is also open, it is an Oka system by Proposition \ref{fact_12_3}. That $\p$ must be prime follows from Proposition \ref{proposition_12_1}.
    If $\p$ is not closed let $a\in\overline{\p}\backslash\p$. As $\overline{\p}$ is an ideal and $\p$ is prime, $a^2\in\overline{\p}\backslash\p$. Now, $\p+a^2A$ is open by the maximality of $\p$, as this is an ideal, and by Proposition \ref{fact_11_2}, this is also closed. By the minimality of closures, $\overline{\p} = \p+a^2A$.
    Since $a\in\overline{\p} = \p+a^2A$, there is $b\in A$ such that $a - a^2b\in\p$. As $a\not\in\p, 1 -ab\in \p$. Thus $1\in\overline{\p}$ as $a\in\overline{\p}$ and $\overline{\p}$ is an ideal.
    Now the previous consideration can be applied to all $a\in A$, and show that $A/\p$ is a field. Thus $\p$ is a dense maximal ideal.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_15_7}
    A continuous valuation $v$ of $A$ and its equivalence class is called analytic if the closed prime ideal $\supp v$ fails to be open. We denote
    \begin{align*}
        \Cont(A)_a &= \{x\in\Cont(A)\sep \text{$x$ is analytic}\},\\
% REVIEW [set]: Spa(A,A+)_a should be defined as analytic points INSIDE Spa(A,A+), not as {x in Cont(A)}. [original line 2459]
        \Spa(A,A^+)_a &= \{x\in\Cont(A)\sep\text{$x$ is analytic}\}.
    \end{align*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_15_14} For a topological ring $A$ with (tn0), the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
        \item The ideal of $A$ generated by $A^\dcirc$ is $A$.
        \item A has no open maximal ideal.
        \item $A$ has no open prime ideal.
        \item $A$ has no open proper ideal.
        \item For every subset $A^+\subseteq A$, all elements of $\Spa(A,A^+)$ are analytic.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [logic]: "iv=>ii=>ii" is a typo. Also i=>iv needs: if J is open and a in A^{oo}, some power a^n lies in J, hence A^{oo} subset sqrt(J), not A^{oo} subset J. [original line 2474]
    i$\Rightarrow$iv, as every open ideals contains $A^\dcirc$. iv$\Rightarrow$ii$\Rightarrow$ii are trivial.
    For ii$\Rightarrow$i, let $I= (A^\dcirc)_A$. Consider $B=A/I$, and recall that we have a correspondence of maximal ideals in $B$ and maximal ideals in $A$ containing $I$. But any ideal containing $I$ is open thus $B$ does not admit any maximal ideal which is possible only when $B=\{0\}$. Therefore $A=I$.
    \par iii$\Rightarrow$v is trivial as $\supp v$ is always prime. For v$\Rightarrow $iii, for every open prime ideal $\p$, we can consider a trivial valuation with support $\p$.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    A ring is called analytic if the ideal of $A$ generated by $A^\dcirc$ is $A$.
\end{definition}
\begin{example}
    Every ring with (E) ($A^\times$ intersects every neighborhood of $0$, see \textsection3.1) is analytic. In particular, every ring with (T) is analytic.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_15_15_1}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring with (tn0). A valuation of $A$ is not analytic if and only if its support contains $A^\dcirc$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    $\Leftarrow$ is clear. Let $x$ be a non-analytic valuation. As $\supp x$ is an open neighborhood of $0$, for each $a\in A^\dcirc$ there is $n\in\N$ such that $a^n\in\supp x$. But the support is prime therefore, $a\in\supp x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_15_15}
    When $A$ is a topological ring with (oifin) and (tn0) and $X=\Cont(A)$ or $\Spa(A,A^+)$. Then $X_a$ is a finite union of elements of $\Rat_X$. In particular, it is open.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    By Lemma \ref{lemma_15_15_1}, $x\in X_a$ if and only if $A^\dcirc\not\subseteq \supp x$. By our assumption, there are finitely many elements $f_1,\cdots,f_m\in A^\dcirc$ generating an open ideal in $A$. Then $\p\in\Spec A$ is open if and only if $f_1,\cdots,f_m\in\p$. Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
        X_a = \bigcup_{i=1}^m\cR_x(f_i|f_1,\cdots,f_m).
    \end{equation*}
    Note that these are only finitely many elements thus each valuation admits $i$ taking maximum among them.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    If only (tn0) holds then,
    \begin{equation*}
        X_a = \bigcup_{f\in A^\dcirc}\cR_X(f|)
    \end{equation*}
    is still open.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_15_16}
    Let $A\stackrel{f}{\to} B$ be a morphism of topological rings which is open. If $M\subseteq A$ is bounded then $f(M)$ is bounded in $B$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let $U\subseteq B$ be an open neighborhood of $0$. Since $M$ is bounded, there is $V\subseteq A$ open neighborhood of $0$ such that $V\cdot M\subseteq f^{-1}(U)$. Since $f$ is open, $f(V)$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $B$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_15_6}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring with (oisq), (oifin), and (tn0). Suppose further that it has no dense maximal ideals.
    Let $A^+$ be an open subring of $A$ which is a subset of $A^\circ$. For $m\in\N$ and $(a_i)_{i=1}^m\in A^m$, the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item The ideal $I=\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_A$ is open.
        \item For all $x\in\Spa(A,A^+)_a$, there is $1\leq i\leq m$ such that $\ab{a_i(x)}\not=0$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    By the definition, $\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{a_i}_x=0$ if and only if $I\subseteq\supp x$. If $I$ is open, no such $x$ can be analytic. Thus a$\Rightarrow$b.
% REVIEW [variables]: The proof switches between x and s for the chosen topologically nilpotent element. Choose one symbol consistently and, if needed, replace it by a power to put it in A+. [original line 2534]
    \par Assume that $I$ is not open. By Proposition \ref{proposition_15_5}, there is a closed prime ideal $\p$ such that $I\subseteq \p$ which is maximal among non-open ideals. By (tn0), there is $x\in A^\dcirc\backslash\p$. As $A^+$ is a neighborhood of $0$, without losing generality, (as $s^j\stackrel{j\to\infty}{\to} 0$), $x\in A^+$.
    We denote the image of $A^+$ in $A/\p\subseteq k(\p)$ by $B$. By Proposition \ref{fact_15_16},
    \begin{equation}
        B\subseteq(A/\p)^\circ.
    \end{equation}
    We now show that $(x\mod\p)^{-1}\in k(\p)$ is not integral over $B$. Suppose not, thus there will be $(p_i)^d_{i=1}\in B$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        1 = \sum_{i=1}^d p_i(x\mod\p)^i = (s\mod\p)\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}p_{i+1}(x\mod\p)^i \in (x\mod\p)B.
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [math]: "a sum of topologically nilpotent elements is topologically nilpotent" is false in this generality. Use 1=(s mod p)q with q powerbounded, so the product is topologically nilpotent. [original line 2543]
As $(x\mod\p)\in (A/\p)^\dcirc$ and each $p_i$ is powerbounded, $1$ is a sum of topologically nilpotent elements. Thus this implies that $1$ is topologically nilpotent in $A/\p$. This contradicts the fact that $\p$ is closed in $A$ as $1\not\in\p$ in $A$ but $1^k\stackrel{k\to\infty}{\to}0\in\p$.
By Proposition \ref{proposition_8_2}, there is a valuation ring $R$ of $k(\p)$ containing $B$ but not $(x\mod\p)^{-1}$. Let $v$ denote the composition of
\begin{equation*}
    A\to A/\p \to k(\p)\stackrel{\text{valuation defined by $R$}}{\to}(k(\p)^\times/R^\times)\cup\{0\}.
\end{equation*}
There is a valuation and the support $\p$ is not open and $v(a)\leq1$ for $a\in A^+$, as $a\mod\p\in B\subseteq R$ by construction.
Thus the equivalence class of $v$ is in $\Spa(A,A^+)_a$ and all $v(f_i)=0$ as $I\subseteq\p$. Hence to show that b cannot hold, it remains to show that $v$ is continuous.
% REVIEW [major]: The continuity argument in the remaining case (no a with v(as^m)>=1) contains unjustified containments and index mismatches; this part needs re-derivation. [original line 2550]
\par We first show that $K_{v\leq x^m}$ is a neighborhood of $0$, for all $m\in\N$. If $m=0$, this follows from $A^+\subseteq K_{v\leq 1}$ and the openness of $A^+$. If there is $a\in A$ such that $v(ax^m)\geq1$, then $K_{v\leq x^m}$ contains the preimage under $A\stackrel{a}{\to}A$ of $K_{v\leq1}$, hence it is a neighborhood of $0$. If no such $a$ exists then $K_{v\leq x^n}$ contains $\p+x^n A$ which is open by the maximality of $\p$.
Let $\alpha\in A\backslash\p$. Then $\p+\alpha A$ is an open ideal by the maximality of $\p$. Since $x\in A^\dcirc$, $x^n\in\p+\alpha A$ for some positive integer. Thus there is $b\in A$ such that $x^n-b\alpha\in\p$ and
\begin{equation*}
    v(b)v(\alpha) = v(x^r)
\end{equation*}
hence $v(b)v(\alpha)>v(x^{n+1})$ as $(s\mod{\p})^{-1}\not\in R$ and thus $v(s)<1$.
By the continuity and the previous part, $b^{-1}K_{v\leq s^{n+1}}$ is a neighborhood of $0$ which is contained in $K_{v<\alpha}$. As $\supp(v)=\p$, this shows $v$ is continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_16_3}
    Under the assumption of Proposition \ref{proposition_15_6} on $A$ and $A^+$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_A = A\iff \forall x\in \Spa(A,A^+), \max_{1\leq i\leq m}\vert a_i\vert_x >0.
     \end{equation*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    The necessity is obvious from Proposition \ref{proposition_15_6}. If the condition holds, $I=\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_A$ is open by Proposition \ref{proposition_15_6}. If it is different from $A$, then there is an open maximal ideal $\m$ such that $I\subseteq \m$. If $x\in\Spa(A,A^+)$ is given by the trivial valuation for $\m$, then $\vert a_i\vert_x=0$, contradicting to our assumption.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
    In particular, $a\in A^\times$ if and only if $\vert a\vert_x\not=0$ for all $x\in\Spa(A,A^+)$ under the same assumption as in Proposition \ref{proposition_15_6}.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}
    Let $f,g$ be continuous homomorphisms of topological groups such that $gf$ is open then $g$ is open.
    \label{lemma_16_17_1}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    Let $U$ be a neighborhood of $x$. Then
    \begin{equation*}
    g(U) = g(Ux^{-1})g(x).
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore,  $g$ is open if and only if for any neighborhood $U$ of $1$, $g(U)$ is a neighborhood of $1$.
    Since $gf$ is open, $gf(f^{-1}(U))$ is a neighborhood of $1$. Since $g(U)$ contains $gf(f^{-1}(U))$ is a neighborhood of $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_17}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring and $M$ be a topological $A$-module.
    Let $m,n\in\N$, $\vec{a} = (a_i)_{i=1}^m\in A^m$, $I= \langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle,$ and $\vec{b} = (b_i)_{i=1}^n\in I^n$. If
    \begin{equation*}
        M^n\stackrel{\vec{b}}{\to}M, (m_i)_{i=1}^n\mapsto \sum_{i=1}^n b_im_i
    \end{equation*}
    is open, then $M^m\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to}M$ is also open.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Set
    \begin{equation*}
        b_i =\sum_{j=1}^m c_{ij}a_j.
    \end{equation*}
    Then we have a commutative diagram,
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
M^n \arrow[d, "(c_{ij})_{ij}"'] \arrow[rd, "\vec{b}"] &   \\
M^m \arrow[r, "\vec{a}"']                             & M
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    Now apply Lemma \ref{lemma_16_17_1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    In particular, the openness of the map $\vec{a}:M^m\to M$ depends only on $I = \langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_18}
    Let $A$ be a topological ring such that the set of open ideals form a neighborhood basis of $0$ and $M$ be a topological $A$-module.Then the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item For every neighborhood $U$ of $0$, there are $m\in\N$ and $\vec{a}\in U^m$ such that $M^m\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to}M$ is open.
        \item The above statement holds for open ideals.
    \end{enumerate}
    If $A$ has (oifin), this is equivalent to
