% REVIEW [LaTeX]: Separate enumerate[c.] causes the counter warning. Use enumerate[a.] with setcounter{enumi}{2}, or keep a single enumerate. [original line 2622]
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]\setcounter{enumi}{2}
        \item If $m\in\N$ and $\vec{a}\in A^m$ generates an open ideal, then $M^m\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to}M$ is open.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [proof]: This only proves a<=>b; the equivalence with c under (oifin) still needs both directions, using a finitely generated open ideal and Lemma 16.17. [original line 2627]
    $\Rightarrow$ is trivial. For the converse, for an arbitrary open neighborhood of $0$, take $I\subseteq U$, and apply Lemma \ref{lemma_16_17_1}.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    An $A$-module $M$ is called adic if the equivalent conditions a and b hold in Proposition \ref{fact_16_18}. An $A$-algebra is called adic if and only if it is adic as an $A$-module.
    \par $A$ is called $\id$-adic if itself together with $\id_A$ is an $A$-adic algebra.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_19}
    Every $\id$-adic ring satisfies (oifin) and (oisq).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    If $I$ is an open ideal. Then there are $(a_j)^m_{j=1}\in I^m$ such that $A^m\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to}A$ is open. Then $\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_A = \vec{a}(A^m)$, hence (oifin).
    Moreover, the image of $I^m$ under $\vec{a}$ is also open and contained in the ideal square $I^2$, which is therefore open.
\end{proof}

% REVIEW [proof]: For (E), choose a unit in the requested neighborhood, so multiplication by it is a homeomorphism. For analytic A, use topologically nilpotent generators and a split/open row map; "no proper open ideal" alone does not prove every module adic. [original line 2643]
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_20}
    If $A$ is analytic or satisfies (E), then every $A$-module (hence every $A$-algebra) is adic. In particular, $A$ is $\id$-adic.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
     If $A$ is analytic, there are $(a_i)_{i=1}^m\in (A^\dcirc)^m$ such that $\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle=A$. Then also $\langle a_1^j,\cdots,a_m^j\rangle =A$ for all $j\in\N$. For any open ideal $I$, $I$ contains all $a_1^j,\cdots,a_m^j$ for big enough $j$ hence $I = A$. As $A$ admits no proper open ideal, Proposition \ref{fact_16_18} b can be checked only for $A$ and clearly identity map is open thus $n=1,a_1=1$ works. The latter is trivial as every topological ring $A$ with (E) is analytic.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_21}
    Let $A\stackrel{f}{\to} B$ be a morphism of topological rings and $M$ be a topological $B$-module which is adic as an $A$-module .Then $M$ is adic as a $B$-module. In particular, if $B$ is an adic $A$-algebra, then $B$ is $\id$-adic.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $I\subseteq B$ be an open ideal. By the continuity of $f$ and adicity of $A$, there are $\vec{a}\in(f^{-1}(I))^n$ such that $M^n\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to} M$ is open.
Then such open map coincides to $M^n\stackrel{f(\vec{a})}{\to} M$ thus this is also open and $f(a_1),\cdots,f(a_n)\in I$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_22}
    Let $B\subseteq A$ be an open subring, $M$ be a topological $A$-module, $N$ an open sub-$B$-module of $M$. Then $M$ is adic as an $A$-module if and only if $N$ is adic over $B$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    $\Rightarrow$, let $U\subseteq B$ be open neighborhood of $0$, then $U$ is open in $A$, so we find $\vec{a}:M^n\to M$ open. As $N$ is a open submodule of $M$, and $\vec{a}\in B^n$, $\vec{a}:N^n\to N$ is also open.
    \par For the converse, for any open neighborhood $U$ of $A$, apply the assumption to $B\cap U$, and use that $N\subseteq M$ is open, we get the statement.
\end{proof}
\subsection{Nat-rings, Nat-modules and Huber Ring}

\begin{definition}
    A commutative (additively written) topological group is called nat (for non-Archimedean topological) if it has a neighborhood base of $0$ where each element is an open subgroup.
    A topological ring $A$ is nat if $(A,+)$ is so. A topological $A$-module $M$ is nat if its underlying group is so.
    \par A morphism of nat rings or nat $A$-modules is a morphism whose source and target are nat.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    $\Q_p$ with topology given by $\ab{\cdot}_p$ or any other field with ultrametric absolute value and any ring with its discrete topology are nat.
\end{example}
\begin{remark}
    For any topological ring $A$, $A^{\mathrm{nat}}$ is a topological ring $A$ equipped with the topology where the open subgroups of $(A,+)$ are a neighborhood base of $0$. Then $A^{\nat}$ is a nat-ring. Every morphism $A\to B$ of topological ring with $B$ being nat factors over $A^{\nat}$, and $\Cont(A^\nat)\to \Cont(A)$ is bijective.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_1}
    Let $R$ be a topological ring, $M$ be a $R$-module which is nat, $X\subseteq M$ a subset of $M$, and $X'\subseteq M$ be the subgroup generated by $X$. Then $X$ is bounded if and only if $X'$ is.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    As $X\subseteq X'$, $\Leftarrow$ is clear.
    For $\Rightarrow$, take a neighborhood $U\subseteq M$ of $0$. Without loss of generality, $U$ is a subgroup of $(M,+)$. If $X$ is bounded, there is a neighborhood $V$ of $0$ in $R$ such that $VX\subseteq U$. Since $U$ is a subgroup, $VX'\subseteq U$. Thus $X'$ is bounded.
\end{proof}

% REVIEW [statement]: The parenthetical sentence about topological nilpotence/powerboundedness is malformed. State the two iff assertions separately. [original line 2692]
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_1_b}
    Let $A$ be a nat ring, $X\subseteq A$ be a subset, and $X'$ be a subgroup generated by $X$. Then $X$ is power-bounded if and only if $X'$ is. (Respectively, it is topologically nilpotent and powerbounded if $X'$ is).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [proof]: To inductively prove (X_prime)^m subset Y_prime you need X_prime Y_prime subset Y_prime. The displayed target Y_doubleprime is too weak for that step. [original line 2698]
    Let $Y = \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty X^n$ and $Y'\subseteq A$ be the subgroup generated by $Y$. Let $Y''$ be the subgroup generated by $\bigcup_{n=0}^\infty {X'}^n$ then $Y'\subseteq Y''$ is clear. $XY'\subseteq Y'$ and $X'Y'
     \subseteq Y''$ are clear.
    Thus ${X'}^m\subseteq Y'$ by induction on $m$. Hence $Y''\subseteq Y'$. $X$ is powerbounded if and only if $Y$ is bounded by definition. By Proposition \ref{fact_16_1},, $Y$ is bounded if and only if $Y'= Y''$ bounded. And $Y''$ is bounded if and only if $X'$ is powerbounded. The latter case is similar.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_2}
    Let $A$ be a nat-ring, $X\subseteq A$ be a subset and $X'\subseteq A$ be the subring generated by $X$. Then the following condition are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $X$ is powerbounded.
        \item $X'$ is bounded.
        \item $X'$ is powerbounded.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Set $X^* = \bigcup_{n=0}^\infty X^n$. Then $X'$ is generated by $X^*$. Therefore a$\Leftrightarrow$ b is clear from Proposition \ref{fact_16_1}. As $X'$ is a subring, $X'^n\subseteq X'$ for any $n\in\Z_{\geq0}$. Therefore b$\Leftrightarrow$c.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_3}
    Let $A$ be a nat ring, $(X_i)_{i=1}^m$ are powerbounded subsets. Then the subring generated by $\bigcup_{i=1}^mX_i$ is powerbounded.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Follows from Proposition \ref{fact_11_5} and Proposition \ref{fact_16_2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_16_5}
    For a nat ring $A$, $A^\circ$ is a subring of $A$ which is integrally closed in $A$, $A^\dcirc$ is an ideal in $A^\circ$ and $\sqrt{A^\dcirc}=A^\dcirc$. If $A$ is (tn1), then $A^\circ$ is clopen in $A$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    The fact that $A^\circ$ is a subring follows from Proposition \ref{fact_16_2}. That $A^\dcirc$ is a subgroup of $(A^\circ,+)$ follows from Proposition \ref{fact_16_1_b} and $A^\circ A^\dcirc\subseteq A^\dcirc$ follows from Proposition \ref{fact_11_5}. That $\sqrt{A^\dcirc}=A^\dcirc$ follows from the definition. Indeed, if $r^n\in A^\dcirc$ with $n>0$, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{j\to\infty}r^{i+jn}=0
    \end{equation*}
    for all $0\leq i <n$. Hence $\lim_{j\to\infty}r^j=0$. If $x\in A$ is integral over $A^\circ$, then there are $n\in\N$ and $(p_j)_{j=0}^{n-1}\in A^\circ$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        x^n = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}p_jx^j.
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [CRITICAL]: Integral-closedness proof is wrong: A^circ may be unbounded, so the subgroup generated by A^circ is not bounded; and the conclusion should be x in A^circ, not A^{oo}. Use the bounded subring generated by the FINITELY MANY coefficients p_i and the recurrence for powers of x. [original line 2737]
    Since finite set is bounded, a subgroup $S$ generated by $A^\circ\cup\{p_0,\cdots,p_{n-1}\}$ is bounded by Proposition \ref{fact_16_1}. Moreover, $x^n\in S$ thus $x$ is powerbounded hence $x\in A^\dcirc$. If (tn1) holds then $A^\dcirc$ is a neighborhood of $0$ hence so is $A^\circ$. Thus $A^\circ$ is open, and in particular it is clopen as it is a subgroup.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    \label{remark_16_2}
% REVIEW [sign/meaning]: The C_p example shows A^{oo} as a SET is generally NOT topologically nilpotent (indeed (A^{oo})^2=A^{oo}); "will often happen to be" should be "may fail to be". [original line 2742]
    Note that $A^\circ$ can be unbounded (e.g. $A=\Q_p[T]/T^2\Q_p[T]$, $A^\circ = \Z_p+T\cdot\Q_p$) and $A^\dcirc$ will often happen to be topologically nilpotent. For example, $A=\C_p$, then
    \begin{equation*}
        A^\dcirc = \{x\in\C_p\sep \vert x\vert<1\}
    \end{equation*}
    such that $(A^\dcirc)^2 = A^\dcirc$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
% REVIEW [hypothesis/proof]: "sequentially compact" appears wrong/redundant. Completeness plus a^n->0 is what makes the geometric series converge. The continuity of inversion also needs an actual argument. [original line 2750]
    \label{proposition_16_1}
    Let $A$ be a complete nat ring with (tn1) (hence $A^\dcirc$ is open subgroup). If $A$ is sequentially compact, then the following statements hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A^\times$ is an open subset of $A$.
        \item $A^\times\stackrel{a\mapsto a^{-1}}{\to} A$ is continuous
        \item $A$ has no dense proper ideals (hence no dense maximal ideals).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    For a, set
    \begin{equation*}
        s_n = \sum_{k=0}^n (-a)^k.
    \end{equation*}
    Using the sequentially compactness of $A$, it has a convergent subsequence $s_{n_k}$. Let the limit be $s$, then,
    \begin{equation*}
        (1+a)s_{n_k} = 1+(-1)^{n_k}a^{n_k+1}\stackrel{k\to\infty}{\to}1 = (1+a)s.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore, $A^\times$ contains $A^\dcirc+1$ which is an open neighborhood of $1$. Using the continuity of multiplication, for each $a\in A^\times$, $A^\dcirc +a$ is open. This also shows b as any preimage of an open subset is a union of $A^\dcirc+a$.
    \par For c, let $I\subseteq A$ be an dense ideal. Then $(A^{\dcirc}+1)\cap I\not=\emptyset$. Thus $I$ contains a unit hence it is the whole.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_17_6}
    Let $A$ be a nat ring, $B$ be a nat $A$-algebra which is adic over $A$, and $X\subseteq A$ bounded. Then the image of $X$ in $B$ is bounded.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Let $U$ be a neighborhood of $0$ in $B$, we need to find a neighborhood $V$ of $0$ in $B$ such that $Vf(X)\subseteq U$, where $A\stackrel{f}{\to}B$ is the homomorphism making $B$ into an $A$-algebra. Take a neighborhood $U'$ of $0$ in $B$ such that $U'U'\subseteq U$. Without loss of generality, $U$ and $U'$ can be assumed to be subgroups of $(B,+)$.
% REVIEW [proof]: The omega_i supplied by adicity must be chosen in the preimage neighborhood Omega. Rewrite the final multiplication estimate for a general sum in V. [original line 2779]
    Let $W=f^{-1}(U')\subseteq A$, then take $\Omega\subseteq A$ such that $\Omega X\subseteq W$. By the adicity, there are $(\omega_i)_{i=1}^n\defeq\vec{\omega}$ such that $B^n\stackrel{\vec{\omega}}{\to}B$ is an open map.
    Let $V = \vec{\omega}((U')^n)$ then
    \begin{equation*}
        Vf(X)\subseteq Vf(W)\subseteq V U'
    \end{equation*} For $r = f(\omega_i)u$ with $u\in U'$, then
    $rf(X) \subseteq f(\Omega X)U'\subseteq f(W)U'\subseteq U'U'\subseteq U$, and write? $U$ as a subgroup and the general elements of $V$ is a sum of such $r$, we indeed have $Vf(X)\subseteq U$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    \label{remark_17_2}
    In general, $A^\circ$ may well be unbounded in $A$ for example,
    \begin{equation*}
        A=\Q_p[T]/T^2,A^\circ = \Z_p+\Q_pT.
    \end{equation*}
    But every finite subset of it is contained in some bounded subring.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_17_2}
    Let $A$ be a ring and $I\subseteq A$ be an ideal. The $I$-adic topology on $A$ is the topology for which $(A,+)$ is a topological group and $(I^n)_{n=0}^\infty$ is a neighborhood base of $0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_17_2}
    For a nat ring $A$, the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A$ is $\id$-adic satisfies (tn1) and has an open bounded subring $B$.
        \item $A$ is $\id$-adic, satisfies (tn0) and has an open bounded subring $B$.
        \item $A$ has an open bounded subring $B$ for which there is a finitely generated ideal $I\subseteq B$ such that the topology on $B$ is the $I$-adic one.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    a$\Rightarrow$b is trivial as (tn1) implies (tn0). For the converse,  $A^\dcirc$ generates an open ideal $I$ by (tn0) and using the adicity of $A$, there are $(a_i)_{i=1}^m\in {A^\dcirc}^m$ such that $A^m\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to} A$ is an open map.
    Note that $B$ is bounded and $B^n\subseteq B$ for any $n\in\N$ thus it is powerbounded. The image of $B^m$ under this map is the open and topologically nilpotent since all $a_i$ are topologically nilpotent.
    \par b$\Rightarrow$ c, take $\vec{a}=(a_i)_{i=1,\cdots,m}$ as above, then for each $n\in\N$, the ideal generated by the $a_i^n$ is also open since $A$ has (oisq) by Proposition \ref{fact_16_19}. Without loss of generality, all $a_i\in B$. It is justified as for large enough $n\in\N$, $a_i^n\in B$ by the openness of $B$ thus replace $a_i$ by $a_i^n$. We claim $I=\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_B$ can be taken as such an open ideal. Indeed, $B^m\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to} B$ is open and $I^{\cdot(j+1)}$ is the image of $(I^{\cdot j})^{\oplus m}$.
    Here we used $\cdot$ and $\oplus$ to distinguish ideal powers and Cartesian powers. Thus this is open using (oisq).
    To show that powers of $I$ forms a neighborhood base of $0$, take $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$ which we can assume to be a subgroup by nat. The boundedness of $B$ implies there is a neighborhood $U'$ of $0$ such that $U'B\subseteq U$.
    As the $\{a_1,\cdots,a_m\}$ is topologically nilpotent, there is $N$ such that for any
    \begin{equation*}
    \forall\alpha\in\N^m,\ab{\alpha}\geq N\Rightarrow \vec{a}^\alpha = \prod_{i=1}^m a_i\alpha_i\in U'.
    \end{equation*}
    Then $I^{\cdot N}\subseteq U$, showing $\{I^n\}_{n\in\Z_{\geq0}}$ is indeed a neighborhood base of $0$.
% REVIEW [reason]: I subset A^{oo} follows because each x in I has x^n in I^n ->0, not because I^n subset B. [original line 2822]
    \par To show c$\Rightarrow$a, note that $I^n\subseteq B$ for any $n\in\N$ thus $I\subseteq A^\dcirc$, in particular $A$ satisfies (tn1). It remains to show $\id$-adicity.
    If $J$ is an open ideal in $A$ there is $N$ such that $I^{\cdot N}\subseteq J\cap B\subseteq J$ using $I$-adicity of $B$. Denote $I^{\cdot N}=\langle b_1,\cdots,b_l\rangle_B$ then $A^l\stackrel{\vec{b}}{\to}A$ is open.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_17_3}
    A nat-ring with satisfying the equivalent conditions in Proposition \ref{proposition_17_2} is called a Huber ring.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_17_3_b}
   Let $A$ be a Huber ring. A ring of definition of $A$ is an open bounded subring. A pair of definition of $A$ is a pair $(B,I)$ where $B$ is an open bounded subring and $I\subseteq B$ a finitely generated ideal such that $B$ is $I$-adic.
\end{definition}

\begin{corollary}
    Every ring of definition of a Huber ring can be extended to a pair of definition.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    b$\Rightarrow$c in Proposition \ref{proposition_17_2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_17_7}
    Every Huber ring satisfies (oisq), (oifin), and (tn1).
    Moreover $A^\circ$ is clopen and integrally closed subring of $A$.
    If $A$ is (sequentially) compact and complete
    then $A^\times$ is an open subset of $A$ and $A$ has no proper dense ideal. In particular, every maximal ideal is then closed.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The first part follows from Proposition \ref{fact_16_19}. The second part is by definition or equivalently by Proposition \ref{proposition_17_2}.b.
% REVIEW [CRITICAL]: "A is compact as it is I-adic" is false. I-adic gives a countable neighborhood basis (under finite generation), not compactness. Use completeness/first-countability only where appropriate. [original line 2852]
    The integrally closedness and the clopenness follows from (tn1) and Proposition \ref{fact_16_5}. The last part follows from Proposition \ref{proposition_16_1}. Note that $A$ is compact as it is $I$-adic and thus every point admits a countable open neighborhood basis.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_17_8}
    Let $B$ be an open subring of a nat ring $A$. Then $B$ is Huber if and only if $A$ is. In this case, $B$ has a pair of definition which is a pair of definition for $A$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_17_9}
    Let $(B,I)$ be a pair of definition for a Huber ring $A$. Then a neighborhood $X$ of $A$ is bounded if and only if there is $l\in\N$ such that $I^{\cdot l}X\subseteq B$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
    Let $I\subseteq A$ be an ideal of a topological ring. The formal/$I$-adic completion is the limit
    \begin{equation}
        \widehat{A} \defeq \varprojlim A/I^n.
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_17_10}
    Every Huber ring has a countable neighborhood base of $0$, the completion of a Huber ring is a Huber ring (If $(B,I)$ is a pair of definition, then $\widehat{B}$ is an open subring of $\widehat{A}$ and the topology of $\widehat{B}$ is $\widehat{I}$-adic with $\widehat{I}=\langle f_1,\cdots,f_n\rangle_{\widehat{B}}$ where $I= \langle f_1,\cdots,f_m\rangle_B$).
\end{proposition}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_17_4}
    A Huber pair
    is a pair $A=(A^{\triangleleft},A^+)$ such that $A^{\triangleleft}$ is a Huber ring and $A^+\subseteq (A^{\triangleleft})^\circ$ is an open and integrally closed subring of $A^{\triangleleft}$.
    \par A Huber pair is complete if $A^{\triangleleft}$ is (sequential) complete.
    \par A morphism $A\to B$ of Huber pairs is a continuous ring homomorphism $A^\triangleleft\to B^\triangleleft$ mapping $A^+$ to $B^+$. It is adic if $A^\triangleleft\to B^\triangleleft$ is adic
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    Let $A = (A^\triangleleft, A^+)$ be a Huber pair. Then we have $(A^\triangleleft)^\dcirc\subseteq A^+$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [symbol]: B is undefined; it should be A+. The argument is simply x^n in A+ and T^n-x^n monic, so x is integral over A+. [original line 2889]
    Since $A^+$ is open for each $x\in A^\dcirc$, there is $n\in \N$ such that $x^n\in B$. Using the integrally closedness of $A^+$, all roots of $T^n-x^n$ is in $A^+$. Thus $x\in A^+$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    Let $B\subseteq A$ be an open subgroup of a topological group $A$. Then $A$ is sequentially complete if and only if $B$ is. In our case, a Huber ring $(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$, $A^\triangleleft$ is complete if and only if $A^+$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Open subgroups are closed and using that $B$ is an open neighborhood of $0$ in $A$, the statement is proven.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{theorem_17_2}
    Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ be a Huber pair and $X=\Spa (A,A^+)$.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A^+=\{a\in A^\triangleleft\sep \ab{a}_x\leq 1\text{ for all $x\in X$}\}$.
        \item $X$ is a spectral space.
    \end{enumerate}
    If $A$ is complete, we also have,
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \setcounter{enumi}{2}
        \item The ideal $\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}$ is open if and only if $\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{a_i}_x>0$ for all $x\in X_a$
        This is the case if and only if $A^{\triangleleft m}\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to}A$ is an open map.
        \item $A^{\triangleleft}=\langle a_1,\cdots,a_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}$ if and only if $\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{a_i}_x>0$ for all $x\in X$.
    \end{enumerate}
    A morphism of Huber pairs $\varphi:(A^\triangleleft,A^+)\to (B^\triangleleft, B^+)$ defines a continuous map $Y=\Spa (B^\triangleleft,B^+)\to \Spa (A^\triangleleft, A^+)=X$ which is the case of an adic morphisms is a spectral map.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    a follows from Corollary \ref{corollary_12_1}. b is due to Corollary \ref{corollary_14_2}.
    The first part of c follows from Proposition \ref{proposition_15_6}, and the second part is from $\id$-adicity with Proposition \ref{fact_16_18}.c.
    d follows from Corollary \ref{corollary_16_3}.
    The morphism $\mathrm{Spa}(\varphi):Y\to X$ is well-defined as for any $y\in Y$,
    \begin{equation*}
        a\in A^+\Rightarrow \varphi(a)\in B^+, y\circ\varphi(a)\leq 1.
    \end{equation*}
    The continuity is easily observed as the topology is induced from $\Spv$ and proven for $\Spv$.
% REVIEW [circularity]: The proof of Theorem 3.3.1 should not invoke Theorem 3.3.1 itself to identify strong and weak topologies; use the previously proved topology proposition plus Huber properties. [original line 2925]
    Combining Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2} and Proposition \ref{fact_14_9}, the strong and the weak topologies on $X,Y$ coincide.
    Suppose $B^{\triangleleft}/A^{\triangleleft}$ is adic and take $\Omega=\cR(a_0|a_1,\cdots,a_m)\in\Rat_X$ with $\langle a_0,\cdots,a_m\rangle_A$ open.
    By the adicity, $B^{m+1}\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\to}B$ is open hence $\langle \varphi(a_0),\cdots,\varphi(a_m)\rangle_B$ is open hence the preimage
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathrm{Spa}(\varphi)^{-1}(\Omega) = \cR_Y(\varphi(a_0)|\varphi(a_1),\cdots,\varphi(a_m)),
    \end{equation*}
    is rational open by the definition of the weak topology. It follows from Proposition \ref{facr1.2.6} such maps are spectral.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    \label{remark_18_5}
    Note that $D(T) = \cR(T|)$ is quasi-compact in $\Spv\T_{1,\Q_p}$ but not in $\Spa(\T_1,\T_1^\circ)$ as
    \begin{equation*}
        D(T) = \bigcup_{l=0}^\infty \cR(T|p^l).
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore $\Spa(\T_1,\T_1^\circ)\to \Spv(\T_1)$ is not spectral.
\end{remark}
% REVIEW [CRITICAL]: The perturbation proof is unfinished (it ends at "Hence M") and has matrix/index errors. It does not yet prove equality of rational subsets. [original line 2942]
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_18_3}
    Let $A$ be a (sequentially) complete Huber ring.
    Let $m\in \N$ and $(f_i)_{i=0}^m\in A^{m+1}$ be such that
    \begin{equation*}
        I=(f_0,\cdots,f_m)_A
    \end{equation*}
    is open in $A$. Let $X=\Cont(A)$. Put $f_i=0$ when $i>m$. Then there is a neighborhood $U$ of $0$ in $A$ such that for $n\geq m$ and $(g_i)_{i=0}^n\in A^{n+1}$ such that $g_i-f_i\in U$ for $0\leq i\leq n$ with the following additional properties.
    \begin{enumerate}[1.]
        \item $I=( g_0,\cdots,g_n)_A$,
        \item $\cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)=\cR_X(g_0|g_1,\cdots,g_n)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $\vec{f}=(f_i)_{i=0}^m$ and $\vec{g}=(g_i)_{i=0}^m$. By the assumption, $A^{m+1}\stackrel{\vec{f}\cdot}{\to}A$ is open, hence the image $U$ of $(A^\dcirc)^{m+1}$ under $\vec{f}\cdot$ is a neighborhood of $0$. By our assumption about $\vec{g}$, all $g_i-f_i\in U\subseteq I$, hence all $g_i\in I$. We have $\vec{g} = (I_{m+1}+N)\vec{f}$ where
    $I_{m+1}$ is an identity $(m+1)$-square matrix and $N$ is an $(m+1)$-matrix with coefficients from $A^\dcirc$. Then $det(I_{m+1}+N)\in 1+A^\dcirc\subseteq A^\times$ as the completeness of $A$ is assumed. Hence $1_{m+1}+N$ is invertible and,
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle = \langle g_0,\cdots,g_m\rangle.
    \end{equation*}
    For 2)., we distinguish the case $x\not\in X_a$, ($\p=\supp x$ open) and $x\in X_a$, $(\p\in\supp x$ not open).
    In the first case, $A^\dcirc\subseteq\p$ ($a\in A^\dcirc\Rightarrow a^n\in\p$ when $n$ is large $\Rightarrow a\in\p$) hence $U\subseteq \p$ hence $g_i-f_i\in\p$ and $\ab{f_i}_x=\ab{g_i}_x$ and $x$ is in the right hand side of 2).
    \par In the second case, $M= \max_{0\leq i\leq m}\ab{f_i}_x$ is positive (otherwise $I\subseteq \p$ hence $\p$ is open ad $I$ is). For $a\in A^\dcirc$, $\ab{a}_x<1$ as,
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\ab{a}_x^n=\lim_{n\to\infty}\ab{a_i^n}_x = \left\vert \lim_{n\to\infty}a^n\right\vert_x = 0
    \end{equation*}
    by the continuity. Let $\rho = \max_{\substack{0\leq i\leq n\\0\leq j\leq m}}\ab{n_{ij}}_x$ where
    \begin{equation*}
        g_i - f_i = \sum_{j=1}^mn_{ij}f_j.
    \end{equation*}
    Then $\ab{f_i-g_i}_x\leq\rho M$ hence,
    \begin{equation*}
        \max(\ab{f_i}_{x},\rho M) = \max(\ab{g_i}_x,\rho M).
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, $\ab{g_i}_x=M$ when $M=\ab{f_i}_x$. Hence $M$
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    By Proposition \ref{proposition_18_3}.2, the same about $\Spa(A,A^+)\subseteq X$ follows.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_18_5}

    A Tate ring
    is a nat ring $A$ satisfying,
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
        \item $A^\dcirc\cap A^\times\not=\emptyset$,
        \item $A$ contains a bounded open subring.
    \end{enumerate}
    A ring of definition of $A$ is an open bounded subring of $A$. A pair of definition of a Tate ring $A$ is a pair $(B,s)$ where $B$ is a bounded open subring of $A$ and $s\in B^{\dcirc}\cap A^\times$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}$\:$
    \label{fact_18_11}
    Let $A$ be a Tate ring and $B$ be a ring of definition.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item There is $s\in B$ such that $(B,s)$ is a ring of definition in Tate sense.
        \item If $(B,s)$ is a Tate pair of definition for $A$, $(s^jB)_{j\in\Z}$ or $(s^jB)^{\infty}_{j=n}$ for any $n\in\N$ are neighborhood bases of $0$, and moreover $X\subseteq A$ is bounded if and only if $X\subseteq s^jB$ for some $j\in\Z$.
        \item If $(B,s)$ is a Tate pair of definition for $A$ then $(B,sB)$ is a pair of definition in the sense of Huber rings. Hence any Tate ring is Huber.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    For a, by assumption, there is $s\in A^\dcirc\cap A^\times$. By the openness of $B$, there is $j\in\N$ such that $s^j\in B$. Thus $s^j$ can be taken.
    \par For b, the first part is clear from that $s\in B^\dcirc$. For the second statement, we must show that $V X\subseteq U$ for any $U$. But since $\{s^jB\}_{j\in\Z}$ is a topology basis $V = s^jB$ for some $j\in\Z$. And by taking $U=s^kB$, we obtain the statement. c follows immediately from b.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    \label{remark_18_3}
    Even in the Tate case, $A^\circ$ can fail to be bounded. For example,
    \begin{equation*}
        A=\Q_p[T]/(T^2), A^\circ = \Z_p+T\Q_p.
    \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
    \label{remark_18_4}
    If $A$ is a Tate ring then the image of a bounded or powerbounded subset of $A$ under the morphism $A\to B$ of topological rings is bounded or powerbounded.
% REVIEW [typo]: A<X_1,...,X_0> should be A<X_1,...,X_n>. [original line 3020]
    Thus the universal property of $A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_0\rangle$ takes the exact same one for $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Structure Presheaves on $\Spa(A^{\triangleleft},A^+)$}

We start with the special case of a topological localization of $A^\triangleleft$ with respect to $f\in A^\triangleleft$ such that $A^{\triangleleft}\stackrel{f}{\to} A^\triangleleft$ is open, giving $\Ouv_X(\cR_X(f|))$.
\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_18_1}
    If $C\stackrel{r}{\to}C$ is open then $(C/I)\stackrel{r\tmod I}{\to}(C/I)$ is always open.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [proof]: To conclude pi^{-1}(rU) is open from containing rV, take U,V to be open additive subgroups, so pi^{-1}(rU) is a subgroup containing an open subgroup. [original line 3031]
    Let $U$ be a neighborhood of $0$ in $C/I$ then $V = \pi^{-1}(U)$ is also a neighborhood of $0$ in $C$ where $C\stackrel{\pi}{\to}C/I$. To show that $r\tmod\:I$ is open, we need to show that $rU$ is open which is equivalent to $\pi^{-1}(rU)$ being open by definition.
    Since $\pi$ is $C$-equivariant, $\pi^{-1}(rU)\supseteq rV$ and $rV$ is also a neighborhood of $0$ in $C$ thus the statement follows.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_18_1}
    Let $A$ be a complete nat ring with a countable neighborhood base of $0$ (e.g. a complete Huber ring).
    Let $f\in A$ such that $A\stackrel{f}{\to}A$ is an open map and $I=\bigcup_{j=0}^\infty \Ker(A\stackrel{f^j}{\to}A)$ and $\overline{I}$ the closure of $I$ in $A$.
    Then we have the following statements,
    \begin{enumerate}
    \item  $B=A/\overline{I}$ is a (sequentially) complete nat ring.
    \item $B\stackrel{f\tmod \overline{I}}{\to}B$ open.
    \item $A\to B$ has the universal property for morphisms $A\stackrel{\tau}{\to}T$ such that $T\stackrel{\tau(f)}{\to}T$ is injective and $T$ is a complete nat ring. That is such $\tau$ must factor through $\pi:A\to B$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Take a countable neighborhood basis $A=U_0\supseteq U_1\supseteq \cdots$ of $A$. Let $(b_n)_{n\in\N}$ be a Cauchy sequence in $B$, then we can consider a lift $(a_n)_{n\in \N}$ in $A$. By the construction, for any $k\in\N$, there is $N_k$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
    m,n\geq N_k\Rightarrow a_m-a_n\in U_k + \overline{I}.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore we modify $(a_n)_{n\in\N}$ inductively so that
    \begin{equation*}
    m,n\geq N_k\Rightarrow a_m-a_n\in U_k.
    \end{equation*}
    Now $(a_n)_{n\in\N}$ is Cauchy and admits a limit $a$ in $A$. Since $\overline{I}$ is closed, $B$ is Hausdorff thus $\pi(a)$ is indeed a limit of $(b_n)_{n\in\N}$. Therefore $B$ is complete.
    \par The second assertion follows from Lemma \ref{lemma_18_1}.
% REVIEW [CRITICAL]: An element of closure(I) need not be killed by a power of f. First show tau(I)=0; then use continuity and closedness of ker(tau) to deduce tau(closure(I))=0. [original line 3056]
    \par For each $x\in\overline{I}$, there is $n\in\N$ such that $f^nx = 0$. Thus
    \begin{equation*}
    0 = \tau(0) = \tau(f^nx) = \tau(f)^n\tau(x).
    \end{equation*}
    But $\tau(f)$ is injective thus $\tau(x)=0$. More generally, $\tau(\overline{I})=0$.
% REVIEW [indices]: Start d_n at n=1 (or set d_0=0); U_{n-1} is undefined at n=0. Also f(a-j)=0 already implies a-j in I; "a_j in I" is a typo. [original line 3061]
    \par It remains to show that $\pi$ itself satisfies the condition in 3. Let $a\in A$ be such that $fa\in \overline{I}$. We may pick elements $(i_n)_{n\in\N}$ from $I$ so that $fa-i_n\in f\cdot U_n$ with $i_0 = 0$. Since $i_{n-1}-i_n\in fU_{n-1}$, we may pick $d_n$ from $U_{n-1}$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
    i_{n-1}-i_n = fd_n.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore, $f d_n\in I$ and by construction $d_n\in I$. Now set,
    \begin{equation*}
    j = \sum_{n=0}^\infty d_n.
    \end{equation*}
    Then $j\in \overline{I}$. Furthermore $f(a-j) = 0\in I\subseteq\overline{I}$. Again by construction $a_j\in I$, therefore $a-j\in\overline{I}$. In particular, $a\in\overline{I}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_18_1_a}
    Inheriting the notions from Proposition \ref{proposition_18_1}, if $A$ is Huber (Tate, Analytic), then so is $B$ (as these are identified by $A/I$).
\end{corollary}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_18_1}
    Let $C$ be a complete nat ring with a countable neighborhood base of $0$, $J_0\subseteq C$ be a closed ideal, and $f\in C$ such that $C\stackrel{f}{\to}C$ is an open map. Let $J = \bigcup_{j=0}^\infty (J_0:f^j)$ and $\overline{J}$ be the closure of $J$ in $C$. The following statements hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
    \item $B=C/\overline{J}$ is a complete nat ring and $B\stackrel{f\tmod\overline{J}}{\to}B$ is an injective open map.
    \item $C\to B$ has the universal property for morphisms $C\stackrel{\tau}{\to}T$ to complete topological rings such that $\tau(J_0)=0$ and $T\stackrel{\tau(f)}{\to}T$ is injective. (i.e. such $\tau$ factors through $C\to B$)
    \item $C$ is Huber, Tate, or analytic then so is $B$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    We would like to apply Proposition \ref{proposition_18_1} to $A = C/J_0$. As $J_0$ and $C\stackrel{\cdot f}{\to}C$ is open, we can apply Lemma \ref{lemma_18_1} and Proposition \ref{proposition_18_1}. That is $A$ is a complete nat ring and $\cdot \overline{f} = \cdot f\tmod{J_0}$ is open. Set
    \begin{equation*}
        I\defeq\bigcup_{j=0}^\infty(0:(f\tmod J_0)^j)_A = \bigcup_{j=0}^\infty J_j/J_0 = J/J_0.
    \end{equation*}
    Using this we will show that $B\cong A/\overline{I}$. To do so we need to show that $\pi:C\to A$, $\pi(\overline{J}) = \overline{I}$. The above equation shows $\pi(J) = I$ and also clearly $\pi(\overline{J})\subseteq\overline{I}$. As $\pi$ is a quotient map and $\pi^{-1}(\overline{J}/J_0) = \overline{J}$, $\pi(\overline{J})$ is closed hence $\pi(\overline{J})=\overline{I}$.
    \par We now apply Proposition \ref{proposition_18_1} to $A/\overline{I}$. Observe that $B\cong A/\overline{I}$. First $\tau$ factors through $A$, using the universality from Proposition \ref{proposition_18_1}, it factors through $B$.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_18_2}
% REVIEW [major]: Part (c) of the localization proposition (Huber/completeness equivalence) is not proved at all. [original line 3095]
    Let $A$ be a nat ring and $f\in A$ such that $A\stackrel{f}{\to}A$ is an injective open map. Consider $A$ as a subring of the localization $A_f = A[T]/(1-T\cdot f)_{A[T]} = A[f^{-1}]$. Equip $(A_f,+)$ with the topology for which $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A_f$ if and only if $U\cap A$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$. We have the following statements.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A_f$ is a nat ring and has the universal property for morphisms $A\stackrel{\tau}{\to}T$ of topological rings such that $\tau(f)\in T^\times$.
        \item $A$ is an open subring of $A_f$.
        \item $A$ is Huber (respectively complete, sequentially complete) if and only if $A_f$ is.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We need to check that $A_f$ is indeed a topological ring. The addition follows easily from the fact that $A$ is open in $A_f$ as $A\cap A = A$ and the additive structure inherits directly from the one of $A$.
More concretely, the continuity of addition can be checked at the origin and it translates to other points.
\par Now let us show the continuity of the multiplication of $A_f\times A_f\to A_f$. Certainly $A\times A\to A\subseteq A_f$ is continuous.
Also observe that $A\stackrel{\cdot f}{\to}A$ is open, thus for an open set $U\subseteq A_f$, $(fU\cap A) = f(U\cap A)$ which is open. Hence $A_f\stackrel{\cdot f}{\to}A_f$ is a bijective open map, therefore a homeomorphism.
% REVIEW [ambient group]: f^{-m}A is an open subgroup of (A_f,+), not of (A,+). [original line 3107]
Now take $m\in\Z,(f^{-m}A,+)$ is an open subgroup of $(A,+)$ and the continuity of
\begin{equation*}
    (f^{-m}A)\times(f^{-m}A)\to A_f
\end{equation*}
follows from the diagram below in which the vertical arrows are homeomorphisms and the bottom horizontal arrow is continuous by our initial observation. As $(A_f,+)$ is the ascending union of its open subgroups $f^{-m}A$, this shows the continuity of $A_f\times A_f\to A_f$.
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
(f^{-m}A)\times(f^{-m}A) \arrow[r, "\cdot"] \arrow[d, "(f^m\cdot)\times(f^m\cdot)"'] & A_f \arrow[d, "f^{2m}"] \\
A\times A \arrow[r]                                                                  & A_f
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
If $A\stackrel{\tau}{\to}T$ is a morphism of topological rings and $\tau(f)\in T^\times$, then we have a ring homomorphism $A_f\stackrel{\tilde{\tau}}{\to}T$ by the universal property of localization. This is continuous as for any $U\subseteq T$ open,  $\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(U)\cap A = \tau^{-1}(U)\cap A$.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_19_2}
    In the situation of Corollary \ref{corollary_18_1}, $B_{f\tmod\overline{J}}$ is a complete nat-ring and $C\to B_{f\tmod{\overline{J}}}$ has the universal property for morphisms $C\stackrel{\tau}{\to}T$ such that $\tau(J_0)=0$ and $\tau(f)\in T^\times$. If $C$ is Huber, (Tate, analytic), then so is $B_{f\tmod{\overline{J}}}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
    Follows from Corollary \ref{corollary_18_1} and Proposition \ref{proposition_18_2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_19_1}
    Let $A$ be a nat-ring. We equip $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ with the topology such that $(A[X_1,\cdots,X_n],+)$ is a topological group and a neighborhood base of $0$ is given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \{U[X_1,\cdots,X_n]\sep \text{$U$ a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$}\},
    \end{equation*}
    where
    \begin{equation*}
        U[X_1,\cdots,X_n] = \left\{f = \sum_{\alpha\in\N^n}f_\alpha X^\alpha\in A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]\sep \forall \alpha\in\N^n, f_\alpha\in U\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    We define $A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle $ as the completion of $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$. If $M$ is an $A$-module, then $M[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ a module over $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$, is the set of formal sum $\sum_{\alpha\in\N^n}m_\alpha X^\alpha$ where $m_\alpha\in M$, all but finitely many $m_\alpha=0$.
    The neighborhood base of $0$ is given by $U[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$, where $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $M$ as above. Similarly, $M\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is defined as the completion of $M[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
\end{definition}
\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_19_1}
    Let $A$ be a nat-ring, $B$ be a nat-$A$-algebra.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is a nat ring in which $\{X_1,\cdots,X_n\}$ are powerbounded.
        \item If $C\subseteq A$ is an open subring, $C[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is an open subring of $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
        \item If $A$ is Huber(Tate, analytic), then so is $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
        \item If $(C,I)$ is a pair of definition for $A$, $(C[X_1,\cdots,X_n],I[X_1,\cdots,X_n])$ is also a pair of definition for $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
        \item If $(s_1,\cdots,s_n)\in (A^{\dcirc})^n$ generates $A$ as an ideal (i.e. $\langle s_1,\cdots,s_n\rangle_A =A$) then they generate $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ in $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
        \item If $(b_i)_{i=1}^n$ are powerbounded in $B$, then the homomorphism,
        \begin{equation*}
            A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]\ni p\mapsto p(b_1,\cdots,b_n)\in B,
        \end{equation*}
        is continuous.
        \item If $B$ is an adic $A$-algebra (e.g. as $A$ is Tate or analytic), then we have a bijection,
        \begin{equation*}
            \Hom_{(A-\mathrm{alg})^{\mathrm{top}}}(A[X_1,\cdots,X_n],B)\ni\varphi\stackrel{\cong}{\mapsto}(\varphi(X_i))_{i=1}^n\in(B^\circ)^n.
        \end{equation*}
        \item Suppose $\langle f_0,\cdots,f_n\rangle_A$ is open. Set $I=\langle f_0X_1-f_1,\cdots,f_0X_n-f_n\rangle_{A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]}$ then $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]/I\stackrel{f_0}{\to}A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]/I$ is an open map.
        \item If $M$ is an adic $A$-module, then so is $M[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$. In particular, $A$ being $\id$-adic implies $A\to A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is adic.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    For \textbf{b}, clearly $(C[X_1,\cdots,X_n],+)$ is a topological group. Being open follows from the definition and $C$ itself is open.
% REVIEW [symbol]: The nat-group being proved is (A[X_1,...,X_n],+), not (A,+). [original line 3166]
    That $(A,+)$ is indeed a nat-group follows from the fact that the $U$ in Definition \ref{definition_19_1} can be taken to be open subgroups of $(A,+)$. Therefore we obtain an open subgroup $(U[X_1,\cdots,X_n],+)$ of $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$.
    \par If $P,Q\in A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ and $U$ is an open subgroup of $(A,+)$, then by the continuity of multiplication in $A$, we have a neighborhood $V$ of $0$, such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall \alpha\in\N^n, P_\alpha\cdot V,Q_\alpha\cdot V, V\cdot V\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    (Note that only finitely many $\alpha\in\N^n$, $P_\alpha,Q_\alpha\not=0$). Then
    \begin{equation*}
        (P+V[X_1,\cdots,X_n])(Q+V[X_1,\cdots,X_n])\subseteq (PQ)+U[X_1,\cdots,X_n].
    \end{equation*}
    Thus, multiplication is continuous.
    \par For \textbf{a}, we need to show that
    \begin{equation*}
        \Theta = \{X^\alpha\sep \alpha\in\N^n\}
    \end{equation*}
    is bounded.
    By Definition \ref{definition_19_1}, it suffices to consider $U'=U[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ in which case, obviously $\Theta U'\subseteq U'$.
    \par \textbf{f}. Consider $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]\stackrel{\varphi}{\rightarrow}B$ such that $\varphi(P) = P(b_1,\cdots,b_n)$. This is a ring homomorphism. To show its continuity, consider a neighborhood $\Omega$ of $0$ in $B$. We must find a neighborhood $V'$ of $0$ in $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ such that $\varphi(U')\subseteq\Omega$. Without loss of generality, we may take $\Omega$ to be a subgroup of $(B,+)$.
    By the powerboundedness of $\{b_1,\cdots,b_n\}$, there is a neighborhood $V$ of $0$ in $B$ such that $b^\alpha V\subseteq\Omega$. Let $U$ be the preimage of $V$ in $A$. If $P\in U[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$, then all $ b^\alpha P_\alpha\in b^\alpha V\subseteq\Omega$ hence $\varphi(U[X_1,\cdots,X_n])\subseteq\Omega$ as $\Omega$ is a subgroup of $(B,+)$.
% REVIEW [reference]: The proof of item g cites item d, but continuity/evaluation is item f; re-check the exact dependency. [original line 3184]
    \par\textbf{g}. The surjectivity follows from \textbf{d} and the well-definedness follows from Remark \ref{remark_18_5}.
% REVIEW [hypothesis]: Openness of the ideal (f_0,...,f_n) alone does not imply the row map A^{n+1}->A is open for an arbitrary nat ring unless the needed adicity/oifin hypothesis has already been assumed. [original line 3185]
    \par \textbf{h}. Let $U$ be an open subgroup of $(A,+)$ and $V\subseteq A$ the image of $U^{n+1}$ under $A^{n+1}\stackrel{\vec{f}}{\to}A$. Observe that by Proposition \ref{fact_16_17}, $\vec{f}$ is an open map hence $V$ is open.
    Since $U[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is a basis element of the topology of $A[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$. Need to show that $f_0U[X_1,\cdots,X_n]/I$ is open. Recall that for any $v\in V$, there are $u_0,\cdots,u_n\in U$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
    v = \sum_{i=0}^n f_iu_i.
    \end{equation*}
    Observe that
    \begin{equation*}
    v + I = f_0(u_0+u_1X_1+\cdots+u_nX_n) + I.
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [major]: The proof only handles constant coefficients and even states V[X] subset U[X], which need not hold. Show coefficientwise that V[X] is contained in f_0 U[X]+I. [original line 3194]
    Hence $V[X_0,\cdots,X_n]\subseteq U[X_0,\cdots,X_n]$ and this proves that $f_0\tmod{I}$ is open as $U,V$ were taken topological groups.
   \par \textbf{i}. If $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A$, then by the assumption, there are $(\omega_i)_{i=1}^m\in U$ such that $M^m\stackrel{\vec{\omega}}{\to}M$ is an open map. Taking $U$ to be a subgroup and $V = \vec{\omega}(U^m)$, we see that $M[X_1,\cdots,X_n]^m\stackrel{\vec{\omega}}{\to}M[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is also open.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_20_2}
    Let $X\stackrel{f}{\to}Y$ be a continuous homomorphism between abelian nat-groups which have countable neighborhood bases of $0$. Let $\widehat{X}$ and $\widehat{Y}$ be their completions. If $f$ is an open map then so is $\widehat{X}\stackrel{\widehat{f}}{\to}\widehat{Y}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    There are open subgroups $(X_i)_{i=1}^\infty$ of $X$ forming a neigh
    base of $0$, with
    \begin{equation*}
        X_1\supseteq X_2\supseteq\cdots
    \end{equation*}
    As $f(X_i)$ is open, there is a countable neighborhood base,
    \begin{equation*}
    Y_1\supseteq Y_2\supseteq\cdots
    \end{equation*}
    of $Y$ satisfying $Y_i\subseteq f(X_i)$ where $Y_i$ is an open subgroup of $Y$ for each $i$. Thus we aim to prove the statement by showing
    that $\widehat{Y}_i\subseteq \widehat{f}(\widehat{X}_i)$ for each $i$. An element $y$ of $\widehat{Y}_i$ is represented as an equivalence class of Cauchy sequence $(y_j)_{j=1}^\infty$ with elements $y_i\in Y_i$. Without loss of generality by taking subsequences, we assume $y_{j+1}-y_j\in Y_j$
    for all $j\geq 1$. By assumption, there is $x_1\in X_1$ with $f(x_1) = y_1$ and there are $(\xi_j)_{j=1}^\infty$ where $\xi_j\in X_{j}$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        f(\xi_j) = y_{j+1}-y_j.
    \end{equation*}
    Let
    \begin{equation*}
    x_j = x_1+\sum_{l=1}^{j-1}\xi_l.
    \end{equation*}
    Then
    \begin{equation*}
        x_{j+1}-x_j = \xi_j\in X_j.
    \end{equation*}
    Therefore $(x_j)_{j=1}^\infty$ is Cauchy and $f(x_j)=y_j$. Thus $\widehat{Y}_1\subseteq f(\widehat{X}_1)$. The argument generalizes directly to $\widehat{X}_i$ and proves our statement.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_20_3}
    Let $A$ be a nat-ring with a countable neighborhood base of $0$ and $B$ be a complete nat-$A$-algebra.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is a nat ring in which $\{X_1,\cdots,X_n\}$ is powerbounded.
        \item If $C$ is an open subring of $A$ then $C\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is an open subring of $A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ in which $C[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is dense.
        \item If $A$ is Huber (Tate, analytic) then so is $A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$. For example, in \textbf{b}, if $C$ is Huber and $\langle c_1,\cdots,c_n\rangle_C$-adic, then $C\comppol{n}$ is $\langle c_1,\cdots,c_n\rangle$-adic.
        \item For each $(b_i)_{i=1}^n\in (B^{\circ})^n$, there is a unique continuous $A\comppol{n}\to B$ sending $X_i$ to $b_i$.
        \item If $B$ is adic over $A$ (e.g. $A$ is Tate, analytic or 3.1(E) holds), then we have the following bijection,
        \begin{equation*}
        \Hom_{\mathrm{nat-} A\mathrm{-alg}}(A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle,B)\ni\varphi\stackrel{\simeq}{\mapsto}(\varphi(X_i))_{i=1}^n\in(B^\circ)^n.
        \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [statement]: The open ideal should be <f_0,...,f_n>, and write I subset A<X> for the arbitrary closed ideal. [original line 3239]
        \item If $\langle f_1,\cdots,f_n\rangle_A$ is open and $I=A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is an arbitrary closed ideal containing all $f_0X_i-f_i$. Set $B=A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle/I$. We have $B\stackrel{f_0}{\to}B$ is open.
        \item If $M$ is an adic $A$-module with a countable neighborhood base of $0$ (e.g. $M=A$ if $A$ is $\id$-adic). Then $M\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is an adic $A$-module.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [definition]: This sentence gives the definition of BOUNDED, not powerbounded. To prove X_i powerbounded, show the set of all monomials {X^alpha} is bounded. [original line 3244]
    \textbf{a.} By definition a subset $S\subseteq A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is powerbounded if for any neighborhood $U$ of $0$ there is another neighborhood $V$ of $0$ such that $VS\subseteq U$. The topology base of $A\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ is given by
    \begin{equation*}
    \{U\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle\sep U\subseteq A\text{ an open additive subgroup}\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Note that $X_i U\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle\subseteq U\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$, therefore $\{X^\alpha\sep \alpha\in\N^n\}$ is powerbounded.
    \par\textbf{b.} The openness follows from that for any open subgroup $U\subseteq C$,
    \begin{equation*}
        U\comppol{n}\subseteq C\comppol{n}.
    \end{equation*}
    The density follows from the definition.
    \par\textbf{c.} Lemma \ref{lemma_19_1} and Proposition \ref{fact_17_10}.
    \par\textbf{d.} Since $B$ is complete and morphisms of topological rings are continuous, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
        \Hom_{\mathrm{nat-}A\mathrm{-alg}}(A\comppol{n},B)\stackrel{\text{\makebox[0pt][c]{Continuity}}}{\cong} \Hom_{\mathrm{nat-}A\mathrm{-alg}}(A[X_1,\cdots,X_n],B)\stackrel{\text{\makebox[0pt][c]{Lemma \ref{lemma_19_1}.f}}}{\cong}(B^\circ)^n.
    \end{equation*}
    \par\textbf{e.} By Proposition \ref{fact_16_21}, all such $\varphi$-s are adic and use that $X_1,\cdots,X_n$ are powerbounded from Lemma \ref{lemma_19_1}.a.
    \par\textbf{f.} Use Lemma \ref{lemma_19_1} and Proposition \ref{fact_20_2}.
    \par\textbf{g.} Follows from Proposition \ref{fact_20_2}.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    Let $\sC$ be a category. A functor $F:\sC\to(\mathrm{Sets})$ is said to be represented by $B\in\Ob(\sC)$ if there is a natural isomorphism,
    \begin{equation*}
        F\cong \mathrm{Mor}_\sC(B,\cdot).
    \end{equation*}
    Equivalently, $B$ represents $F$ if for any $C\in\Ob(\sC)$, there is $b\in F(B)$ such that evaluation at $b$ gives a bijection
    \begin{equation*}
% REVIEW [type]: F(phi)(b) lies in F(C), not "in (Sets)". [original line 3270]
        \mathrm{Mor}_\sC(B,C)\ni\varphi\stackrel{\mathrm{ev}_b\circ F}{\mapsto}F(\varphi)(b)\in(\mathrm{Sets}).
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    If $F:\sC\to (\mathrm{Sets})$ is represented by $B\in\Ob(\sC)$, then such $B$ is unique up to isomorphisms.
\end{remark}

\begin{notation}
    $\mathcal{P}$ denotes the category of complete Huber pairs.
\end{notation}
\begin{definition}
% REVIEW [variance]: C |-> {A->C} is covariant in C, so F_Omega should be P -> Sets, not P^{op}->Sets, if morphisms act by postcomposition. [original line 3282]
    Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ and $X=\Spa(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$. For a rational open subset $\Omega = \cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$, we define a functor $\cF_\Omega:\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{op}}\to(\mathrm{Sets})$ to be
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{P}\ni C\stackrel{\cF_\Omega}{\mapsto}\cF_\Omega(C) \defeq\{\varphi\in\Hom_{\mathrm{Huber}}(A,C)\sep \Spa(\varphi)(\Spa(C))\subseteq\Omega\}.
    \end{equation*}
    If $\cF_\Omega$ is representable, we will denote the representing object to be $(\cO_X(\Omega),\cO_X^+(\Omega))$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \label{theorem_20_3_3}
    \label{theorem_20_3_c}
    Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$, $X=\Spa(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$, and $\Omega = \cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$ be rational open. Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        \cO_X(\Omega) = (A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/J)_{f_0}
    \end{equation*}
    for some ideal $J$ and $\cO^+_X(\Omega)$ to be the integral closure of $A^+\comppol{m}+J$ in the localization. More concretely, let
    \begin{equation*}
        I \defeq \overline{\langle f_i-f_0X_i\rangle_{i=1,\cdots,m}}.
    \end{equation*}
    Then we can take
    \begin{equation*}
        J =\overline{\bigcup_{l=0}^\infty(I:f_0^l)}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    Using $J$ in the statement, set
    \begin{equation*}
        B^\triangleleft \defeq \left(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/J\right)[f_0^{-1}],
    \end{equation*}
    and $B^+$ to be the integral closure of $A^+\comppol{m}+J$ in the localization.
    \par $A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}$ is complete by definition and Huber by Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.c. By Corollary \ref{corollary_18_1}, $A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/J$ is again complete and Huber. Using Proposition \ref{proposition_18_2}, $B^\triangleleft$ is complete and Huber. Using Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.b and Proposition \ref{proposition_18_2}, we see that $A^+\comppol{m}+J$ is open. Since $A^+\comppol{m}+J\subseteq B^+$ (i.e. it contains an open subring), $B^+$ is also open.
    Furthermore,
    \begin{equation}
        \label{equation_open_embedding}
% REVIEW [target]: The localization target is (A<X>/J)_{f_0}, not (A<X>)_{f_0}. [original line 3315]
        A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}\to A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/J\to (A^\triangleleft\comppol{m})_{f_0}=B^\triangleleft
    \end{equation}
    is a composition of open maps, hence open. By Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.a, each indeterminate is powerbounded and by definition $A^+\subseteq(A^\triangleleft)^\circ$, hence
    \begin{equation*}
        A^+\comppol{m}\subseteq (A^\triangleleft\comppol{m})^\circ.
    \end{equation*}
    By Proposition \ref{fact_15_16} and using the openness of the morphism in Equation \eqref{equation_open_embedding}, the image of $A^+\comppol{m}$ is open. By Proposition \ref{fact_16_5}, $B^+\subseteq (B^\triangleleft)^\circ$.
    Precomposing the canonical inclusion $A^\triangleleft\to A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}$ to the morphism in Equation \eqref{equation_open_embedding}, we obtain a morphism of complete Huber pairs $b:(A^\triangleleft,A^+)\to(B^\triangleleft,B^+)$.
% REVIEW [map]: b:A->B is not defined on X_i. Let q_i be the image of X_i under A<X>->B. [original line 3323]
    We now show that $b\in\cF_\Omega(B)$, that is $\Spa(b)(\Spa(B))\subseteq\Omega$. Let $q_i \defeq b(X_i)$ for each $X_i\in A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}$. Then by Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2}.a and the construction of $I$,
    \begin{equation*}
% REVIEW [CRITICAL formula]: y(b(f_i))=y(q_i)y(b(f_0)), not y(q_i)y(b(f_i)). The desired inequality is <= y(b(f_0)). [original line 3325]
        \forall y\in \Spa(B), y(b(f_i)) = y(b(f_0X_i)) = y(q_i)y(b(f_i))\leq y(b(f_i)).
    \end{equation*}
    As $f_0$ is a unit in $B^\triangleleft$ by construction, we have $y(b(f_0))\not=0$. Hence $\Spa(b)(\Spa(B))\subseteq\Omega$ showing $b\in\cF_\Omega(B)$.
% REVIEW [index]: q_i exists for i=1,...,m; q_0 would be 1 if introduced. [original line 3328]
    \par It remains to show that evaluation at $b$ gives a desired bijection. Let $C\in\mathcal{P}$ and $\varphi\in\cF_\Omega(C)$. By definition and Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2}, $\varphi(f_0)\in (C^\triangleleft)^\times$. Set $q_i = {\frac {\varphi(f_i)}{\varphi(f_0)}}$, then for any $z\in\Spa(C)$, $z(q_i)\leq 1$. Hence by Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2}.a, $q_i\in C^+$ for each $i=0,\cdots,m$.
    Hence by Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.d, there is a unique morphism of nat-rings $\varphi_1:A\comppol{m}\to C$ sending $X_i$ to $q_i$ and the composition with $A^\triangleleft\to A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}$ gives $\varphi$. By construction and the continuity of $\varphi_1$, we obtain $\varphi_1(I)=0$.
    Recalling that $\varphi(f_0)$ is invertible, we see $\varphi_1$ annihilates $(I:f_0^l)$ for any $l\geq0$. Again by the continuity $\varphi_1(J)=0$. Using Corollary \ref{corollary_19_2}, $\varphi_1$ factors through $B^\triangleleft$. We denote the lifted morphism $\varphi_2:B^\triangleleft\to C^\triangleleft$. It remains to show that $\varphi_2$ is a morphism of Huber pairs. At the moment, we have the following diagram,
    \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
                                                              & A^\triangleleft \arrow[rdd, "b"] \arrow[ldd] \arrow[d, "\varphi"] &                                         \\
                                                              & C^\triangleleft                                                   &                                         \\
A^\triangleleft\comppol{m} \arrow[rr] \arrow[ru, "\varphi_1"] &                                                                   & B^\triangleleft \arrow[lu, "\varphi_2"]
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    Using that $\varphi(A^+)\subseteq C^+$ and $q_i\in C^+$ for each $i=0,\cdots,m$, we obtain
    \begin{equation*}
% REVIEW [variables]: Use A^+[X_1,...,X_m], not X_0,...,X_m. [original line 3340]
        \varphi_1(A^+[X_0,\cdots,X_m])\subseteq C^+.
    \end{equation*}
    Using the density of $A^+[X_0,\cdots,X_m]$ in $A^+\comppol{m}$ by Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.b, the continuity of $\varphi_1$, and $C^+$ is an open subgroup hence also closed, we get
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi_1(A^+\comppol{m})\subseteq C^+.
    \end{equation*}
    Using the commutative diagram,
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi_2(A^+\comppol{m})\subseteq C^+.
    \end{equation*}
    Finally $\varphi_2(B^+)$ is integral over $\varphi_2(A^+\comppol{m})$ by construction and $C^+$ is integrally closed by definition, we obtain,
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi_2(B^+)\subseteq C^+.
    \end{equation*}
    We have shown the surjectivity. Furthermore, the construction of morphisms has been all unique thus the injectivity also follows.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_20_3_b}
    In the situation of Theorem \ref{theorem_20_3_c}, if $\langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle_A = A$ holds (e.g. $A$ is Tate or analytic), then we can take,
    \begin{equation*}
        B^\triangleleft \defeq A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I.
    \end{equation*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    By the assumption, we have,
    \begin{align*}
        A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I & = (f_0,\cdots,f_m)(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I),\\
% REVIEW [punctuation/math]: (f_0,f_0X_1...f_0X_m) must be a list (f_0,f_0X_1,...,f_0X_m), not a product. [original line 3367]
        & = (f_0,f_0X_1\cdots,f_0X_m)(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I),\\
        & = f_0(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I).
    \end{align*}
    Hence $f_0$ is already a unit in $(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I)$. In particular,
    \begin{equation*}
        (I:f_0^l) = I \tand (A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I)_{f_0} = A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/I.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_20_3}
% REVIEW [TeX]: Missing closing $ after X=Spa(A^triangleleft,A^+). [original line 3377]
    Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$, $X=\Spa(A^\triangleleft,A^+),\Omega = \cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$ a rational open subset.
    We denote $\mathcal{P}$ to be the category of complete Huber pairs $(B^\triangleleft,B^+)$.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $\cF_\Omega$ is representable.
        \item The image of $f_0$ in $\cO_X(\Omega)$ is a unit and the image of the resulting morphism $A_{f_0}^\triangleleft\to\cO_X(\Omega)$ is a dense subring of $\cO_X(\Omega)$.
        \item The morphism $A^\triangleleft\to\cO_X(\Omega)$ is adic. Furthermore, if $A^\triangleleft$ is Tate or analytic then so is $\cO_X(\Omega)$.
        \item The subring $\cO_X^+(\Omega)$ is integral over the closure in $\cO_X(\Omega)$ of the subring generated by $b(A^+)$ and the ${\frac {b(f_i)} {b(f_0)}}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    \textbf{a} follows immediately from Theorem \ref{theorem_20_3_c}. For the sake of simplicity, we denote $(B^\triangleleft,B^+)=(\cO_X(\Omega),\cO^+_X(\Omega))$.
% REVIEW [enumeration]: The corollary has items a-d, so "d and e" is impossible; item d is the one already established. [original line 3388]
    \par\textbf{d} and \textbf{e} are already shown in the proof of Theorem \ref{theorem_20_3_c}. For \textbf{b.} we have seen that by Proposition \ref{proposition_18_2}.b and Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.b that
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{A^\triangleleft[X_0,\cdots,X_m]}\subseteq A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}\subseteq A_{f_0}\comppol{m}.
    \end{equation*}
    By construction,
    \begin{equation*}
% REVIEW [symbol]: Use f_0, not f, and write the localization/quotient subsets precisely. [original line 3394]
        B^\triangleleft = \bigcup_{j\geq0}f^{-j}(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/J).
    \end{equation*}
    However, $f^{-j}(A[X_0,\cdots,X_m]+J)$ is dense in $f^{-j}(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}/J)$ and $X_i = {\frac {f_i} {f_0}}$ in $B^\triangleleft$, the image of $A_{f_0}^\triangleleft \to B^\triangleleft$ does contain every elements of $f^{-j}(A[X_0,\cdots,X_m]+J)$, hence it is dense.
    \par\textbf{c.} Use Proposition \ref{proposition_20_3}.c, Corollary \ref{corollary_18_1}.c, and Corollary \ref{corollary_19_2} for the latter statement. For the first part, note that $A$ is Huber hence $\id$-adic by definition. Using \ref{proposition_20_3}.g, $A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}$ is adic. Hence for any open ideal $I\subseteq A$, there are finitely many $\{a_1,\cdots,a_r\}\subseteq I$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        (A^\triangleleft\comppol{m})^r\ni(\alpha_i)_{i=1,\cdots,r}\stackrel{\vec{a}}{\mapsto} \sum_{i=1}^r a_i\alpha_i\in A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}
    \end{equation*}
    is open. Consider the following diagram,
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
(A^\triangleleft\comppol{m})^r \arrow[d] \arrow[r, "\vec{a}"] & A^\triangleleft\comppol{m} \arrow[d] \\
(B)^r \arrow[r, "\vec{a}"']                                   & B
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    Since $\vec{a}$ on the top and the canonical morphism $A^\triangleleft\comppol{m}\to B$ is open, using Lemma \ref{lemma_16_17_1}, we see $\vec{a}$ on the bottom is also open. Hence $B$ is an adic $A$-module.
\end{proof}
\par It remains to prove the last two assertions of Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}. Bijectivity of $Y\stackrel{\beta}{\to}\Omega$, plus identification of topologies of $\Rat$ and $(\Ouv(\cdots),\Ouv^+(\cdots))$. That $\beta(Y)\subseteq\Omega$ follows from $b\in\mathcal{F}_\Omega(B)$ which was checked before. For the bijectivity of $\beta$, we need the following proposition.
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_21_4}
% REVIEW [notation]: The proposition calls the completion A but the proof uses hat A with A dense. Choose one convention (e.g. mathcal A and its completion hat{mathcal A}). [original line 3413]
    Let $A$ be the (completion or) sequential completion of a topological ring $\mathcal{A}$. Then $\Cont(A)\to \Cont(\mathcal{A})$ is bijective.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [CRITICAL variable]: Set gamma=v(alpha), not gamma=v(a) with a undefined; then choose a in the dense subring with a-alpha in K_{v<gamma}. [original line 3416]
    We will first show that for a valuation $v\in\Cont(\hat{A})$ and the restriction $v_0$ of $v$ by precomposing $A\to\hat{A}$, we have $\Gamma_v = \Gamma_{v_0}$. First the inclusion $\Gamma_{v_0}\subseteq\Gamma_v$ is immediate. For any $\alpha\in \hat{A}$ and $\gamma=v(a)\not=0$, $W=K_{v<\gamma}$ is open by assumption. As $A$ is dense in $\hat{A}$, there is $a\in A$ such that
    $a\in \alpha+W$. By definition, $v(\alpha+W) = \{\gamma\}$. Hence $v_0(a) = \gamma$, therefore $\Gamma_{v}\subseteq\Gamma_{v_0}$.
    \par Let $u,v\in\Cont(A)$ such that $u_0\sim v_0$. Hence $\Gamma_{u_0}\cong \Gamma_{v_0}$. By the previous claim, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \Gamma_u =\Gamma_{u_0} \cong \Gamma_{v_0} =\Gamma_v.
    \end{equation*}
    Thus we may assume $u_0=v_0$. Then $u,v$ coincide on $A$ which is dense in $\hat{A}$. By the continuity and the topology of the codomain is Hausdorff, $u=v$.
    \par For the surjectivity, let $v\in\Cont(A)$ and we need to show that this lifts to $\hat{v}\in\Cont(\hat{A})$. Let $(a_n)_{n\in\N}$ be a Cauchy sequence in $A$. If $a_n\stackrel{n\to\infty}{\to}0$, then we put $\hat{v}((a_n)_{n\in\N}) = 0$.
% REVIEW [ordered group]: delta should be an element of Gamma_v (not a real "delta>0"). Independence of representatives and continuity of the extension still need proof. [original line 3424]
    Otherwise, there is $\delta>0$ such that $n\gg 0\Rightarrow a_n\not\in [0,\delta)\subseteq\Gamma\cup\{0\}$. As $(a_n)_{n\in\N}$ is Cauchy, there is $N\in\N$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        i,j\geq N\Rightarrow v(a_j-a_i)<\delta.
    \end{equation*}
    Suppose $N\gg0$ so that $i\geq N\Rightarrow v(a_i)\geq\delta$. By definition,
    \begin{equation*}
        v(a_j) = v(a_i+(a_j-a_i)) = v(a_i).
    \end{equation*}
    Hence $(v(a_n))_{n\in\N}$ stabilizes eventually to some $\gamma$. Hence we put $\hat{v}((a_n)_{n\in\N})= \gamma$. Since we have defined the value for the Cauchy sequence converging to $0$, this gives us the independence of the choice of Cauchy sequence.
    Furthermore, all axioms of valuations are closed conditions, hence by the continuity such $\hat{v}$ is indeed a valuation.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
    \label{theorem_21_1}
% REVIEW [grammar]: State that beta is a homeomorphism identifying Rat_Y with the rational opens of X contained in Omega. [original line 3437]
    In the situation of Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}, the map $Y\defeq\Spa(B)\stackrel{\Spa(b)}{\to}\Omega$ where $b$ is the universal element is a homeomorphism sends following $\Rat_Y$ with $(\Rat_Y)_\Omega \defeq\{\Theta\in\Rat_X\sep \Theta\subseteq\Omega\}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We denote $\beta = \Spa(b)$. By the assumption $\beta(Y)\subseteq \Omega$ hence the question is well-defined.
Let $v\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ be a valuation since $v\in\Omega$, under the homeomorphism in Remark \ref{fact_6_3}, we lift $v_{f_0}\in\Cont(A_{f_0})$.
Then by precomposing the evaluation map,
\begin{equation*}
    A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m]\ni P\stackrel{\ev}{\mapsto}P\left({\frac {f_1} {f_0}},\cdots{\frac {f_m} {f_0}}\right)\in A^\triangleleft_{f_0},
\end{equation*}
we obtain a lift $v_1\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m],A^+[X_1,\cdots,X_m])$.
\begin{align*}
    v_1(P) &= v_{f_0}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\N^m}p_\alpha\prod_{i=1}^m\left({\frac {f_i}{f_0}}\right)^{\alpha_i}\right),\\
% REVIEW [symbol]: v_0 is not defined here; use v_{f_0}. [original line 3449]
    &\leq \max_{\alpha\in\N^m}v_0\left(p_\alpha\prod_{i=1}^m\left({\frac {f_i} {f_0}}\right)^{\alpha_i}\right),\\
    & \leq \max_{\alpha\in\N^m}v(p_\alpha)\prod_{i=1}^m\left({\frac {v(f_i)} {v(f_0)}}\right)^{\alpha_i},\\
    &\leq \max_{\alpha\in\N^m}v(p_\alpha) \tag{b}
\end{align*}
% REVIEW [continuity]: Use K_{v<gamma}, not K_{v<=gamma}, if the image must lie in [0,gamma). Also keep m/n consistent. [original line 3453]
Hence for any $[0,\gamma)\subseteq\Gamma\cup\{0\}$ open, $K_{v\leq \gamma}[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ is open and the image under $v_1$ is contained in $[0,\gamma)$, hence $v_1\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_n],A^+[X_1,\cdots,X_n])$.
\par By Proposition \ref{proposition_21_4}, there is a unique continuous extension,
\begin{equation*}
    \widehat{v_1}:A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle\stackrel{r}{\to}\Gamma\cup\{0\}.
\end{equation*}
By construction $\widehat{v_1}$ annihilates $I$ since $\widehat{v_1}(f_0X_i-f_i) = 0$ and $\widehat{v_1}$ is continuous.
% REVIEW [function/value]: The intended statement is hat v_1(f_0)=v(f_0)!=0, not hat v_1=v(f_0). Explain why this kills every (I:f_0^l) and hence J. [original line 3459]
Furthermore, as $\widehat{v_1} = v(f_0) \not = 0$, hence this also kills $J$. Again using the identification in Remark \ref{fact_6_3}, we obtain $w:B^\triangleleft = (A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle/J)_{f_0}\to\Gamma\cup\{0\}$.
As $v_1\in\Spa(A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m],A^+[X_1,\cdots,X_m])$, using the continuity,
\begin{equation*}
    A^+\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle\subseteq K_{w\leq 1}.
\end{equation*}
By Proposition \ref{fact_6_5}, $K_{w\leq 1}$ is integrally closed. Since $B^+$ is the integral closure of $A^+\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle$, $B^+\subseteq K_{w\leq 1}$. Hence $\beta(w) = v$.
\par Suppose we obtained another valuation $\widehat{v}_1'$ of $A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle$ from $v_1$.
Let $P\in A^\triangleleft[X_1,\cdots,X_m]$ be of degree $d$, then set
\begin{equation*}
    Q(X_0,\cdots,X_m) \defeq X_0^dP\left({\frac {X_1} {X_0}},\cdots,{\frac {X_m} {X_0}}\right).
\end{equation*}
% REVIEW [definition]: q should be Q(f_0,...,f_m), not the tuple (f_0,...,f_m). [original line 3470]
Set $q\defeq (f_0,\cdots,f_m)$, then $f_0^dP - q\in I$ since $Q \equiv q \mod{I}$. Since $\widehat{v}_1'$ kills $I$, hence
\begin{equation*}
    \widehat{v}_1'(f_0)^d\widehat{v}_1'(P) = \widehat{v}_1'(q).
\end{equation*}
However, $\widehat{v}_1'(f_0) = v_{f_0}(f_0)\not=0$ and $\widehat{v}_1'(q) = v_{f_0}(q)$. Hence, $\widehat{v}_1'(P)$ depends solely on $v_{f_0}$ therefore it must coincide with $\widehat{v}_1$ on a dense subset. Using the continuity, we have shown the uniqueness of the extension.
All the other extensions were obtained from homeomorphisms, hence showing the injectivity.
\par It remains to show that $\beta$ is a homeomorphism. To do so, it is enough to show that $\beta$ is an open map. Consider a rational open $\Theta=\cR_Y(g_0|g_1,\cdots,g_k)$ where $\langle g_0,\cdots,g_k\rangle_B$ generates an open ideal.
Using Proposition \ref{proposition_18_3} together with the density of $A_{f_0}^\triangleleft$ in $B^\triangleleft$ by Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}.b, there is an open subset $U\subseteq B^\triangleleft$ such that $g_i-b(r_i)\in U$ for each $i=0,\cdots,k$ and
\begin{equation*}
\langle g_0,\cdots,g_k\rangle = \langle b(r_0),\cdots,b(r_k)\rangle,
\end{equation*}
hence open. Furthermore, as $f_0$ is a unit in $B^\triangleleft$, we may assume that $g_i = b(\alpha_i)$ for some $\alpha_i = f_0^dr_i\in A^\triangleleft$ for each $i=0,\cdots,k$ and fixed $d\geq0$. As $f^d_0U$ is an open subset, $V=b^{-1}(f_0^dU)$ is also open. Since $A^\triangleleft$ is Huber, by Proposition \ref{fact_17_10}, there is an finitely generated open ideal whose generators are topologically nilpotent elements $\{s_1,\cdots,s_l\}$.
We may choose these elements small enough so that,
\begin{equation*}
    \langle s_1,\cdots,s_l\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}\subseteq V.
\end{equation*}
We extend $\alpha_i$ by those in order to obtain an open ideal $\langle \alpha_0,\cdots,\alpha_r\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}$.
\par Let $y\in Y$ and $x=\beta(y)$, then
\begin{equation*}
    y(b(\alpha_i)) = x(\alpha_i).
\end{equation*}
% REVIEW [proof]: The claim that extra generators are "small enough" should explicitly invoke the perturbation proposition to show the rational set is unchanged. [original line 3491]
As we can take the extending elements small enough, we conclude,
\begin{equation*}
    y\in\Theta\iff x\in\cR_X(\alpha_0|\alpha_1,\cdots,\alpha_r).
\end{equation*}
Hence,
\begin{equation*}
    \beta(\Theta) = \Omega\cap \cR_X(\alpha_0|\alpha_1,\cdots,\alpha_r)
\end{equation*}
which is open. Hence we have shown $\beta$ is a continuous open bijection hence a homeomorphism.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
    In the setting of Corollary \ref{corollary_20_3}, let $\Theta\in\Rat_Y,C = (\Ouv_Y(\Theta),\Ouv_Y^+(\Theta))$. Set $\Theta_X\defeq \beta(\Theta)$ and take $c\in\mathcal{F}_\Theta(C)$ the universal element. The composition $A\stackrel{b}{\to}B\stackrel{c}{\to}C$ is an element of $\cF_{\Theta_X}$ which is a universal element induces an isomorphism,
        \begin{equation*}
            (\Ouv_X(\Theta_X),\Ouv_{X}^+(\Theta_X))\to(\Ouv_Y(\Theta),\Ouv_Y^+(\Theta))=C.
        \end{equation*}
        \label{theorem_21_2}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem \ref{theorem_21_1}, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \Theta_X = \beta(\Theta) = \Omega\cap\cR_X(\varphi_0|\varphi_1,\cdots,\varphi_m) = \cR_X(f_0\varphi_0|\text{ all other }f_i\varphi_j)
\end{equation*}
The topological claim then follows. If $\Theta_X = \beta(\Theta)$, one checks that
\begin{align*}
    \mathcal{F}_\Theta(C)\ni(B\stackrel{c}{\to}C)&\rightarrow (A\stackrel{b}{\to}B\stackrel{c}{\to}C)\in\mathcal{F}_{\Theta_X}(C),
\end{align*}
is bijective using the definition and the bijectivity of $\beta$. Thus the composition of structure presheaves also holds.
\end{proof}
\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_22_1}
    \begin{equation*}
        \Spa(A)\cong \Spa(\widehat{A})
    \end{equation*}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Note that $\Spa(A) = \cR_{\Spa(A)}(1|)$. Hence the corresponding ring $B$ is just the completion of $A$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A Version of The Weierstraß Preparation Theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_26_1}
    Let $A$ be a complete Tate ring, we call $P\in A\langle T\rangle$, $(A,T)$-distinguished of degree $d$ if $P_d\in A^\times$ and ${\frac {P_i} {P_d}}$ belongs to $A^\circ$ if $0\leq i\leq d$, if othewise $i>d$, then it belongs to $A^{\dcirc}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_26_1}
    Let $R$ be a (discrete topology) ring, $I\subseteq R$ be a nilpotent ideal, $M$ be a $R$-module, $P = \sum_{i=0}^NP_iT^i\in R[T]$, and $d\in\N$ such tha $P_d\in R^\times$ and $P_j\in I$ for any $j>d$. 
    Then 
    \begin{equation*}
        M^d\ni(m_0,\cdots,m_{d-1})\mapsto \left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}m_iT^i\right)\mod PM[T] \in M[T]/P\cdot M[T].
    \end{equation*}
    is bijective.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    The proof proceeds induction on $k$ such that $I^{k} = 0$. If this holds with $k=0$, then $I=0$< hence $P_i=0$ for $i>d$ and since $P_d$ is a unit in $R$, the assertion is a straitforward polynomial division with remainders.
    \par Let $I^k = 0$ with $k>$ and suppose the assertion is shown for $R'$ and $I'$ with $I^{k-1} = 0$. In particular, it holds for $R' = R/I^{k-1}$, $I' = I/I^{k-1}$ and the image of $P$ in $R'$. Let $M' = IM, M'' = M/M'$. These are $R'$-modules and the claim holds for them. 
    Now consider the diagram,
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
0 \arrow[r] & {M'}^d \arrow[r] \arrow[d]          & M^d \arrow[r] \arrow[d]           & {M''}^d \arrow[r] \arrow[d]           & 0 \\
0 \arrow[r] & {{M'}^d[T]/P\cdot {M'}^d} \arrow[r] & {{M}^d[T]/P\cdot {M}^d} \arrow[r] & {{M''}^d[T]/P\cdot {M''}^d} \arrow[r] & 0
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    and the claim for $M$ follows from the five lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_26_2}
    Let $A$ be complete with respect to the $i$-adic topology, where $I\subseteq A$ is a finitely generated ideal (hence $I\subseteq A^\dcirc$). 
    Let $P = \sum_{k=0}^\infty P_k T^k\in A\langle T\rangle$ and $d\in\N$ be such that $P_d\in A^\times$ and $P_k\in A^\dcirc$ for $k>d$. Then $P\cdot A\langle T\rangle$ is closed in $A\langle T\rangle$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    By assumption, $A\stackrel{\simeq}{\to}\varprojlim A/I^j$ and $A\langle T\rangle\stackrel{\simeq}{\to}\varprojlim(A/I^j)[T]$. By Lemma \ref{lemma_26_1}, 
    \begin{center}
        \begin{tikzcd}
A^d\oplus A\langle T\rangle \arrow[r, "{((a_i)_{i=0,\cdots,d-n},f)\mapsto f\cdot P+\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}a_iT^i}"] \arrow[d, "\simeq"']                                                                       & A\langle T\rangle \arrow[d, "\simeq"] \\
(\varprojlim(A/I^j))^d\oplus\varprojlim(A/I^j)\langle T\rangle \arrow[r, "{\substack{((a_i)_{i=0,\cdots,d-n},f)\mapsto f\cdot P+\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}a_iT^i\\\text{bijective by Lemma \ref{lemma_26_1}}}}"'] & \varprojlim(A/I^j)\langle T\rangle   
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    where the vertial arrows are homeormophisms, and so is the bottom horizontal arrow by Lemma \ref{lemma_26_1} from which the claim follows as in the proof of the proposition.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Weierstraß Division]
    \label{proposition_26_1}
    Let $A$ be a complete Tate ring and $P\in A\langle T\rangle$ be $(A,T)$-distinguished of degree $d$. Then $I = P\cdot A\langle T\rangle$ is closed and $A\langle T\rangle/I$ is a free $A$-module with generators $(1,\cdots,T^{d-1})$. 
    Moreover, 
    \begin{equation*}
        A^d\ni (a_0,\cdots,a_{d-1})\mapsto \sum_{i=0}^{d-1}a_iT^i\mod{I}
    \end{equation*}
    is a homermorphism when $A/I$ has the quotient topology.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proposition}
    Without loss of generality, we assume $P_d=1$. Then $P_i\in A^\circ$ for all $i$, $\lim_{i\to \infty}P_i=0$, and $P_i\in A^\dcirc$ for $i>d$. 
    There is an open bounded subring $A^\#$ and $N\in\N$ such that $P_i\in A^\#$ for $i>N$. There is an open bounded subrig $A^b\supseteq A^\#$ such that $P_i\in A^b$ for $0\leq i<N$. Then all $P_i\in A^b$. If there is $s\in (A^b)^\circ\cap A^\times$, then Lemma \ref{lemma_26_2} can be applied to $A^b$ and the ideal $s\cdot A^b$. 
    \par If 
    \begin{equation*}
        \label{proposition_26_1_eq_1}
    \sum_{i=0}^{d-1}a_iT^i = P\cdot f\tag{$\dagger$}
    \end{equation*} where $f = \sum_{k=0}^\infty f_kT^k$, we would like for all $a_i=0$, where all but finitely many $f_i\in A^b$ as $\lim_{i\to\infty}f_i = 0$. Therefore by multiplying with an appropriate power of $s$ all $f_i\in A^b$ and all $a_i\in A^b$. Then \eqref{proposition_26_1_eq_1} holds in $A^b$, contradicting to Lemma \ref{lemma_26_2}. 
    \par Let $g\in A\langle T\rangle$ which will like to express in $g=\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}a_iT^i+P\cdot f$ with $f\in A\langle T\rangle$. It is possible to replace $g$ by $s^ig$ (afterwards $f\ast = s^{-j}f_i, a_j\ast = s^ja_i$). Since $\lim_{i\to\infty}g_i=0$, all but finitely many $g_i\in A^b$ clearly. 
    Choosign $i$, (in $s^i$) large enough, all $s^igj\in A^b$, adn the same application of Lemma \ref{lemma_26_2} shows our claim. This is 
    \begin{align*}
       A^d\oplus A\langle T\rangle \ni (a_0,\cdots,a_{d-1},f)\mapsto \left(\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}a_iT^i\right)+P\cdot f \in A\langle T\rangle,\\
       s^j(A^b)^d\oplus sjA^b\langle T\rangle \stackrel{\simeq}{\to}s^jA^b\langle T\rangle
    \end{align*}
    is topological and these form neighborhood basis of $0$ on both sides. Sice $A^d$ is closed in the left hand side and mapped onto $P\cdot A\langle T\rangle,P\cdot A\langle T\rangle$ is closed in the right hand side.
\end{proposition}

\begin{corollary}[Weierstrass Preparation Lemma]
    Let $A$ be a complete Tate ring.
    \begin{enumerate}[1. ]
        \item Eery $(A,T)$-distinguished $f\in A\langle T\rangle$ is a multiplicatively equivalent to a unique irreducible $P\in A^\circ[T]$ which is called a Weierstrass poynomial, where the polynomial degree of $P$ equals to the degree of distinguished one of $f$.
        \item If $P\in A^\circ[T]$ is a Weierstrass polynomialand $f\in A[T]$ then $P$ divides $f$ i n$A[T]$ if and only if $P$ divides $f$ in $A^\langle T\rangle$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    \textbf{1.} Let $f$ be $(A,T)$-distinguished of degree $d$. After dividing by $f_d$, without loss of generality that all $f_i\in A^\circ$ and $f_d=1$. 
    Choosing a ring of definition $A^\#$ containing all coefficients of $f$ (as in the proof of Proposition \ref{proposition_26_1}), 
    \begin{equation*}
        T^d = -\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}P_iT^i + q(T)f(T).
    \end{equation*}
    Hence $P(T) = T^d+\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}P_iT^i\in A^\circ[T]$ is a Weierstrass polynomial ofdegree $d$ divisible by $f$ in $A\langle T\rangle$. Hence there is a morphism,
    \begin{equation*}
        A\langle T\rangle/P\cdot A\langle T\rangle\to A\langle T\rangle/f\cdot A\langle T\rangle
    \end{equation*}
    sending $g\mod{P}$ to $g\mod{f}$. Thisis an isomorphism since using Proposition \ref{proposition_26_1}, both modules are free with generatorss $(T^i)_{i=0,\cdots,d-1}$. 
    Then, $f$ is also divisible by $P$, hence $P$ and $f$ are multiplicatively equivalent. If $f$ is multiplicatively equivalent to the Weierstrass polynomial $Q$ of the degree $e$, then $e=d$ as $A\langle T\rangle/Q=A\langle T\rangle /f$ as $A$-modules and these have ranks $d$ and $e$, respectively. 
    In \textbf{1}, without loss of generality, $A^\#$ contains all coefficient of $Q$. Then $P=Q$ by the unique remark made after \textbf{1}, $f$ being divisible by $Q$.
    \par\textbf{2.} Trivial as we have unique division by $P$ with remainders of degree less than $d$ in $A\langle T\rangle$ and $A[T]$, by Proposition \ref{proposition_26_1}, and elementary algebra.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Topological Noetheriannes}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_27_1}
