    We say that $(m_i)_{i=1}^k$ generates the $A$-module $M$ topologically if $\vec{m}:A^k\to M$ is open and surjective. We say that $M$ is topologically finitely generated if such $k$ and $(m_i)_{i=1,\cdots,k}\in M^k$ can be taken.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_27_1}
    We have the following fact about Definition \ref{definition_27_1}.
    \begin{enumerate}[1.]
        \item Every topological finitely generated $M$ is also finitely generated in the algebraic sense. However, the converse is not true in general.
        \item In the notation of Definition \ref{definition_27_1}, if $l>k$, and $(m_i)_{i=k+1,\cdots,l}\in M^{l-k}$ are given then $(m_i)_{i=1,\cdots,l}$ also generate $M$ topologically.
        \item If $M$ is topologically finitely generated then the topology of $M$ is the finite nat-$A$-module topology. In particular, it does not depend the chosen generator.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    \textbf{3.} Indeed, if $U\subseteq M$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $M$ for some nat-$A$-module topology, then by the continuity of the module operation, there is a neighborhood $V$ of $0$ in $A$ such that $\vec{m}(V^k)\subseteq U$. By the assumption $\vec{m}(V^k)$ is also open hence $U$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $M$ in the original topology.
\end{proof}
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_27_2}
    Let $M$ be a nat-$A$-module, we say that $M$ is topologically Noetherian if every submodule $N\subseteq M$ is topologically finitely generated when equipped with the induced topology.
\end{definition}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_27_2}
    We have following facts about Definition \ref{definition_27_2}.
    \begin{enumerate}[1.]
        \item Every topologically Noetherian $A$-module is Noetherian.
        \item Every submodule of a topologically Noetherian module is topologically Noetherian when equipped with the induced topology.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_27_1}
    Let $M$ be a nat-$A$-module ad $M'\subseteq M$ a submodule equipped with the induced topology. We equip $M''=M/M'$ the quotient topology. If $M',M''$ are both topologically Noetherian/topologically finitely generated, then so is $M$. If $N\subseteq M$ is a submodule such that $N\cap M'$ is topologically finitely generated for the topology induced from $M$ and $N''=N/N'$ is topologically finitely generated for the topology induced from $M''$, then $N$ is also topologically finitely generated.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    For the finitely generated case, let $N=M,N'=M',N''=M''$ other wise let $N\subseteq M$ be any submodule and $N' = M'\cap N$, both equipped with the topology induced from $M$, and $N''=N/N'\subseteq M''$ equipeed with the topology induced from $M''$. In each case, $N'$ and $N''$ are topologically finitely generated. 
    By the assertion there are topological generators $(\eta_i\mod{N'})_{i=1}^k$ of $N''$ and $(\nu_i)_{i=0}^l$ of $N'$. THen $(\eta_1,\cdots,\eta_k,\nu_1,\cdots,\nu_l)$ generates $N$ and we would like to show that they do as topologically.
    \par We demand the open surjective maps $\vec{\eta}:A^k\to N'',\vec{\nu}:A^l\to N'$. Let $U\subseteq A$ be an open subgroup. We must find an open subgroup $V\subseteq M$ such that $\vec{m}(U^{k+l}) \supseteq V$ where $\vec{m}$ corresonds to $(\eta_1,\cdots,\eta_k,\nu_1,\cdots,\nu_l)$. 
    By assumption about $\nu_1,\cdots,\nu_l$, there is an open subgroup $v_1\subseteq U$ such that $\vec{\nu}(U^l)\supseteq V_1$. Similarly there is $U_1\subseteq U$ such that $\vec{\eta}(U^k)\subseteq V_1\cap N'$. By the continuity of the module operation there is an open subgroup $U_1\subseteq U$ such that $\vec{\eta}(U_1^k)\subseteq V_1$. 
    by the assumption on $\vec{\eta}$, there is an open subgroup $W\subseteq M''$ such that $\vec{\eta}(U_1^k)\supseteq W\cap N''$. Then $V= \pi^{-1}(W)\cap V_1$ will do where $M\stackrel{\pi}{\to}M''$ denotes the projection. Indeed, if $n\in N\cap V$ then $\pi(n)\in W$, hence there are $(a_i)_{i=1}^k\in U_1^k\subseteq U^k$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        n- \sum_{i=1}^ka_i\eta_i\in V_1\cap N
    \end{equation*}
    (it is in $N'$ and subtrahend is in $V_1$ by selection of $V_1$). Thus there are $(\alpha)_{i=1}^l\in U^l$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        n = \vec{\eta}(\vec{a})+\vec{\nu}(\vec{\alpha})
    \end{equation*}
    as claimed for $V$. The latter statement is shown in the proof.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}
    If $A$ is complete and has a countable neighborhood basis of $0$ and $M$ is a complete nat-$A$-module with a countable neighborhood base of $0$, then every topologically finitely generated (with induced topology) submodule $N\subseteq M$ is closed in $M$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $U_1\supseteq U_2\supseteq\cdots$ be a sequence of open subgruops of $A$ which forms a neighborhood basis of $0$ in $A$. Let $m = \lim_{i\to\infty}n_i$ in $M$ and all $n_i\in N$, let $\vec{\nu} = (\nu_i)_{i=1,\cdots,k}$ be topological generator of $N$ hence $\vec{\nu}:A^k\to N$ is open and surjective. Without loss of generality (taking subsequence), $n_{i+1}-n_i$ is in the image of $U_i^l$ under the above map. Let $\alpha^i_1,\cdots,\alpha^i_l$ be such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        n_{i+1}-n_i = \vec{\nu}(\vec{\alpha^i}).
    \end{equation*}
    let $n_0 = \vec{\nu}(\vec{\alpha^0})$ and set 
    \begin{equation*}
        \alpha_ j =\sum_{i=0}^\infty \alpha^i_j.
    \end{equation*}
    THese converge in $A$ as they are Cauchy and $\vec{\nu}(\vec{\alpha}) = m$ hence $m\in N$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_28_3}
    $A$ and $M$ be complete with countable neighborhood bases of $0$, and $N\subseteq M$ be a topologically finitely generated with topology induced from $M$, then $N$ is closed in $M$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_28_2}
    Let $A$ be a complete, topologically Noetherian ring with a countable neighborhood basis of $0$. The following statements hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[1.]
        \item All $A^n$ equipped with the product topologies are topologically Noetherian.
        \item Every finitely generated $A$-module $M$ has a unique nat-$A$-module topology from which it is topologically Noetherian. This is the final nat-$A$-topology on $M$. (follows from Fact 1). If $M =A^n$ this coincides with the product topology.
        \item Let $M,N$ be finitely generated $A$-module with the topology in \textbf{2}. Then every surjective morphism $M\stackrel{f}{\to}N$ is continuous and open.
        \item Every topologically Noetherian $A$-module is Hausdorff and every submodule is closed.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}$\:$
    \par\textbf{1.} Induction on $n$ using Proposition \ref{proposition_27_1}.
    \par\textbf{2.} Let $M$ be finitely generated. Choose $\vec{m}=(m_i)_{i=1}^n\in M^n$ such that $A^n\stackrel{\vec{m}}{\to}M$ is surjective and $M$ is equipped with the quotient topology. Then $M$ is a topologically Noetherian (need to check?). Automatically the continuity is clear as any $M\to N$ can be lifted as 
    \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
A^k \arrow[d, "\vec{m}"'] \arrow[r] & A^l \arrow[d, "\vec{n}"] \\
M \arrow[r, "f"']                   & N                       
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center} 
    The upper horizontal arrow is continuous. If the map is surjective, the nthe set of generators of $M$ consisting of $\vec{m}$ can be chosen such that it induces preimage under $f$ of the generator and for $N$, and $A^k\to A^l$ to be surjective, hence split surjective hence open.
    \par\textbf{4.} Since $M$ is topologically Noetherian it is topologically finitely generated and carries the quotient topology from $A^k\stackrel{\vec{m}}{\to}M$ for some set of $k$ generators of $M$. 
    Let $N\subseteq M$ be a submoule. Since $A^k$ is topologically Noetherian by \textbf{1}, $\vec{m}^{-1}(M)\subseteq A^k$ is topologically finitely generated for the induced topology hence closed in $A^k$ by Proposition \ref{fact_28_3}. As $M$ carries the quotient topoloy, $N$ is closed. If $N=\{0\}$, this shows that $M$ is Hausdorff.
\end{proof}
\subsection{Ring Norms}
\begin{definition}
    Let $R$ be a ring. A map $\norm{\cdot}:R\to \R$ is calle a ring norm if it satisfies the following conditions.
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
        \item $\norm{x+y}\leq \max\{\norm{x},\norm{y}\}$.
        \item $\norm{1} = 1$.
        \item $\norm{ab}\leq \norm{a}\norm{b}$.
        \item $\norm{x} = 0\iff x=0$.
    \end{enumerate}
    \label{definition_28_2}
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
    A ring norm induces a metric on $R$ and hence a topology on $R$ as well.
    \begin{align*}
        \{x\in R\sep \norm{x}\leq 1\}\subseteq R^\circ,\\
        \{x\in R\sep \norm{x}<1\}\subseteq R^\dcirc.
    \end{align*}
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
    A ring norm is power-multiplicative if for any $x\in R$ and $r\in \N$, we have $\norm{x^r}=\norm{x}^r$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
    If $R$ is power-multiplicative, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \{x\in R\sep \norm{x}<1\}= R^\dcirc.
    \end{equation*}
\end{remark}
\begin{definition}
    A ring norm is multiplicative if the third condition in Definition \ref{definition_28_2} is an equality.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    If $\norm{\cdot}:R\to \R$ is multiplicative, then any subset $X$ of $R$ is bounded if $\norm{X}$ is bounded with respect to the Euclidean norm on $\R$. Furthermore, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \{x\in R\sep \norm{x}\leq1\}= R^\circ.
    \end{equation*}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}$\:$
    \label{fact_29_4}
    \begin{enumerate}[1.]
        \item If $M$ is a nat-$A$-module, $M'\subseteq N\subseteq M$ submodules with the induced topology, and $M/M'$ is equipped with the quotient topology, then the topology on $N/M'$ is induced from $M/M'$ conincides with the quotient topology.
        \item The quotient of a topologically Noetherian module by a submodule is topologically Noetherian when equipped with the quotient topology.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}\textbf{1.} 
    It is clear that any open set for the induced topology is open for the quotient topology. Indeed, let $M\stackrel{\pi}{\to}M/M'$ denotes the projection. If $\Omega\subseteq M/M'$ is open then $\pi^{-1}(\Omega\cap(N/M'))=N\cap \pi^{-1}(\Omega)$ is open in $N$. Let $\Theta\subseteq N/M'$ be such that $\pi^{-1}(\Theta)$ is an open subgroup of $N$. 
    Then there is an open subgropu $V\subseteq M$ such that $\pi^{-1}(\Theta)\supseteq N\cap V$. Without loss of generality, we assume $V\supseteq M'$, otherwise $V\leftarrow V+M'$, as $\pi^{-1}(\Omega)$ is invariant
    under translation by elements of $M'$ and $M'\subseteq N$. Let $\Omega = \pi(V)$, then $\Omega\cap(N/M')\subseteq \Theta$. If $\pi(n)\in\Theta$, then $n\in\Theta+M'=\Theta$ hence $n\in N\cap V\subseteq\pi^{-1}(\Omega)$.
    \par\textbf{2.} Follows from \textbf{1}. If $N''\subseteq M/M''$ then $\pi^{-1}(N'')$ is topologically finitely generated with the induced topology from $M$. Hence the same holds for its 
    quotient by $M''$ which by \textbf{1}, coincides with the topology induced from $M/M'$. 
\end{proof}

End of proof of Proposition 2

Since $A^N$ is topologically Noetherian by Proposition 2.1, so is any finitely generated $A$-module with the quotient topology from $A^N$ which is the topologically finitely generated, hence the finite nat-topology by Fact 1, hence is independent of the choice of generators.


\begin{remark}
    Without $M'\subseteq N$, Fact 4.1 will be false.
    \par Let $V$ be a $\Q_p$-Banach space and $M=V\oplus V$, $M'=V\oplus\{0\}$, $N\subseteq V\oplus V$ the graph of a bounded linear on-surjective operator with a dense image by $V=l_\infty$, $L(X_i)_{i=0}^\infty =(p^iX_i)^\infty_{i=0}$, $N=(\text{Graph of }L)$.
\end{remark}
\section{Acyclicity proofs}

The Acyclicity proofs usually consider a special kinds of Čech cohomology to show that $\cO_X$ extends from $\Rat_X$ to $\Ouv_X$. Acyclicity will typically follow using the following statements.
\begin{proposition*}[Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma]
% REVIEW [acyclicity lemma]: In condition (c), the conclusion should be X_doubleprime in mathscr X (X_prime is already assumed in mathscr X). [original line 3533]
    Let $\mathscr{A}\stackrel{F}{\to}\mathscr{B}$ be a left exact functor between abelian categories where $\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B}$ contain sufficiently many injective objects. Let $\mathscr{X}$ be a class of objects of $\mathscr{A}$
    such that
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item Every direct summand of (in particular, every object isomorphic to) an element of $\mathscr{X}$ is in $\mathscr{X}$.
        \item Every objet $A$ of $\mathscr{A}$ has a monomorphism $A\to X$ with $X\in\mathscr{X}$.
        \item If
        \begin{equation*}
            0\to X'\to X\to X''\to 0,
        \end{equation*}
         is a short exact sequence in $\mathscr{A}$ such that $X',X\in\mathscr{X}$, then $X'\in\mathscr{X}$ and
        \begin{equation*}
            0\to FX'\to FX\to FX''\to 0.
        \end{equation*}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition*}
\begin{proof}
    See Grothendieck Tohoku Paper Lemma 3.3.1.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary*}
% REVIEW [corollary]: State: every injective is in mathscr X, and for every X in mathscr X and p>0, R^pF(X)=0. [original line 3553]
    All injective objects of $\sA$ belongs to $\sX$ and furthermore $X\in\sX$ and $p>0$, we have $R^pFX = 0$.
\end{corollary*}

\subsection{Acyclicity Criteria for General Sheaves on $\Spa A$}

In this subsection, we denote $X=\Spa A$ where $A$ is a Huber pair.

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_23_1}
    Let $x\in X$ be such that $\cGamma_x = \Gamma_x$ and $U$ be an open subset of $X$ such that $x\in U$. Then there is $m\in\N$ and $(f_i)_{i=0}^m\in(A^\triangleleft)^{m+1}$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft
    \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, Proposition \ref{fact_15_11}, this holds when $x$ is closed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
    Since rational open subsets form a topology base, there are $(\varphi_i)_{i=0}^m$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR_X(\varphi_0|\varphi_1,\cdots,\varphi_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    If $a\in A$ is such that $\ab{a}_x\not=0$, then this implies
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR(a\varphi_0|a\varphi_1,\cdots,a\varphi_m)\subseteq\cR(\varphi_0|\varphi_1,\cdots,\varphi_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    As $\ab{\varphi_0}_x\in\Gamma_x = \cGamma_x$, such $a$ can be chosen so that
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{\varphi_0a}_x\geq 1.
    \end{equation*}
    Then
    \begin{equation*}
        x\in\cR(a\varphi_0|a\varphi_1,\cdots,a\varphi_m,1)\subseteq\cR(a\varphi_0|a\varphi_1,\cdots,a\varphi_m)\subseteq U.
    \end{equation*}
    Obviously,
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle a\varphi_0,\cdots,a\varphi_m,1\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}$\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item $A$ being Tate or analytic is also sufficient to the above assertion for arbitrary $x$.
% REVIEW [DRAFT PLACEHOLDER]: This line still contains literal question marks and is not a finished mathematical remark. [original line 3599]
        \item Thus $\mathcal{O}_X(\cR(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m))$ can be bounded by the ? Then 3b) (where ? $f_0$ is not necessary).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}$\:$
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item A rational covering of $X$ is a covering $X = \bigcup_{i=1}^m\cR_X(f_i|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$ such that
        \begin{equation*}
            \langle f_1,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft.
        \end{equation*}
        \item The rational sieve defined by such $(f_i)_{i=1}^m$ is
        \begin{equation*}
            [\mathcal{R}(f_i|f_1,\cdots,f_m)\sep i=1,\cdots,m].
        \end{equation*}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_23_2}
    $\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Every open covering $\U$ of $X$ has a refinement which is a rational covering.
        \item Every covering sieve (i.e. $X=\bigcup_{U\in\sS}U$) of $X$ contains a rational sieve.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [scope]: Proposition 23.1 applies here to closed points (where cGamma=Gamma), not arbitrary x. Start the construction with x in X_cl. [original line 3626]
    Let $\sS$ be a covering sieve of $X$. By Proposition \ref{proposition_23_1}, for $x\in X$, we have $U_x\in\sS$ such that $x\in U_x$ and $(f_{x,i})_{i=0,\cdots,m_x}$ with
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_{x,0},\cdots,f_{x,m_x}\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = A^\triangleleft
    \end{equation*}
    such that $x\in\cR(f_{x,0}|f_{x,1},\cdots,f_{x,m_x})\subseteq U_x$. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        V = \bigcup_{x\in X_{\mathrm{cl}}}\cR_X(f_{x,0}|f_{x,1},\cdots,f_{x,m_x}).
    \end{equation*}
    Looking at the right hand side $V$ contains all closed points. If $V\subsetneqq X$, then $X\backslash V$ would be a non-empty closed subset without closed points, a contradiction by Proposition \ref{proposition_1_2_7}.
    \par As $X$ is spectral, it is quasi-compact, hence there are finitely many closed points $(x_i)_{i=1}^n$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        X = \bigcup_{i=1}^n\cR(f_{x_i,0}|f_{x_i,1},\cdots,f_{x_i,m_i}).
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [index]: f_{i_j} should be f_{i,j}; line 3649 also needs m_j instead of m. [original line 3639]
    We denote $f_{i_j} = f_{x_i,j},n_i = m_{x_i},U_i = \cR(f_{i,0}|f_{i,1},\cdots,f_{i,m_i})$. Let $J$ be the set of functions defined on $\{1,\cdots,n\}$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
       \forall \iota\in J, 0\leq \iota(j)\leq m_j,
    \end{equation*}
    and let $J_0\subseteq J$ be the subset set of all $\iota\in J$ such that there is $j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}$ with $\iota(j)=0$. For all $\iota\in J$, let
    \begin{equation*}
        f_\iota = \prod_{j=1}^nf_{j,\iota(j)}.
    \end{equation*}
    Clearly,
    \begin{equation*}
        \langle f_\iota\sep \iota\in J\rangle_{A^\triangleleft} = \prod_{j=1}^n\langle f_{j,0},\cdots,f_{j,m}\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}=A^\triangleleft
    \end{equation*}
    by the assumption. Note that
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
        \item $\langle f_{j,0},\cdots,f_{j,m_j}\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}=A^\triangleleft$.
        \item Since for every $x\in X$, there is $j$ such that $x\in U_j$, we can improve any $f_{j,\iota(j)}$ by replacing it to $f_{j,0}$ hence $\max_{\iota\in J}\ab{f_\iota}_x = \max_{\iota\in J_0}\ab{f_\iota}_x$.
        \item Using Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2} twice, to $\langle f_\iota\sep \iota\in J\rangle$ and $\langle f_\iota\sep \iota\in J_0\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}$. We see that the latter also generates $A^\triangleleft$.
    \end{enumerate}
    Then the $(f_\iota)_{\iota\in J_0}$ defines a rational covering. We will show that the rational sieve generated by this is contained in $\sS$. To prove this claim, it suffices to consider,
    \begin{equation*}
        \Omega_\lambda = \cR(f_\lambda|\text{ all $f_\iota$ with $\iota\in J_0$}).
    \end{equation*}
    with $\lambda\in J_0$ and to show that $\Omega_\lambda\in\sS$, it suffices to find $j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \Omega_\lambda\subseteq U_j.
    \end{equation*}
    We claim that this holds when $\lambda(j) = 0$.
    Indeed, let $x\in\Omega_\lambda$ then
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{f_\lambda}_x = \prod_{k=1}^n\ab{f_{k,\lambda(k)}}\not=0.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence $0\not=\ab{f_{j,\lambda(j)}}_x = \ab{f_{j,0}}_x$.
    If $x\not\in U_j$, there would be $i\in \{1,\cdots,m_j\}$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
% REVIEW [notation]: Add the missing subscript x on |f_{j,0}|. [original line 3673]
        \ab{f_{j,i}}_x>\ab{f_{j,0}}.
    \end{equation*}
    Let
    \begin{equation*}
        \lambda'(k) = \begin{cases}
            \lambda(k),\quad k\not=j,\\
            i,\quad k=j.
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{f_{\lambda'}(x)} = \ab{f_{j,i}(x)}\prod_{k\not=j}\ab{f_{k,\lambda(k)}}_x>\ab{f_{j,0}}_x\prod_{k\not=j}\ab{f_{k,\lambda(k)}}_x = \ab{f_\lambda(x)}
    \end{equation*}
    Hence there is $\theta\in J_0$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
    \ab{f_\theta(x)}\geq \ab{f_{\lambda'}}_x>\ab{f_\lambda(x)},
    \end{equation*}
    contradicting $x\in\cR(f_\lambda|\text{ all $f_\iota$ for $\iota\in J_0$})\subseteq\cR(f_\lambda|f_\theta)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_3}
    Let $A$ be a Huber pair and $X=\Spa A$ and $\mathcal{G}$ be a presheaf of abelian groups on $\Rat_X$ such that for every $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and every rational covering $\mathcal{U}$ of $\Omega$, the map,
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{G}(\Omega)\to \check{H}^0(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{G})
    \end{equation*}
    is bijective. Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{G}\to(\underline{\Sheaf}(\mathcal{G}))_{\Rat_X}
    \end{equation*}
    is an isomorphism in the category of presheaves on $\Rat_X$.
\end{proposition}

% REVIEW [major]: The acyclicity-class proposition has no proof that the class satisfies Grothendieck's acyclicity-lemma hypotheses; the current wording is circular/unfinished. [original line 3706]
\begin{proposition}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where $A$ is a Huber pair. Set $\mathscr{X}$ to be a class of sheaves of abelian groups such that
    \begin{equation*}
    \mathscr{X} = \left\{\cF\in\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)\:\bigg{\vert}\:\substack{\forall p>0, \forall \Omega\in\Rat_X, \\\text{ and $\mathcal{U}$ rational covering of $\Omega$} \check{H}^p(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{F}) = 0}\right\},
    \end{equation*}
    together with a left exact functor
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}(\Omega)
    \end{equation*}
    where $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ satisfies the assumption of Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma. Hence due to corollary of it, all injective sheaves are in $\mathscr{X}$. In particular, $\Omega\in\Rat_X,\mathcal{F}\in\mathscr{X}$ and $p>0$ then $H^p(\Omega,\mathcal{F})=0$.
\end{proposition}

Under the assumption of $A$ being Tate, we have more convenient acyclicity criterion.

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_24_2}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where $(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ is a Huber pair. We inductively define the property of a sieve $\sS$ in $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ to have a Laurent order at most $o$ as follows.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item A sieve has Laurent order $0$ if it is the all sieve.
        \item If $o>0$ and Laurent order $\leq o-1$ has already been defined then $\sS$ has Laurent order $\leq o$ if there is $g\in \cO_X(\Omega)$ such that $\sS|_{\cR_\Omega(g|1)}$ and $\sS|_{\cR_\Omega(1|g)}$ both have Laurent order $\leq o-1$.
    \end{enumerate}
    The Laurent order of $\sS$ is the smallest $o\in N$ such that $\sS$ has Laurent order $\leq o$ if no such $o$ exists then its Laurent order is $\infty$. We denote $o_L(\sS)$ to be the Laurent order of $\sS$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_24_1}Let $\sS$ be a sieve on $\Omega$. Then the following statements hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[a.]
        \item $\sS\subseteq\mathcal{T}\Rightarrow o_L(\mathcal{T})\leq o_L(\sS)$.
        \item $\Theta\subseteq\Omega\Rightarrow o_{L,\Theta}(\sS|_\Theta)\leq o_{L,\Omega}(\sS)$.
        \item $o_L(\sS)<\infty\Rightarrow \sS$ is a covering sieve.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_24_1}
    Let $G=\{g_i\sep 1\leq i\leq n\}$ be a non-empty finite subset of $\cO_X(\Omega)^\times$.
    Then the rational sieve defined by $G$ has finite Laurent order.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
% REVIEW [terminology]: preceq is a preorder ("partial order up to equivalence"), not a partial order. [original line 3745]
    We write $g_i\preceq g_j$ if $\Omega=\cR(g_j|g_i)$. Obviously, this is a partial order with allowed equivalence. We call $(i,j)$ in order if at least one of $g_i\preceq g_j$ or $g_j\preceq g_i$ holds.
    We prove the claim by induction on the number of pairs not in order.
% REVIEW [indices]: G has n elements, but the proof switches to m and even uses g_0. After sorting use g_1 preceq ... preceq g_n and R(g_n|g_1,...,g_{n-1})=Omega. [original line 3747]
    \par If all pairs are in order then without loss of generality by renumbering $G$, we can assume $g_1\preceq \cdots\preceq g_m$. Thus, all $g_i\preceq g_m$. In this case, observe that
    \begin{equation*}
    \Omega = \cR_\Omega(g_m|g_0,\cdots,g_{m-1}).
    \end{equation*}
    Hence the rational sieve generated by the rational cover of $G$ contains $\Omega$ hence is the all sieve. Therefore by definition, it has Laurent order $0$.
    \par Suppose $(i,j)$ is not in order and the assertion shown for a smaller number of pairs not in order. Let $q ={\frac {g_i} {g_j}}$ and $\Theta_1 = \cR_\Omega(q|1)$ and $\Theta_2=\cR_\Omega(1|q)$. Then the pairs in order stay in order after restricting to $\Theta_i$ as they are subsets of $\Omega$. Furthermore, $g_j|_{\Theta_1}\preceq g_i|_{\Theta_1}$ and $g_i|_{\Theta_2}\preceq g_j|_{\Theta2}$.
    Thus $(i,j)$ becomes in order for $G_k = \{g_i|_{\Theta_k}\sep 1\leq i\leq n\}$ where $k=1,2$. By the induction assumption and Definition \ref{definition_24_2}, the rational sieve defined by $G$ has finite Laurent order.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_24_2}
    Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ be a Huber pair such that $A^\triangleleft$ is Tate and there is $(f_i)_{i=0}^m\in A^{m+1}$ with $\langle f_0,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}=A^\triangleleft$. Then we can take $\varepsilon\in (A^{\triangleleft})^\times\cap (A^{\triangleleft})^\dcirc$ satisfying
    \begin{equation*}
% REVIEW [index]: max should run 0<=i<=m, not n. [original line 3760]
        \forall x\in X,\ab{\varepsilon}_x \leq\max_{0\leq i\leq n}\ab{f_i}_x.\label{eq:lemma_24_2_3}
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    Let $s\in (A^{\triangleleft})^\dcirc\cap (A^{\triangleleft})^\times$. By assumption, there are $(\lambda_i)_{i=0,\cdots,m}\in (A^\triangleleft)^{m+1}$ giving
    \begin{equation*}
        \sum_{i=0}^m \lambda_i f_i = 1.
    \end{equation*}
% REVIEW [choice]: Ensure j>=1 so epsilon=s^j is still topologically nilpotent; j=0 would give epsilon=1. [original line 3769]
    Since $A^+$ is an open subring and $\lim_{j\to\infty} s^j\lambda_i = 0$, there is $j\in\N$ such that for each $i=0,\cdots,m$, $\lambda_is^j\in A^+$. Then one can set $\varepsilon = s^j$. Indeed,
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{\varepsilon}_x =\left\vert\sum_{i=0}^m (s^j\lambda_i)f_i\right\vert_x\leq\max_{0\leq i\leq m}\ab{\lambda_is^j}_x\ab{f_i}_x\leq \max_{0\leq i\leq m}\ab{f_i}_x.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_5}
    In the situation of Definition \ref{definition_24_2}, assume $A$ (i.e $A^\triangleleft$) to be Tate. Then a sieve $\sS$ on $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ covers $\Omega$ if and only if $o_L(\sS)<\infty$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The proof will work by showing that every rational sieve has finite $o_L$ which proves the claim by Proposition \ref{proposition_23_2} and \ref{fact_24_1} a.
    \par It is sufficient to show that every rational sieve has finite Laurent order. Let $\sS$ be the rational sieve defined by the finite subset $G$ of $\cO_X(\Omega)$. We use induction on the number of elements of $G$ which are not in $\mathcal{O}_X^\times(\Omega)$.
    If this number is $0$, Lemma \ref{lemma_24_1} can be applied. Suppose $g_1\not\in\mathcal{O}_X^\times(\Omega)$ and the assertion shown for a smaller number of non-elements.
    Since $A$ is Tate, there is $\delta\in\mathcal{O}_X^\times(\Omega)$ such that $\ab{\delta}_\omega <\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{g_i}_\omega$ holds for all $\omega\in\Omega$. Indeed, from the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma_24_2} shows the strict inequality can be attained. Consider
    \begin{equation*}
        \Theta_1 = \cR_\Omega(g_1|\delta)=\cR\left({\frac {g_1} \delta}\bigg{\vert}1\right),\Theta_2 = \cR_\Omega(\delta|g_1) = \cR\left(1\bigg{\vert}{\frac {g_1} \delta}\right).
    \end{equation*}
    On $\Theta_1,g_1$ becomes a unit as $\ab{g_1}_\theta\geq\ab{\delta}_\theta\not=0$ when $\theta\in\Theta_1$ hence the induction assumption applies to $G|_{\Theta_1}$.
    On the other hand for $\theta_2\in\Theta_2$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{g_1}_{\theta_2}\leq \ab{\delta}_{\theta_2}<\max_{1\leq i\leq m}\ab{g_i}_{\theta_2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, we obtain,
    \begin{equation*}
        \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_1|g_2,\cdots,g_m) = \emptyset.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, the inequality shows,
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall 2\leq i\leq m, \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_i|g_1,\cdots,g_m) = \cR_{\Theta_2}(g_i|g_2,\cdots,g_m).
    \end{equation*}
    Applying Theorem \ref{theorem_17_2}.d, and that $g_2|_{\Theta_2},\cdots g_m|_{\Theta_2}$ have no common zero on $\Theta_2$, these also define a rational covering, hence the induction hypothesis can be applied.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_6}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where $A$ is Tate, $\mathcal{G}$ be a presheaf of abelian groups on $\Rat_X$ such that for all $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and all $g\in\mathcal{O}_X(\Omega)$, the sequence,
    \begin{equation*}
        0\to\mathcal{G}(\Omega)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{r\mapsto(r|_{\Omega_1},r|_{\Omega_2})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}(\Omega_1)\oplus\mathcal{G}(\Omega_2)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{(r_1,r_2)\mapsto(r_1|_{\Omega_{12}}-r_2|_{\Omega_{12}})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}(\Omega_{12})\to0.
    \end{equation*}
    is exact where $\Omega_1 = \cR_\Omega(g|1),\Omega_2 = \cR_\Omega(1|g)$ and $\Omega_{12} = \Omega_1\cap \Omega_2$.
    \par Then $\mathcal{G}\to(\underline{\Sheaf}(\mathcal{G}))|_{\Rat_X}$ is an isomorphism in the category of presheaves of abelian groups on $X$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_24_7}
    Let $X=\Spa A$ where the Huber pair $A$ is Tate.
    Let $\mathscr{X}$ be a subclass of $\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)$ containing any $\cF\in\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        0\to\mathcal{F}(\Omega)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{f\mapsto(f|_{\Omega_1},f|_{\Omega_2})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{F}(\Omega_1)\oplus\mathcal{F}(\Omega_2)\stackrel{\substack{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{(f_1,f_2)\mapsto(f_1|_{\Omega_12}-f_2|_{\Omega_{12}})}\\\:}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{F}(\Omega_{12})\to 0
    \end{equation*}
    is exact for all $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and all $g\in\mathcal{O}_X(\Omega)$ where $\Omega_1,\Omega_2$ and $\Omega_{12}$ are as in Proposition \ref{proposition_24_6}. Then the assumption in Grothendieck's acyclicity lemma holds for $\mathscr{X}$ and $\mathscr{A}=\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(X)$ with the functor $\cF\mapsto \cF(\Omega)$ for any $\Omega\in\Rat_X$.
\end{proposition}

\subsection{Acyclicity Criterion for Tate Maps}

\begin{definition}
  Let $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ be a Huber pair such that $A^\triangleleft$ is Tate. Set $X=\Spa(A)$ then for $\Omega\in\Rat_X$ and $g\in\cO_X(\Omega)$, $\Omega_1 = \cR_\Omega(g|1),\Omega_2 = \cR_\Omega(1|g)$ are called Laurent covering of $\Omega$.
\end{definition}
\begin{remark}
  \begin{equation*}
  \Omega_{12} = \Omega_1\cap \Omega_2 = \cR_\Omega(g|g^2,1). 
\end{equation*}
Note that $\ab{g}_x^2\leq \ab{g}_x$ gives that for all $x\in\Omega_{12},\ab{g}_x\leq 1$.
\end{remark}
We know that for $\Theta=\cR_X(f_0|f_1,\cdots,f_m)$, the structure presheaf $\cO_X(\Theta) = A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle/\overline{I}$ where 
\begin{equation*}
  I = \langle f_i-f_0X_i\sep i=1,\cdots,m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle}
\end{equation*}
provided that 
\begin{equation*}
  \langle f_0,f_1,\cdots,f_m\rangle_{A^\triangleleft}= A^\triangleleft,
\end{equation*}
which is automatic as $A^\triangleleft$ is Tate and $\Theta$ is rational open.

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_25_1}
 Let $A$ be a sequentially complete nat-ring.  We denote 
 \begin{equation*}
    A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle = \left\{f = \sum_{k=-\infty}^\infty f_kT^k \:\bigg{\vert}\: \forall k\in\Z, f_k\in A,\lim_{k\to\pm\infty}f_k=0\right\}.
 \end{equation*}
 The addition is defined coefficient wise and the multiplication is define by 
 \begin{equation*}
    fg = \sum_{k\in\Z}\left(\sum_{i+j=k}f_ig_j\right)T^k.
 \end{equation*}
 The neighborhood of $0$ in this space is given by the subsets of. the form,
 \begin{equation*}
    U\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle = \left\{f = \sum_{k=-\infty}^\infty f_kT^k \:\bigg{\vert}\: \forall k\in\Z, f_k\in U,\lim_{k\to\pm\infty}f_k=0\right\},
 \end{equation*}
 where $U$ running over all open subgroups of $A$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_25_1}
    \label{proposition_25_1_fact}
    Such $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ in Definition \ref{definition_25_1} has the following properties.
    \begin{enumerate}[a. ]
        \item $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ is a complete nat ring.
        \item If $A$ is Tate then so is $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$.
        \item The ideal $I = (XY-1)A\langle X,Y\rangle$ is closed in $A\langle X,Y\rangle$ and there is an isomorphism,
        \begin{equation*}
            A\langle X,Y\rangle/(1-XY)A\langle X,Y\rangle \stackrel{\mathmakebox[0pt][c]{X\mapsto T,Y\mapsto T^{-1}}}{\to} A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle.
        \end{equation*}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Exercise in Sheet 1 beside Tateness.
    \par Let $s\in A^\dcirc\cap A^\times$, then $s$ is a topologically nilpotent unit of $A\langle X,Y\rangle$ and also of $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$.
    \par Let $A^\#\subseteq A$ be an open bounded subring. Then $A^\#\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ is an open subring. If $W\subseteq A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ is a neighborhood of $0$, 
    there are open subgropus $U\subseteq A$ such that $U\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle\subseteq W$ and as $A^\#$ is bouned, an open subgroup $V\subseteq A$ such that $A^\#V\subseteq U$. 
    In this case $\Omega = V\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ is a neighborhood of $0$ in $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ and $\Omega A^\#\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle\subseteq U\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle\subseteq W$ sharing boundedness of $A^\#\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$.
\end{proof}

The following observation does not assume Tateness yet. 
\par Let $Z=\Spa(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ with $A^\triangleleft$ complete and $\Omega_1 = \cR_Z(g|1),\Omega_2= \cR_Z(1|g)$ be a Laurent covering for some $g\in A^\triangleleft$. Due to Theorem \ref{theorem_20_3_3}, we have 
\begin{align*}
    \cO_Z(\Omega_1)&\cong A^\triangleleft\langle T\rangle/\overline{(1-gT)A^\triangleleft\langle T\rangle},\\
    \cO_Z(\Omega_2) &\cong A^\triangleleft\langle T\rangle/\overline{(g-T)A^\triangleleft\langle T\rangle},\\
    \cO_Z(\Omega_{12})&\cong A^\triangleleft\langle X,Y\rangle/\overline{(1-gX,g^2-gY)A^\triangleleft\langle X,Y\rangle}.
\end{align*}

In the last ring, $g$ is a unit and $g^2= gY$, hence $Y = g$ and $X= g^{-1}$. Hence showing $XY=1$ and by Proposition \ref{fact_25_1}.3, the presheaf ring is isomorphic to
\begin{align*}
    \cO_Z(\Omega_{12}) &\cong A^\triangleleft\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle/\overline{(1-gT)A^\triangleleft\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle},\\
    &\cong A^\triangleleft\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle/\overline{(T^{-1}-g)A^\triangleleft\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle}.
\end{align*}

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_25_2}
    For a Huber pair $A= (A^\triangleleft,A^+)$,
    We put 
\begin{align*}
    A^\triangleleft\langle g\rangle&\defeq A\langle T\rangle/\overline{(T-g)A^\triangleleft\langle T\rangle},\\
    A^\triangleleft\langle g^{-1}\rangle &\defeq A\langle T\rangle/\overline{(1-gT)A^\triangleleft\langle T\rangle},\\
    A^\triangleleft\langle g,g^{-1}\rangle &\defeq A^\triangleleft\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle /\overline{(1-gT)A^\triangleleft\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle}.
\end{align*}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    Let $B^\triangleleft$ be a complete Tate ring. A Huber pair $A=(A^\triangleleft,A^+)$ is called sheafy if the following holds for $X= \Spa(A)$.
    \begin{enumerate}[i. ]
        \item The morphism $\cO_X\to \underline{\Sheaf}(\cO_X)|_{\Rat_X}$ is an isomorphism.
        \item $H^p(X,\cO_X) = 0$ for all $p>0$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{corollary}
    \label{corollary_25_1}
    For a complete Tate ring $B^\triangleleft$. Suppose that for all $A^\triangleleft$ which are quotients of $B^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ by the closure of a finitely generated ideal and for all $g\in A^\triangleleft$, the sequence 
    \begin{equation}
        0\to A^\triangleleft\to A^\triangleleft\langle g\rangle\oplus A^\triangleleft\langle g^{-1}\rangle\to A^\triangleleft\langle g,g^{-1}\rangle\to 0\label{corollary_25_1_eq_1}
    \end{equation}
    is exact. Then for all such $A^\triangleleft$, all Huber pairs whose first component is $A^\triangleleft$ are sheafy.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_25_1}
    Let $A$ be a sequentially complete nat ring. Assume that all principal ideals in $A\langle T\rangle$ and $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle$ are closed. Then Equation \eqref{corollary_25_1_eq_1} is exact for all $g\in A^\triangleleft$. % The professor wrote either in one of them but i'm suspecting that we need this
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Since no Huber pair is being considered, we simply put $A=A^\triangleleft$. Set 
    \begin{align*}
        I &\defeq\overline{(1-gX)A\langle X\rangle}= (1-gX)A\langle X\rangle,\\
        J &\defeq\overline{(g-Y)A\langle Y\rangle} = (g-Y)A\langle Y\rangle,\\
        K &\defeq \overline{(1-gT)A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle}=(1-gT)A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle.
    \end{align*}
    These equalities hold as principal ideals are already closed by the assumption.
    For 
    \begin{align*}
        a & \defeq \sum_{k=0}^\infty a_kX^k\in A\langle X\rangle,\\
        b & \defeq \sum_{k=0}^\infty  b_k Y^k\in A\langle Y\rangle,\\
        f & \defeq \sum_{k=-\infty}^\infty f_kT^k\in A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle,
    \end{align*}
    we define 
    \begin{align*}
        p(a,b)&\defeq a(T)-b(T^{-1})=\sum_{k=0}^\infty \left(a_kT^{k}-b_kT^{-k}\right),\\
        q(a,b)&\defeq (a(g^{-1}),b(g)) = \left(\sum_{k=0}^\infty a_kg^{-k},\sum_{k=0}^\infty b_kg^{k}\right),\\
        r(f)&\defeq f(g^{-1}) = \sum_{k=-\infty}^\infty f_kg^{-k}.
    \end{align*}
    Then we obtain the following commutative diagram,
    \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
            &                                            & 0 \arrow[d]                                                        & 0 \arrow[d]                                    &   \\
            & 0 \arrow[d] \arrow[r]                      & I\oplus J \arrow[d] \arrow[r, "p"]                                 & K \arrow[d] \arrow[r]                          & 0 \\
0 \arrow[r] & A \arrow[r] \arrow[d, no head, Rightarrow] & A\langle X\rangle\oplus A\langle Y\rangle \arrow[d] \arrow[r, "p"] & {A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle} \arrow[d] \arrow[r] & 0 \\
0 \arrow[r] & A \arrow[r]                                & A\langle g^{-1}\rangle\oplus A\langle g\rangle \arrow[d] \arrow[r] & {A\langle g,g^{-1}\rangle} \arrow[d] \arrow[r] & 0 \\
            &                                            & 0                                                                  & 0                                              &  
\end{tikzcd}
    \end{center}
    The second row is clearly exact. Moreover, the first row $p$ maps $I$ onto $(1-gT)A\langle T\rangle$ and and $J$ onto $(g-T^{-1})A\langle T^{-1}\rangle = T^{-1}(1-gT)A\langle T^{-1}\rangle$. 
    Using Laurent expansion, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall f\in A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle, f= f_+(T)+T^{-1}f_-(T^{-1})
    \end{equation*}
    where $f_+,f_-\in A\langle T\rangle$, thus $A\langle T,T^{-1}\rangle = A\langle T\rangle\oplus T^{-1}A\langle T^{-1}\rangle$. Hence showing the injectivity and the first row is exact. Also the colums are exact by constructions. Hence by Snake lemma, the third row is also exact.
\end{proof}

% 10/19

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_26_2}
    Under the assumption of Proposition \ref{proposition_25_1} of $A$ and $g$, pick $a\in A$.
    \begin{enumerate}[1. ]
        \item The image of $a$ in $A\langle g\rangle$ is $0$ if and only if there is a sequence $(b_i)_{i=0,\cdots}$ of elements of $A$ such that $a = gb_0$ and $b_i = gb_{i-1}$ for all $i\in \N$ such that $\lim_{i\to\infty}b_i = 0$ in $A$.
        \item The image of $a$ in $A\langle g^{-1}\rangle$ is $0$ if and only if $\lim_{i\to\infty}g^{i}a=0$ in $A$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.} If $a = (g-T)b$ with $b = \sum_{i=0}^\infty b_iT^i$ then $a = gb_0$, $b_0 = gb_1$ vice versa.
\par\textbf{2.} If $a = (1-gT)b$, then $a=b_0,b_1 = gb_0,\cdots,$ hence $b_i = g^ia$ and some $b\in A\langle T\rangle$, it follows that $\lim_{i\to\infty}g^ia=0$ and vice versa.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_26_2}
    Let $A$ be as in Proposition \ref{proposition_25_1}. Assume also that every ideal $I\subseteq A$ there are finitely many generators $(f_1,\cdots,f_N)$ of it such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        A^N\ni(a_1,\cdots,a_N)\stackrel{\vec{f}}{\mapsto}\sum_{i=1}^N a_if_I\in I,
    \end{equation*}
    is open when $I$ is equipped with the topology induced from $A$, then Equation \eqref{corollary_25_1_eq_1} is exact for every $g\in A$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Due to Proposition \ref{proposition_25_1}, the injectivity of $A\to A\langle g\rangle\oplus A\langle g^{-1}\rangle$ remains to be shown. Let $a\in A$ be in the kernel of this morphism. Then $\lim_{i\to \infty}g^ia=0$ and there are $(b_i)_{i\in\Z_{\geq0}}$ as in Proposition \ref{fact_26_2}. Let $U\subseteq A$ be any open sub group, we will show $a\in U$. 
This implies $a=0$ as $A$ is complete, hence Hausdorf. As $A$ is a nat-ring, there is an open subgroup $V$ such that $VV\subseteq U$. 
Consider 
\begin{equation*}
    I = \{\alpha\in A\sep \lim_{i\to\infty}g^i\alpha = 0\}.
\end{equation*}
Since $g^ib_j = g^{i-j-1}a$ when $i>j$, all $b_i\in I$. Let $(f_i)_{i=1,\cdots,N}$ be generators of $I$ such that $\vec{f}$ is an open map. Then 
\begin{equation*}
    W = \left\{\sum_{i=1}^Nv_if_i\bigg{\vert}v_i\in V\right\}
\end{equation*}
is an open subgroup of $I$. Since $\lim_{i\to\infty}b_i=0$ in $A$ and $I$ has the topology induced from $A$, there is a natural number $j_0$ such that $j\geq j_0$ then $b_j\in W$. Also as $f_i\in I$, there is $k_0\in\N$ with $k\geq k_0\Rightarrow g^kf_i\in V$. 
When $j\geq\max\{j_0,k_0\}$, we have 
\begin{equation*}
    a = g^{1}b_ja \in \sum_{i=1}^N g^{j+1}f_i\cdot V\subseteq U
\end{equation*}
as $g^{j+1}f_i\cdot V\subseteq VV\subseteq U$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{remark_26_1}
    \label{proposition_remark_26_1}
    For a complete nat ring with a countable neighborhood basis of $0$ and a topologicaly nilpotent unit (e.g., any complete Tate ring), the following conditions are equivalent.
    \begin{enumerate}[i. ]
    \item $A$ is Noetherian.
    \item Every ideal of $A$ is closed.
    \item Every ideal $I\subseteq A$ has generator $(f_1,\cdots,f_N)$ such that $\vec{f}$ is open.
    \end{enumerate}
    In this case, if $A^\triangleleft$ is a complete Tate ring and all rings of the form $A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle$ for every $m\in\N$ are Noetherian, then all Huber pairs $B$ fo which $B^\triangleleft$ is the quotient of some $A^\triangleleft\langle X_1,\cdots,X_m\rangle$ by an ideal $I$ are sheafy.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Follows from Proposition \ref{proposition_26_2} and Corollary \ref{corollary_25_1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    Note that \textbf{iii}$\Rightarrow$\textbf{ii} for trivial reasons. Also in the situation of \textbf{iii}, if $(r_j)_{j\in\Z_{\geq0}}$ is a Cauchy sequence in $I$ there are Cauchy sequences $(a_{j,k})_{j\in \N}$ in $A$ for $1\leq k\leq N$ such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        i_j = \sum_{k=1}^N a_{j,k}f_k,
    \end{equation*}
    then $\lim_{j\to\infty}i_j = \sum_{k=1}^N\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}a_{j,k}\right)f_k\in I$. Showing that $I$ is closed hence \textbf{iii}$\Rightarrow$\textbf{i}. 
    It will then be sufficient to verify \textbf{iii}.
\end{remark}

\section{Ultrametric Fields and Ultrametric Functional Analysis}

\subsection{Ultrametric Fields and Their Algebraic Extensions}

Throuout the subsection, $K$ will be a field equipped with some ultrametric absolute value $\ab{\cdot}_K$.

\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_28_1}
    A $K$-norm on a $K$-vectorspace $V$ is a map satisfying the following conditions.
    \begin{enumerate}[i.]
        \item $\forall x,y\in V\norm{x+y}\leq \max\{\norm{x},\norm{y}\}$.
        \item $\forall \lambda\in K,x\in V,\norm{\lambda x} = \ab{\lambda}_K\norm{x}$. 
        \item $\forall x\in V,\norm{x}=0\iff x=0$. 
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
    If the last conditions fails whiel others hold in Definition \ref{definition_28_1}, we say $\norm{\cdot}$ is a seminorm.
\end{remark}

We will always assume that $K$ is non-discrete.

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_28_1}
    \begin{align*}
        K^\circ = \{x\in K\sep \ab{x}_K\leq 1\},\\
        K^\dcirc = \{x\in K\sep \ab{x}_K<1\}.
    \end{align*}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_28_1}
    If $K$ is complete then two norm $\norm{\cdot}$ and $N$ on a finite dimensional $K$vector space are equivalent.
    \par Moreover for these norms, we have the following statements.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Every linear map between finite dimensional $K$-vectorspace is continuous is open onto its image.
        \item Every subspace of $V$ is closed.
        \item Every dense subspace o $V$ is equal to $V$.
        \item $V$ is complete (as $V\cong K^n$ with maximum norm).
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{lemma}
    If $K$ is any field equipped with any multiplicative absolute value, for a natural number $d$, $(\alpha_i)_{i=1,\cdots,d}\in K^d$ and 
    \begin{equation*}
        \prod_{i=1}^d(T-\alpha_i) = \sum_{i=0}^d p_iT^i\in K[T]
    \end{equation*}
    where $p_d=1$, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \max\{\ab{\alpha_1},\cdots,\ab{\alpha_d}\}=\max\{\sqrt[d-i]{\ab{p_i}}\sep 0\leq i<d\}.
    \end{equation*}
    \label{lemma_28_1}
\end{lemma}
\begin{remark}
    Lemma \ref{lemma_28_1} is a special case of the Newton polygon.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_28_2_1}
    Assume that $K$ is complete with respect to $\ab{\cdot}_K$. If $L$ is an algebraic extension of $K$, then there is a unique extension of $\ab{\cdot}_K$. to an absolute value $\ab{\cdot}_R$ on $L$. If $\norm{\cdot}_L$ is any $K$-norm on $L$, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \ab{l}_L=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sqrt[n]{\norm{l^n}_L}.
    \end{equation*}
    If $L/K$ is finite this is complete.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
    The right hand side of the equality in Proposition \ref{proposition_28_2_1} is called a spectral norm.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}
    In the situation of Proposition \ref{proposition_28_2_1}, suppose $L/K$ is Galois and $\alpha,\beta\in L$ be such that $\alpha\not=\beta$ and 
    \begin{equation*}
        \forall \sigma\in\mathrm{Gal}(L/K)\backslash\{1\}, \ab{\alpha-\beta}<\ab{\sigma(\alpha)-\beta}_L
    \end{equation*}
    where $\tilde{\alpha}$ is a conjugate of $\alpha$. Then $\alpha\in K(\beta)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}
    If $L/\mathbb{K}$ is an algeraic extension of $\mathbb{K}$ which is algebraically closed (separably closed, perfect), then the completion $\mathbb{L}$ of $L$ with respect to $\ab{\cdot}_L$ has these properties.
\end{corollary}

\begin{example}
    $\mathbb{C}_p$, the completion of the algebraic closure of $\mathbb{Q}_p$.
\end{example}

We will assume that $\mathbb{K}$ is complete from now on.

\begin{definition}
    A normed $\mathbb{K}$-vectorspace is a $\mathbb{K}$-vectorspace equipped with a $\mathbb{K}$-norm.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    For normed $\mathbb{K}$-vectorspaces $V,W$, we will write 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{L}(V,W)\defeq\{f:V\to W\sep \text{$f$ is continuous and $\mathbb{K}$-linear}\}.
    \end{equation*}
    For $f:V\to W$, $\mathbb{K}$-linear map, we define,
    \begin{equation*}
        \norm{f\vert \mathcal{L}(V,W)} \defeq \inf\{C\in[0,a)_\R\sep\forall v\in V \norm{f(v)\vert W}\leq C\norm{x\vert V}\}.
    \end{equation*}
    With this notion, we have,
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{L}(V,W)\defeq \{f:V\to W\sep \text{$f$ is $\mathbb{K}$-linear and $\norm{f\vert\mathcal{L}(V,W)}$ is finite}\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
    The open maping theorem holds for $\mathbb{K}$-Banach spaces (i.e., a complete $\mathbb{K}$-normed spaces). With a bit more effort, this can also be seen for closed graph theorem. It also holds for Banach-Steinhaus by Baire's principle.
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}
    If $\ab{\mathbb{K}^\times}$ is not discrete in $(0,\infty)_\R$ and $\mathbb{K}$ has a dense countable subset then $\mathbb{K}$ is not spherically complete hence Hahn-Banach fials for normed $\mathbb{K}$-vectorspaces.
\end{remark}

\section{Affnoid $\mathbb{K}$-Algebra and Their Tate Spectrum}
\subsection{Basic Properties of Affnoid $\mathbb{K}$-Algebras}
Let $\mathbb{K}$ be a complete field with respect to a non-discrete ultrametric absolute value $\ab{\cdot}$. The extension o $\ab{\cdot}$ to any algebraic field extensio nof $K$ will also be denoted by $\ab{\cdot}$.
\begin{definition}
    \label{definition_29_1}
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{T}_n\defeq\left\{\sum_{\alpha\in\N^n}f_\alpha X^\alpha\:\bigg{\vert}\:\forall \varepsilon\in (0,\infty)_{\R}, \exists N\in\N \text{ such that }\ab{\alpha}\geq N\Rightarrow \ab{f_\alpha}<\varepsilon\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    We define a norm on this space by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \norm{f|\mathbb{T}_n}= \max\{\ab{f_\alpha}\sep \alpha\in\N^n\}.
    \end{equation*}
    By the condition on formal sum, this is finite and is a max? ? ? ?.
\end{definition}
Obviously this is $\mathbb{K}\langle X_1,\cdots,X_n\rangle$ as a nat ring.

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_29_1}
    With $\norm{\cdot|\mathbb{T}_n}$, $\mathbb{T}_n$ becomes a $\mathbb{K}$-Banach algebra and $\norm{\cdot|\mathbb{T}_n}$ is multiplicative. 
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Exercise sheet 1 from previous term.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_29_2}
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{T}_n^\circ = \{f\in\mathbb{T}_n\sep \norm{f\vert\mathbb{T}_n}\leq 1\},\mathbb{T}_n^\dcirc = \{f\in\mathbb{T}_n\sep \norm{f\vert\mathbb{T}_n}< 1\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore any subset $B\subseteq\mathbb{T}_n$ is bounded if and only if $\{\norm{f|\mathbb{T}_n}\sep f\in B\}$ is bounded in $\R$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Follows from the multiplicativity of the norm.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_29_3}
    Let $k=K^\circ/K^\dcirc$ be the residue field of $K^\circ$. Then we have 
    \begin{equation}
        \label{fact_29_3_equation_1}
        \mathbb{T}_n\circ/\mathbb{T}_n^\dcirc \ni f\stackrel{\cong}{\mapsto}\sum_{\alpha\in\N^n}(f_\alpha\tmod\: K^\dcirc)X^\alpha\in k[X_1,\cdots,X_n].
    \end{equation}
    We then obtain a decomposition of $\mathbb{T}_n^\times$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_29_4_1}
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{T}_n^\times = \mathbb{K}^\times (1+\mathbb{T}_n^\dcirc)=\{f\in\mathbb{T}_n^\times\sep\ab{f_\alpha}<\ab{f_0},\alpha\not=0\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The inclusion $\supseteq$ follows from $1+\mathbb{T}_n^\dcirc\subseteq\mathbb{T}_n^\times$ (geometric series). Let $f\in\mathbb{T}^\times_n$, without loss of generality we assume $\norm{f|\mathbb{T}_n}=1$. This can be achieved by inversion by any $x\in K^\times$ such that $\ab{x} = \norm{f|\mathbb{T}_n}$. 
    Then $\norm{f^{-1}|\mathbb{T}_n}=1$ by Fact 1. Then $f^{-1}\in \mathbb{T}_n^\circ$, and $f\in\mathbb{T}_n^{\circ\times}$. Then the image of $f$ under Equation \eqref{fact_29_3_equation_1} is a unit in $k[X_1,\cdots,X_n]$ hence constant and $\ab{f_\alpha}<1$ when $\alpha\not=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
    For any (discrete) ring $R$, 
    \begin{align*}
        \mathfrak{N}(R) &= \{r\in R\sep \exists n\in\N, r^n = 0\}=\bigcap_{\p\in\Spec R}\p,\\
        \mathfrak{J}(R) &= \{r\in R\sep 1+rx\in R^\times\forall x\in R\} = \bigcap_{\m\in\MaxSpec R}\m.
    \end{align*}
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{fact_29_5}
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathfrak{J}(\mathbb{T}_n) = \{0\}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Let $f\in\mathbb{T}_n\backslash\{0\}$. If $f_0$ and $x\in K^\times$ is such that $\norm{f|\mathbb{T}_n}=\ab{x}$. Then $1+{\frac f x}\not\in\mathbb{T}_n^\times$ by Proposition \ref{fact_29_4_1}. 
    if $f_0\not=0$, then the same holds for $1-{\frac f {f_0}}$ which has $0$-th coefficient equal to $0$ by Proposition \ref{fact_29_4_1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
    \label{proposition_29_1}
    $\:$
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Every $f\in\mathbb{T}_1\backslash\{0\}$ is $(K,T)$-distinguished of degree $d$, for a unique $d = \underline{d}(f)\in\N$ is the same as in Defintion 3.5.1. By $\mathbb{T}_1\backslash\{0\}\stackrel{\underline{d}}{\to}\N$, $\mathbb{T}_1$ becomes a Euclidean ring.
        \item We have $\mathbb{T}_1^\times = K^\times(1\oplus T\mathbb{T}^\dcirc_1) = \{f\in\mathbb{T}_1\backslash\{0\}\sep \underline{d}(f)=0\}$.
        \item A set of representatives for the multiplicative equivalence classes of norm zero elements of $\mathbb{T}_1$ is given by the Weierstrass polynomials ($P\in\mathbb{K}^\circ[T]$ with leading term $1$) or, equivalently $\{f\in K[T]\sep \text{$f$ is monic and all roots $z$ of $f$, $\ab{z}\leq 1$}\}$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Set 
    \begin{equation*}
        \underline{d}(f) = \max\{k\in\N\sep \ab{f_k}=\norm{f|\mathbb{T}_1}\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Then \textbf{1} follows from Definition in 3.5.
    \par\textbf{2}. The equality with the set of Weierstrass polynomials is given in Corollary 3.5.1.
    \begin{equation*}
        \{f\in K[T]\sep \text{$f$ is monic and all roots $z$ of $f$, $\ab{z}\leq 1$}\}.
    \end{equation*}
    by Lemma 5.1.1.
    \par\textbf{Addition to 1}. That we get an Euclidean ring follows from Proposition 3.5.1.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_29_1_1}
    Let $F\subseteq\N^n$ be a finite subset. For $\alpha\in\N^n$ and $\vec{k} =(k_i)_{i=1}^{n-1}$ as in the Lemma, let 
    \begin{equation*}
        w(\vec{k},\alpha) = \sum_{i=1}^n k_1\alpha_i,
    \end{equation*}
    with $k_n=1$. Then it is possible to choose $\vec{k}$ in such a way that $w(\vec{k},\alpha)\not=w(\vec{k},\beta)$ when $\alpha,\beta\in F$ and $\alpha\not=\beta$. 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
    CHoose $k_i$ by downward induction on $i$ such that $k_n=1$ and 
    \begin{equation*}
    k_{i,n}>\max\left\{\sum_{j=1}^n k_j\alpha_j\:\bigg{\vert}\: \alpha\in F\right\}.
    \end{equation*}
    Then if $\alpha\not=\beta$ are elements of $F$ such that $\alpha_l=\alpha_l$ for $1\leq l<j$ and $\alpha_j\not=\beta_j$, the large difference difference,
    \begin{equation*}
        k_j\ab{\alpha_j-\beta_j}\geq k_j,
    \end{equation*}
    can not be compensated by 
    \begin{equation*}
        \left\vert\sum_{l=j+1}^n k_l(\alpha_l-\beta_l)\right\vert = \left\vert\left(\sum_{l=j+1}^n k_l\alpha_l\right)-\left(\sum_{l=j+1}^nk_k\beta_l\right)\right\vert.
    \end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
    \label{lemma_29_1}
    Let $(f_i)_{i=1}^m\in(\mathbb{T}_n\backslash\{0\})^m$ where $m,n\in\N$. Then there are positive integers $(k_i)_{i=1}^{n-1}$ such that the continuous ring homomorphism $\varphi:\mathbb{T}_n\to\mathbb{T}_n$ is given by
    \begin{equation*}
        \varphi|_\mathbb{K} = \id_\mathbb{K},\varphi(X_i)=\begin{cases}
            X_i,\: i=n,\\
            X_i+X_n^{k_i},\:1\leq i<n.
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation*}
    is an isometric $\mathbb{K}$-isomorphism of $\mathbb{K}$-algebraof $\mathbb{T}_n$ onto itself, and such that all $\varphi(f_i)$ are $(\mathbb{T}_n,X_n)$-distinguished in $\mathbb{T}_n\cong \mathbb{T}_{n-1}\langle X_n\rangle$.
\end{lemma}
